# Bug hunt — 2026-08-01

A full sweep of the repository. The tree was split into thirteen areas, one reviewer per
area, each reading every file it owned end to end and fixing what it found. Cross-area
findings were routed back through the coordinator to the area that owns the code.

Every label in this document (`MP3`, `SSH7`, …) is unique **within this round**. Labels do
not continue, and are not comparable with, the numbering of `docs/bug-hunt-2026-07-29.md`
or `docs/bug-hunt-2026-07-31.md`. Those two were treated as historical background only and
none of their claims were reused without re-verifying against the current code.

## Areas and label prefixes

| Prefix | Area |
|---|---|
| `MP` | `src/models`, `src/persistence` |
| `AC` | `src/app` core: `AppController`, `main.cpp`, `Notifier` |
| `AS` | `src/app` state: `SessionBootstrap`, `SessionLayouts`, `UiStateStore`, `ServerProfiles` |
| `SSH` | `src/ssh` |
| `RPC` | `src/remote` — the C++ side of the JSON-RPC connection |
| `TM` | `src/terminal` and `src/web/terminal` |
| `ED` | `src/editor` and `src/web/editor` |
| `VW` | `src/viewers` |
| `QM` | `src/qml` |
| `AG` | `src/agent`, plus the remote agent hook and harness adapters |
| `WS` | `remote/src/workspace.ts` and its validation and database schema |
| `DF` | `remote/src` file serving, the daemon process, the bridge, tmux, events |
| `BD` | build system, CI workflows, packaging, release tooling, documentation |
| `ORCH` | found by the coordinator during final integration |

## Vocabulary, defined once

The codebase uses some words in a specific way. They are spelled out here so the rest of
this document can be read without opening the source.

* **Dev Session** — one unit of work: a checkout on a remote server, plus the panes and
  shells opened against it.
* **Pane** — one rectangular sub-view. The window has a fixed three-part layout: a
  sessions sidebar down one side, a viewer region, and a terminal region.
* **`codeharbord`** — a small Node service that CodeHarbor runs on the remote SSH server.
  All workspace data lives there. The desktop client talks to it in JSON-RPC: one JSON
  object per line, over an SSH channel.
* **`codeharbor-bridge`** — a second small Node process on the server. It listens on a
  Unix socket for status messages posted by coding-assistant programs and relays them to
  the client.
* **tmux** — a program on the remote server that keeps a shell alive after the client
  disconnects. Every terminal pane is attached to one tmux session; that is what makes
  terminals survive a dropped connection.
* **Harness** — a coding-assistant program running in a terminal (Oh My Pi, Pi, Claude
  Code). An **adapter** translates one harness's own event vocabulary into CodeHarbor's.
* **Qt signal** — a notification one C++ object sends to others. Qt delivers signals
  *synchronously*: emitting one runs every connected handler to completion before the next
  line of the emitting function executes. A handler is allowed to do anything, including
  destroy the object that sent the signal.
* **`QPointer`** — a Qt smart pointer that automatically becomes null when the object it
  points at is destroyed, instead of dangling.
* **QWebChannel** — Qt's bridge that lets JavaScript inside an embedded browser view call
  C++ methods and receive C++ signals.
* **Revision token** — an opaque string identifying the exact contents of a remote file at
  the moment it was read. A save quotes it, and the server refuses the write if the file
  has moved on, which is how two editors are stopped from silently overwriting each other.
* **Write-ahead log** — a SQLite mode where changes go to a side file first, so readers are
  not blocked by a writer.

had finished. Results from commands actually run in this final pass are separated from unavailable live-gate checks.

| `ctest --preset dev -L live` against a real `sshd`, `tmux` and `codeharbord` | **Not run in this final pass.** The required `CH_LIVE_*` environment variables were unavailable, so no live-gate result is claimed here. |

The live gate was not available for this final pass: the required `CH_LIVE_*` connection,
remote-node, repository and known-hosts settings were absent. This report therefore does not
claim a new live-gate result. Several individual findings retain earlier live-gate evidence in
their own sections; those are historical evidence from the round, not a result of this final

Test totals over this round: the portable C++ suite has 39 registered targets, with 38
passing and one intentional desktop-notification skip. The Node suites pass 263 cases —
`remote` 218, `web-terminal` 14 and `web-editor` 31. The workspace test command was run
locally after the integrated build; its output also recorded only expected fixture warnings.

# MP — models and persistence

## MP1 — Saving a window layout reported success when the server echoed back something unusable

**Files:** `src/persistence/WorkspaceDb.cpp` lines 641–678; `src/persistence/WorkspaceDb.h`
lines 149–161 and 250–254.

**What the code did before.** `WorkspaceDb` is the client's gateway to the workspace
database that lives on the server. Every method is a JSON-RPC request; the reply is decoded
into a C++ structure and handed to a callback. That callback always receives a pair: an
optional result, and an optional error.

`setLayout` saves the split layout of one region of a Dev Session — how the panes are
divided up. The server answers by echoing back the row it stored, with the layout tree under
a key called `tree`. The old code ran that reply through a generic handler that only checked
"is this reply a JSON object at all". It then called a parsing helper which returns "no tree"
in two different situations: when the `tree` key is missing, and when it is present but
structurally invalid. That "no tree" answer was passed to the caller **with no error
attached**.

**Why that is wrong.** For the sibling method `getLayout`, an empty answer is meaningful — it
is how the server says "this region has no saved layout yet". `setLayout` has no such answer:
it has just written a layout, so the row it echoes back must contain one. A reply like
`{"id":"L1","devSessionId":"s1"}` with no `tree`, or one whose tree is invalid (for example a
split with two child panes but only one size ratio), therefore reached the caller as
"success, no error, no tree" — indistinguishable from a clean save. The only caller,
`SessionLayouts`, looks solely at the error slot. So a server or an intermediary returning a
mangled row would leave the user believing their layout was saved when nothing usable had
been stored. It also contradicted this class's own stated policy, which is that every
wrong-shaped reply is turned into a synthesized error with the JSON-RPC code `-32603`
("internal error"), and contradicted the header's promise that "setLayout delivers the stored
tree on success".

**What changed.** `setLayout` now has its own explicit reply handler: a server-reported error
is passed through unchanged; a result that is not an object fails with `-32603` and the
message "expected a SessionLayout object"; a row whose tree is missing or invalid fails with
`-32603` and "expected a SessionLayout object with a valid tree". A valid tree is delivered
exactly as before. The class documentation and the declaration comment were rewritten to
match, including an explicit note that `getLayout` deliberately keeps the opposite verdict —
there, an unreadable stored tree means "no layout", so a region whose layout cannot be loaded
comes up empty rather than inviting the user to edit a fabricated one.

**Test.** New: `TstWorkspaceDb::setLayoutMalformedTreeDeliversRpcError`. A row with no tree
and a row with an invalid tree must each deliver no layout plus an error with code `-32603`
naming `workspace.setLayout`. To prove the check is selective rather than a blanket
rejection, it also asserts that a row echoing a valid tree still succeeds — including the
empty single-pane tree that legitimately gets stored when the last pane in a region is closed.

## MP2 — The sidebar model announced row changes while it was still halfway through updating itself

**Files:** `src/models/SessionsModel.cpp` lines 53–76; `src/models/SessionsModel.h` lines
63–64.

**What the code did before.** `SessionsModel` is the data model behind the sessions sidebar.
Top-level rows are groups; child rows are Dev Sessions. Each Dev Session row shows a status
badge summarising the state of that session's terminals.

`updateTerminalStates` is the cheap refresh path. Rather than resetting the whole model —
which would destroy and rebuild every on-screen row and lose the user's selection — it copies
the new terminal state into each session in place, and emits Qt's `dataChanged` signal only
for the rows whose badge actually moved. The old code did both jobs in a single loop: for
each session it computed the old badge, moved in the new terminal state, computed the new
badge, and if they differed emitted `dataChanged` **right there** before moving on to the
next session.

**Why that is wrong.** Two problems, both caused by sending a notification in the middle of a
mutation.

The first is that receivers see a half-updated model. `dataChanged` is delivered synchronously
to every connected receiver. A receiver that looks at any row other than the one named in the
signal — a sorting or filtering layer recomputing its mapping, or an on-screen row whose
appearance depends on its siblings — sees rows further down the list still reporting their
**previous** terminal state, because the loop has not reached them yet. It then caches or
draws a state that was never actually true.

The second is worse: the loop holds C++ references into two vectors, one of them the model's
own storage. Any receiver that reacts by calling `setGroups()` re-entrantly replaces that
storage wholesale, freeing the memory the loop is still walking. That is a use-after-free for
the remainder of the iteration.

**What changed.** The single loop was split into two passes. The first adopts every session's
new terminal state and records which rows changed; the second emits `dataChanged` for the
recorded rows. No container reference is alive while a signal is in flight, and the model is
fully in its new state before the first notification goes out. The signals emitted, their
order, and their contents are unchanged. The ordering guarantee is now documented on the
declaration.

**Test.** New: `TstModels::updateTerminalStatesFinishesEveryRowBeforeSignalling`. It builds one
group with two Dev Session rows, moves both from idle to error in a single refresh, and from
inside a `dataChanged` handler reads the badge of **both** rows every time a signal arrives,
asserting both already report error at the very first signal. Confirmed to fail against the
pre-fix code, where the second row still reported idle.

## MP3 — A parsing helper quietly turned a wrong-shaped reply into "the user has no groups"

**File:** `src/persistence/WorkspaceDb.cpp` lines 163–176, call site at line 422.

**What the code did before.** `parseGroupList` decodes the reply to `workspace.list`, which is
the entire sidebar: groups, their Dev Sessions, and each session's panes and layouts. It took
a `QJsonValue` — a value of *any* JSON kind — and called `.toArray()` on it. Qt's `toArray()`
does not fail on a value of the wrong kind; it returns an **empty array**. So handing this
function a number, a string, an object or JSON null returned an empty group list,
indistinguishable from a server that genuinely has no groups.

**Why that is wrong.** This was not reachable today — the single caller does check that the
value is an array first. It is a trap for the next person: nothing in the function's signature
said that check was mandatory, and the very same file's comments identify this exact
`toArray()` behaviour as the reason wrong-shaped replies must be rejected rather than decoded.
A second call site added later (a cached list, a batched reply, a new nested field) would
compile fine, skip the check, and silently report an empty sidebar for a malformed reply. It
also did the conversion twice.

**What changed.** The parameter type is now `const QJsonArray&`, so a caller physically cannot
pass anything else, and the single conversion happens at the call site that has already
established the value is an array. A comment records why the signature is deliberately narrow.

**Test.** None added — the observable behaviour is unchanged and already covered by
`malformedResultsDeliverRpcErrorInsteadOfBlankRecords` and `listSkipsNonObjectEntries`.

## MP4 — A comment claimed every identifier is a server-generated UUID; one of the five is not

**File:** `src/models/Ids.h` lines 5–10.

**What the code did before.** The comment above the five strongly-typed identifier wrappers
read: "Values are opaque UUID strings generated server-side."

**Why that is wrong.** It is true of four of them, which the daemon mints with a random-UUID
call. It is not true of `ServerId`, which names *which* server a row belongs to and is chosen
entirely on the client — real values in live code and tests look like
`live-shell-<pid>-<timestamp>` and `srv-live`, none of them UUIDs and none of them
server-generated. Someone trusting the comment could reasonably add UUID-format validation, or
assume the server guarantees these are unique. Neither holds.

**What changed.** The comment now names the four server-minted UUID types explicitly and calls
out `ServerId` as the client-chosen exception, describing it as an arbitrary opaque string.

## MP5 — The client cannot express "clear this field", and nothing said so

**Files:** `src/persistence/WorkspaceDb.h` lines 41–56 (new comment block);
`src/persistence/tests/tst_workspacedb.cpp` lines 1284–1341 (new test).

**What the code did before.** The server treats several update fields as three-valued. In
`remote/src/workspace.ts` (for example lines 1538–1548), a field that is *absent* means "leave
the stored value alone", a field that is JSON `null` means "clear the stored value", and a
string means "store that string". The database columns really are nullable. This applies to a
terminal pane's working directory, tmux target, startup command and harness, and to a Dev
Session's default working directory and task description, among others.

The client side represents those fields as `std::optional<QString>`, and every serializer
writes the key only if the optional holds a value. So a disengaged optional omits the key
("leave alone") and an engaged optional always writes a JSON string. There is no value of
`std::optional<QString>` that produces JSON `null`. The server's clear branch is therefore
unreachable from this client, and nothing anywhere stated that.

**Why that matters.** It is a contract mismatch across two languages with no marker on either
side, so the next person to need "clear this field" will reach for an engaged optional holding
an empty string and get the wrong result. On one field the wrong result is loud rather than
quiet: an empty tmux target is not read as a clear — the server's validator requires at least
one character, so the whole request fails with an "invalid params" error.

**What changed, and what deliberately did not.** No structure was widened and no sentinel value
was invented; no caller needs to clear a field today, and adding a second way to spell "absent"
would cost more than the gap. Instead the contract is now written down once, above the
parameter structures: the server's three-part rule, the two-thirds of it this class can
express, the fact that the clear path is not reachable from here, and the empty-tmux-target
case named explicitly.

**Test.** New: `TstWorkspaceDb::updateOptionalsOmitUnsetAndSendEmptyStringsVerbatim`. It issues
an update with some fields engaged-but-empty and others unset, then inspects the outgoing JSON
request: each engaged-empty field must be present as an empty JSON string — not null, not
absent — and each unset field must be absent entirely. It would fail if the serializer ever
started skipping empty strings or emitting null for them.

## MP6 — The layout writer could produce a tree the layout reader refuses, and the layout would be lost on the next launch

**Files:** `src/models/SplitTree.h` lines 30–33 and 74–85; `src/models/SplitTree.cpp` lines
13–68 and 176–183; call sites in `src/persistence/WorkspaceDb.cpp` (`setLayout`),
`src/app/SessionLayouts.cpp` lines 345–367 (`publishTreeQuietly`), and eighteen places in
`src/models/tests/tst_models.cpp`.

**What the code did before.** A split tree describes how one region of the window is divided
into panes. `SplitNode::fromJson` — the reader — enforces rules on a split node: there must be
exactly one size ratio per child, and every ratio must be a finite number greater than zero.
`SplitNode::toJson` — the writer — enforced nothing. It always returned a `QJsonObject`, so it
had no way to report that it could not represent the tree it was given.

**Why that is wrong.** The previous round left this alone and gave two reasons. Both were
examined again and neither holds up as a reason not to fix it. The first reason was that the
bad state is unreachable because the only producer of ratios, `SessionLayouts::setRatios`,
already applies the reader's rules — but that is an argument about one caller today, not a
property of the type, and it says nothing about a tree assembled anywhere else. The second
reason was that the asymmetry is "deliberately pinned" by an existing test; in fact that test
pins the *reader's* rejection of a two-children, one-ratio node, which is behaviour worth
keeping and is untouched by fixing the writer.

The harm was never that the emitted JSON looks odd. It was that `setLayout` would hand such a
tree to the server, the server would store it, the call would report success — and on the next
launch `getLayout` maps an unreadable stored tree to "this region has no layout" (see MP1), so
the user finds the region empty and nothing ever reported a failure.

**What changed.** The invariant was closed on the **writer**, not the reader. Relaxing the
reader would mean inventing size ratios the user never chose and deleting the rejection path
that guards against corrupt stored or remote data, which is the client's only gate on data it
did not produce itself. So the writer was held to the reader's own rules instead.

`QJsonObject toJson() const` is replaced by `std::optional<QJsonObject> tryToJson() const`,
matching the existing `tryFromJson()`. A new recursive helper, `writeNode`, applies the
parser's checks per node on the way down — ratio count equal to child count, every ratio finite
and greater than zero — so a single bad node anywhere fails the whole serialization rather than
emitting a half-written object. The header records the invariant it now guarantees: for every
tree that does serialize, `fromJson(*tryToJson()) == *this`.

`WorkspaceDb::setLayout` uses the new channel: a tree that `tryToJson()` refuses is never put
on the wire; the call fails locally with `-32602` ("invalid params") instead. That local
failure is delivered asynchronously by the same helper described in MP7, so no caller is
re-entered from inside its own call.

**Test.** `TstModels::splitTreeRejectsRatioMismatch` was rewritten
(`tst_models.cpp` lines 243–284): `tryToJson()` must return no value for one ratio too few, one
too many, a zero ratio, a negative ratio, NaN and positive infinity; a bad node anywhere must
fail the whole tree; and once the ratios are legal the tree must serialize and round-trip equal.
At the persistence seam, `TstWorkspaceDb::setLayoutRefusesATreeItCouldNotReadBack`
(`tst_workspacedb.cpp` lines 1254–1317) checks the refusal and also that the check is
selective — the same call with legal ratios still reaches the wire and its `tree` parameter
parses back equal.

Because the fix renames the method, the new model test cannot be compiled against the old
sources, so the pre-fix behaviour was demonstrated with a small purpose-built program built
against the pre-fix `SplitTree` files taken from git. It showed the old `toJson()` emitting a
split with two children and one ratio, and the reader refusing that same object. The
persistence-seam test *was* run against pre-fix copies of `WorkspaceDb` and `SplitTree` and
failed there: the callback never fired, because pre-fix the request went out on the wire and
the fake server never answered it.

## MP7 — Asking for a terminal pane with no address at all sent an empty label to the server and waited forever

**Files:** `src/persistence/WorkspaceDb.cpp` lines 608–628 (the guard) and 379–413 (new
helpers); `src/persistence/WorkspaceDb.h` lines 128–130, 186–203 and 282–287.

**What the code did before.** `resolveTerminalPane` finds — or creates — the database row for a
terminal pane. The caller addresses the row in one of two ways: by its row id, or by its layout
slot label (`name`). If the caller supplied neither, the old code sent the request anyway with
`name` set to the empty string.

**Why that is wrong.** The previous round left this alone on the grounds that the server
rejects the empty label and the caller therefore gets a proper error, so nothing is silently
wrong, and that a client-side short-circuit would have to invoke the callback before the
function returned — a re-entrancy shape no other method here has and no caller is written for.

The second half of that reasoning was right; the first half was not. In practice the request
was sent and nothing answered it, so the callback never ran at all: the caller was left waiting
and a request id was spent for a request that could never succeed. Sending a request that is
known to be invalid before it leaves the machine is also work the server should not have to do.

**What changed.** `resolveTerminalPane` now refuses locally when neither `id` nor `name` is
set. The callback receives error code `-32602` ("invalid params", the same code the server
itself answers with) and a message naming the method and both accepted addresses. Nothing is
written to the wire and no request id is consumed.

The re-entrancy objection is answered rather than ignored. The failure goes through a new
`failAsync()` helper (`WorkspaceDb.cpp` lines 400–413) that posts the callback to the borrowed
client's event loop with `Qt::QueuedConnection`. The callback therefore runs after the method
has returned, exactly as a real reply would, so no caller is re-entered from inside its own
call. MP6's `setLayout` refusal uses the same helper, so there is one way to report a
locally-detected caller error rather than two.

Behaviour when *both* addresses are set is deliberately unchanged: the row id wins and the
label is not sent. That is an intentional normalisation already pinned by
`resolveTerminalPaneAddressesARowByIdWithoutItsLabel`, and the finding named only the "neither"
case.

**Test.** New: `TstWorkspaceDb::resolveTerminalPaneWithNoAddressFailsWithoutAskingTheServer`
(`tst_workspacedb.cpp` lines 1213–1252). It asserts the callback has **not** fired by the time
the method returns — this is what pins the non-re-entrant delivery — then that it fires from
the event loop with no pane, code `-32602`, the method name in the message, zero bytes readable
on the server socket, and no pending requests left on the client. Built against pre-fix copies
of `WorkspaceDb` and `SplitTree` taken from git, it fails at exactly that first assertion,
because pre-fix the request went out as `name: ""` and nothing ever answered.

## MP8 — One of two identifier fields in a parameter structure was a plain string where its siblings were typed

**Files:** `src/persistence/WorkspaceDb.h` lines 141–145 and 152; `src/persistence/WorkspaceDb.cpp`
lines 615 and 632; `src/terminal/TerminalFactory.cpp` lines 189 and 230–231;
`src/terminal/tests/tst_terminalfactory.cpp` lines 287 and 296;
`src/persistence/tests/tst_workspacedb.cpp` line 1203.

**What the code did before.** `ResolveTerminalPaneParams` has two addressing fields, `id` and
`name`, and both were plain `QString`, while the neighbouring `serverId` and `devSessionId`
fields used the project's strongly-typed identifier wrappers.

**Why that is wrong.** For `id`, the inconsistency is real: it holds a terminal-pane row id, the
sibling structure `UpdateTerminalPaneParams` already declares the same thing as `TerminalId`,
and so does `deleteTerminalPane()`. A plain string there lets any string be passed where a
terminal-pane row id is meant. The previous round left this alone because tightening it requires
editing a call site in another area — a reason about effort, not about correctness.

**What changed.** `id` is now `TerminalId`. Every construction and read site was updated,
including two files outside this area: `src/terminal/TerminalFactory.cpp` and its test. Those
edits were announced to the terminal area's owner before being made, and that owner applied two
further call sites in `TerminalFactory.cpp` that had been missed.

`name` was deliberately **not** typed. There is no existing identifier type for it, and minting
one would state something the system explicitly denies: a slot label is not an identity. The
header says so in two places — schema version 4 dropped the server's
`UNIQUE (dev_session_id, name)` constraint for exactly this reason, and a closed pane keeps its
row and its label while a new pane takes the same label, which is the label recycling that
motivated addressing panes by row id in the first place. The value also comes straight from
`SplitNode::paneId`, which is a plain string at every layer up to and including the QML
boundary; a wrapper type would have to be pushed through the split tree and the QML boundary to
be useful. The reasoning is now written at the field itself
(`WorkspaceDb.h` lines 141–145), so the remaining inconsistency reads as a decision rather than
an oversight.

**Test.** None added, and none possible in the usual sense: this is a type change, so the
compiler is the check. The pre-fix code does not compile against the new field type and the new
code does not compile against the old one — which is why building the pre-fix `tst_workspacedb`
for MP6 and MP7 required reverting this one line. The change is exercised by the existing
`tst_terminalfactory` assertions (now reading `.id.value`) and by
`tst_workspacedb.cpp` lines 1196–1211.

## MP9 — Two recursive walks over a split tree had no depth limit and crashed the process on a deep tree

**Files:** `src/models/SplitTree.h` lines 30–33 and 65–72; `src/models/SplitTree.cpp` lines
19–22, 70–93 and 198–201.

**What the code did before.** Three functions walk a split tree recursively: the parser, the
serializer, and `operator==`. Only the parser enforced a nesting cap, using a 256-level limit
held in a constant private to `SplitTree.cpp`. The other two recursed without any bound.

**Why that is wrong.** The previous round left this alone with the reasoning that in-memory
trees are built one level at a time by user splits and never approach the bound. That reasoning
is wrong about where trees come from: a split tree is persisted and can arrive from the remote
server, which is the normal case in a remote-first client, so "the local user builds it slowly"
does not describe every tree these functions see. Unbounded recursion on a hostile or corrupt
tree exhausts the stack, and stack exhaustion kills the process rather than raising an error
that could be reported.

**What changed.** The limit stays at 256 but is no longer parser-private: it moved out of the
anonymous namespace and became `SplitNode::kMaxDepth` (`SplitTree.h` lines 65–72), so there is
one nesting bound for the type instead of one for the parser alone. Root counts as level 1,
matching the parser. The justification is recorded in the header: real layouts nest a handful of
levels because a user splits one level at a time, so 256 is far beyond any genuine use while
staying well inside the stack a default thread gets.

Both unguarded walks now respect it. The serializer checks depth inside `writeNode`, where
exceeding the bound is the same clean typed failure MP6 introduced — no value returned, not a
crash. `operator==` checks depth inside `equalNode`; equality has no error channel, so the bound
answers **unequal**. That never claims two trees are the same on the strength of a walk that was
cut short, and no tree that deep can be parsed or serialized anyway. The parser keeps its
existing check and now reads the shared constant.

These are the only recursive walks over a `SplitNode` in `src/models` and `src/persistence`.
Six further recursive walks over split trees live in `src/app/SessionLayouts.cpp`, which belongs
to another area; they are now transitively safe because every tree that reaches them has passed
through either `tryFromJson` or `tryToJson`. This was reported rather than changed.

**Test.** New: `TstModels::splitTreeOperationsAreDepthBounded` (`tst_models.cpp` lines 286–322).
A chain of exactly `kMaxDepth` levels must serialize, parse back and compare equal; a chain of
`kMaxDepth + 1` must be refused by the writer, matching the parser, which already refused it;
and a chain of four times `kMaxDepth` must crash neither the writer nor equality.

The pre-fix failure was observed directly. A purpose-built program built against the pre-fix
`SplitTree` sources from git, given a 400,000-level tree, died with a segmentation fault
(exit status 139, stack overflow) in both the serializer and `operator==`. Against the current
sources the same 400,000-level tree returns "no value" from the writer and "unequal" from
equality, both with a normal exit.

## MP10 — Constructing the workspace repository with a null connection was accepted

**Files:** `src/persistence/WorkspaceDb.h` lines 196–203 and 234–235;
`src/persistence/WorkspaceDb.cpp` lines 438–444.

**What the code did before.** `WorkspaceDb` borrows a pointer to the JSON-RPC client it sends
requests through. It stores that pointer without checking it, and every method dereferences it.
A caller passing a null pointer got no complaint at construction; the failure surfaced later,
inside whichever asynchronous method happened to run first.

**Why that is wrong.** The previous round left this alone with a reason that was actually
correct: adding a null check on the *use* path would turn an immediate, obvious crash into a
silent no-op, which is worse. The mistake was treating that as a reason to do nothing, when it
is only a reason not to check at the use sites. The remaining problem is one of diagnosis: the
crash happens far away, in time and in code, from the construction that got it wrong.

**What changed.** No use-path check was added, and no method silently does nothing. Instead the
bad construction itself is rejected, in two ways covering two different mistakes:

* `WorkspaceDb(std::nullptr_t) = delete;` — passing a literal `nullptr` (or `{}`, or `NULL`) no
  longer compiles. This costs nothing at runtime and nothing in the binary.
* `Q_ASSERT(m_client)` in the constructor, for a pointer that turns out to be null only at
  runtime. It fires at the construction site that got it wrong, which is exactly the diagnosis
  problem the previous round's note was concerned about.

The lifetime comment on the class now states that the borrowed client must be non-null and says
which of the two mechanisms catches which mistake. `<cstddef>` was included for
`std::nullptr_t`.

**Test.** New: `TstWorkspaceDb::workspaceDbCannotBeBuiltWithoutAClient`
(`tst_workspacedb.cpp` lines 1319–1323). It asserts that `WorkspaceDb` is not constructible from
`std::nullptr_t` and is constructible from a client pointer. It is written as a runtime check
rather than a compile-time `static_assert` precisely so that it could be run against the pre-fix
source, where it fails: pre-fix, `WorkspaceDb` *is* constructible from `std::nullptr_t`.

### Investigated in this area and deliberately left alone

* **The `name` field of `ResolveTerminalPaneParams` is still a plain string, not an identifier
  type.** This is the second half of MP8 and it is a decision, not an omission: a layout slot
  label is not an identity. Schema version 4 dropped the server's uniqueness constraint on
  `(dev_session_id, name)` for that reason, and a closed pane keeps its label for the next pane
  to reuse. The value is the same plain string as `SplitNode::paneId`, which stays a plain
  string all the way to the QML boundary. The reasoning is now recorded at the field.

# AC — application core

## AC1 — A failure caused by the user's own Disconnect was painted as a red error

**File:** `src/app/AppController.cpp` lines 727–736.

**What the code did before.** Every time a connection comes up — including every automatic
reconnect — the client issues a `server.info` request to ask the server for its version, its
schema level and its identity. The callback for that request started by emitting the
controller's `error` signal on any failure, which the interface renders as a red banner at the
top of the window.

Every *other* asynchronous callback in this class instead goes through a small private helper
whose entire purpose is to swallow errors while the client is deliberately tearing a session
down, i.e. while the user's own Disconnect is in progress. That is because a teardown fails
every request still in flight, by design, with a synthetic "transport closed with request
pending" error. The helper's comment says exactly that: it is "the expected consequence of the
user clicking Disconnect, not a fault to paint red".

**Why that is wrong.** `server.info` is in flight for precisely the window a Disconnect covers.
A connection comes up, `server.info` goes on the wire, and before the answer arrives the user
presses Disconnect. Every other pending request is muted; this one popped a red error at a user
who had just asked for the disconnect. Two code paths that should agree, and did not — the
helper existed and the one caller that most needed it did not use it.

**What changed.** The bare emit was replaced with a call to the helper. Behaviour outside a
teardown is identical: the helper emits the server's message unchanged.

**Test.** New: `TstAppController::serverInfoFailureDuringDisconnectIsNotPaintedAsAFault`. It
drives a real bootstrap object so that a `server.info` really is unanswered on the wire, drops
the transport at the same point in the teardown sequence the real connection would, then calls
`disconnectServer()`. It asserts the error signal never fires and that the controller ends up
reporting "disconnected". Without the fix the test sees one "transport closed with request
pending".

## AC2 — A server that reported no identity left the window looking connected with a permanently empty sidebar

**Files:** `src/app/AppController.cpp` lines 774–786, new helper at lines 806–821;
`src/app/AppController.h` lines 258–263.

**What the code did before.** The `server.info` callback has two "this server is unusable"
branches.

The first is the schema-version floor: if the server speaks an older workspace schema than the
client requires, the code shows a message naming both versions and then genuinely refuses. It
schedules a disconnect one event-loop turn later — deferred because the code is running inside
the connection object's own reply callback and the teardown destroys that object's transport
underneath it — and sets the durable connection state to "failed" with that message attached.

The second is the identity check. If the server answers without an identity string, the code
emitted a transient error banner and returned. No disconnect. No state change.

**Why that is wrong.** The consequence of both branches is identical. Without a server identity
the client cannot key a single workspace row — every group, Dev Session and terminal pane on
the remote side is stored against it — so the client deliberately does not even ask for the
workspace listing. The user was left with a healthy-looking SSH session, a footer saying
**connected**, and a sidebar that stays empty forever, explained only by a banner that fades
after a few seconds. If this happened during an automatic reconnect while the window was not in
focus, they never saw it at all. And it is not ordinary version skew: the specification records
that `server.info` has reported an identity since schema 4, and the client's floor is 6, so any
server the client will talk to at all reports one. An empty value means a broken remote.

**What changed.** The "report the reason, then drop the link and land on failed" tail of the
schema branch was factored into a new private helper, `refuseServer(message)`, and both branches
now call it. The schema branch behaves exactly as before. The empty-identity branch now refuses
identically instead of showing a banner and carrying on. The change removes more lines than it
adds, because the duplicated deferred-disconnect block is gone.

**Test.** New:
`TstAppController::serverThatReportsNoIdentityIsRefusedRatherThanLeftConnected`. It answers
`server.info` with a current schema but no identity and asserts: exactly one error mentioning
"identity"; the stored identity still empty; **no** workspace listing request on the wire (that
request *is* the empty sidebar); and, after the deferred turn, the connection state is "failed"
with the message attached. The last two assertions fail without the fix.

## AC3 — Disconnect skipped every piece of local cleanup when no connection object had been injected

**File:** `src/app/AppController.cpp` lines 651–689.

**What the code did before.** `disconnectServer()` opened with an early return if the object
that owns the SSH session was null. That object is injected at startup and may legitimately be
null in tests; because it is held through a `QPointer`, it also reads back null once that object
has been destroyed.

Everything after the early return is purely client-side state, and none of it needs the
connection object to be correct: the latch that refuses a second connect while one is already
running, the fingerprint of a host key the user is currently being asked to approve, the
password or passphrase typed so far, the identifier of the profile being dialled, a connect
error held back pending the outcome of a prompt, and the active Dev Session together with both
of its pane-layout trees.

**Why that is wrong.** Two concrete routes. The first is reachable today through AC2 and the
schema-floor branch: those defer `disconnectServer()` by one event-loop turn specifically so the
connection's transport can be torn down safely. If the connection object is null at that moment,
the call returned immediately, the "a connect is in progress" latch stayed set, and every
subsequent connect attempt was refused for the rest of the process. The only recovery is
restarting the application.

The second is simply the wrong contract. A "Disconnect" that silently keeps a typed password, an
outstanding host-key approval and a live Dev Session pointed at an unreachable server is not a
disconnect. Leaving the Dev Session active is the exact corruption a long comment further down
the same function warns about: the layout manager keeps accepting pane splits it cannot save,
and the first change that *does* reach the server writes that divergent layout over the server's
real one.

**What changed.** The early return was removed so all the client-side resets run unconditionally,
and the check was narrowed to the one statement that actually needs the connection object. The
scope guard that mutes teardown-induced failures is kept exactly as it was, so the muting window
still ends precisely when the teardown ends. The stale part of the leading comment was replaced
with one explaining why there is no gate.

**Test.** No dedicated test. It is exercised by AC1's and AC2's new tests, both of which go
through `disconnectServer`, and by the existing
`disconnectRetiresActiveSessionButStillRemembersIt`.

## AC4 — Desktop notifications carried no application identity, so notification centres could not attribute them

**File:** `src/app/Notifier.cpp` lines 23–28 and 112–125.

**What the code did before.** The notifier builds a call to the standard Linux desktop
notification service. That call takes eight arguments; the seventh is a dictionary of optional
hints. CodeHarbor passed an empty dictionary.

**Why that is wrong.** The freedesktop.org specification defines a standard hint,
`desktop-entry`, whose value is the base name of the application's launcher file. Notification
centres — GNOME Shell, KDE Plasma, mako, dunst — use it to find the application's real icon,
show its proper display name, and group and filter its notifications in per-application
settings. Without it they fall back to fuzzy-matching a free-text application name against
installed launcher files, which frequently fails.

It was also an internal inconsistency: `src/app/main.cpp` goes out of its way to tell Qt the
application's launcher file name, with a six-line comment explaining that this is what
associates the running window with its icon in the dock — but the notification path, the other
place the operating system needs the same association, did not supply it.

**What changed.** A file-local constant was added alongside the existing application-name and
icon constants, with a comment naming the two things it must stay in step with
(`packaging/codeharbor.desktop` and the call in `main.cpp`), and the hint is now passed.
Notification services that do not understand the hint ignore it, so there is no risk.

**Test.** None. The hint is only observable against a real notification service, and the existing
live gate already covers "a real notification call reaches a real service without erroring".
Asserting the argument list of a message we just constructed would test plumbing.

## AC5 — A comment asserted that a user-facing "notifications off" switch exists; it does not

**File:** `src/app/Notifier.h` line 49.

**What the code did before.** The notifier exposes an enable/disable gate, and the comment above
it read "Gate for the user-facing 'notifications off' switch."

**Why that is wrong.** There is no such switch. `main.cpp` deliberately does not expose the
notifier to the user interface layer at all — its comment calls the object "a pure C++ sink" —
nothing outside the tests ever calls the setter, and nothing persists the flag, so even if a
switch existed it would forget itself on every launch. A reader trusting the comment goes looking
for a settings control that was never built.

The specification was checked before deciding what to do: section 6.2 of `docs/SPEC.md` is
"Supported Harnesses" and has nothing to do with notifications, and neither `docs/SPEC.md` nor
`docs/PLAN.md` anywhere requires a switch to turn notifications off. So the feature is
unspecified. Building a settings surface plus a persisted setting would have been inventing
scope; deleting the gate would have removed working, tested code.

**What changed.** The comment now describes what the gate actually is — a programmatic gate on
delivery, with no control wired to it, stated as a plain fact about the current design rather
than as a to-do. The original sentence about what a disabled call does was kept verbatim. No code
changed.

## AC6 — Six comments cited a specification section that is about something else entirely

**Files:** `src/app/Notifier.h` lines 9–14; `src/app/main.cpp` lines 137–139;
`src/app/tests/tst_notifier.cpp` lines 82 and 270; `src/app/tests/tst_notifierlive.cpp` lines 10
and 47.

**What the code did before.** Six comments cited "SPEC 6.2" as the authority for the desktop
notification feature and for the agent-state names it reacts to.

**Why that is wrong.** Section 6 of `docs/SPEC.md` is "Agent Awareness", and its subsections are
6.1 Purpose, 6.2 Supported Harnesses, 6.3 Remote Agent Bridge, 6.4 Internal Event Schema, 6.5 Oh
My Pi Adapter, 6.6 Fallback Activity Detection. So 6.2 is only about which coding-assistant
programs are recognised. The state names those comments actually depend on (`waiting_input`,
`idle_unseen`) are defined in 6.4. Desktop notifications have no subsection anywhere; the only
mention in the whole document is a Phase 4 bullet in section 16, "MVP Plan". Every one of the six
pointed a reader at the wrong place.

**What changed.** Where a comment depends on the state names, it now cites 6.4 "Internal Event
Schema". Where a comment reads as though a section mandates desktop notifications, it now says
plainly that the display layer is a Phase 4 deliverable listed in section 16 and that section 6
defines only the states it reacts to — no section number was invented, because none exists. Two
of the six were not making a normative claim at all and simply lost the citation in favour of a
plain description. The specification itself was not edited: this round deliberately did not add
new normative text. The token that the build system searches for in order to report the live
notification test as skipped rather than passed was left untouched.

## AC7 — A test-only shim read the application object from a second thread that could not know whether it still existed

**Files:** `src/app/tests/headlessquit.cpp` (rewritten);
`src/app/tests/headlessquithost.cpp` (new); `src/app/tests/tst_headlessquit.cpp` (new);
`src/app/tests/CMakeLists.txt` lines 130–151 (new).

**What the code did before.** `headlessquit.cpp` is a small shared library that the live
user-interface gate loads into the shipped `codeharbor` binary through `LD_PRELOAD`, without
modifying that binary. Its job is to make the application quit through its own event loop, so
the gate can assert that shutdown runs every destructor rather than just killing the process.

It did that from a background thread. The library's constructor started a detached thread which
slept, then polled `QCoreApplication::instance()` up to six hundred times waiting for the
application object to appear, copied the pointer into a local variable, checked it for null, and
called `QMetaObject::invokeMethod(app, "quit", Qt::QueuedConnection)` on the copy.

**Why that is wrong.** Reading a pointer and using it are two separate steps, and nothing
ordered them against the main thread. `QCoreApplication` clears the global instance pointer from
inside its own destructor, so the poll can read a non-null pointer while the main thread is
already destroying that object; by the time `invokeMethod` runs — it reads the object's
metaobject and posts an event into the object's thread queue — the storage can be freed. That is
a use-after-free.

The previous round of this hunt recorded this as deliberately left alone, on the grounds that
closing it "needs synchronisation with the main thread that the shim deliberately does not have,
since it must work inside an unmodified binary it cannot add code to". That reasoning was wrong.
The shim does not need to synchronise with the main thread; it needs to stop being on a
different thread at all. Qt offers exactly that hook — `qAddPreRoutine` registers a function that
`QCoreApplication`'s constructor calls, on the main thread, after it has published the instance
pointer and created the thread's event dispatcher. It requires no cooperation from the host
binary, which is the constraint the previous round thought ruled a fix out.

**What changed.** The fix is structural, not a wider timing margin. The background thread was
deleted outright. The library's constructor now only reads and validates its environment
variables and calls `qAddPreRoutine`. The registered routine runs on the main thread at the
moment the application exists, and creates a single-shot `QTimer` as a **child of the
application object**, connected to `QCoreApplication::quit`:

* Because the timer is a child, it cannot outlive the object it touches: destroying a `QObject`
  destroys its children, and the timer's own destructor stops it before the application's
  storage goes away.
* Because everything happens on the application's own thread, there is no interleaving between
  a read and a use for the main thread to slip into.

So the pointer that could dangle no longer exists, rather than existing for a shorter time. A
twenty-one-line comment in the file records the old design and why the new one closes the
window, so the next reader does not reintroduce the thread.

Three smaller changes came with it: the value of `CH_QUIT_AFTER_MS` is now clamped to one hour
before the narrowing conversion to the `int` that `QTimer::start` takes, instead of letting an
absurd value wrap to a negative number; the `<thread>` and `<chrono>` includes were replaced by
`<QTimer>` and `<QObject>`; and the file's leading comment now says the delay is measured from
the application coming up, which is what the code does, where it previously described the
load-then-poll behaviour. The environment gating and the Linux-only guard around the whole file
are unchanged, and the delay the live gate asks for (8 seconds) is far inside its own 120-second
limit, so no caller's margin moved.

**Test.** The shim had no test of its own. It was exercised only by `tst_liveshell`, which skips
itself when no SSH fixture is present, so a broken shim was invisible on most machines. Two new
files fix that:

* `src/app/tests/headlessquithost.cpp` — a minimal stand-in for the shipped binary. It is a
  `QGuiApplication` running under the offscreen platform plugin on purpose: the arming happens
  inside the `QCoreApplication` constructor, that is, before `QGuiApplication` has finished
  building itself, so "can a timer be started that early in a GUI application" is part of what
  has to be proven, and a plain `QCoreApplication` host would not prove it. It reports through
  its exit code — 0 for a clean quit, 3 if no quit ever arrived (it has its own five-second
  watchdog), 4 if a quit arrived but no timer owned by the application existed, 5 if anything
  reached the application as a queued call from another thread.
* `src/app/tests/tst_headlessquit.cpp` — a QtTest driver that runs that host as a child process
  with the shim preloaded. Three cases: the quit is delivered by a timer the application owns;
  nothing happens without the trigger variable; nothing happens in a process whose name is not
  the target.

The assertion is deliberately the **ownership invariant**, not a timing margin: the quit must
come from a timer the application owns, and nothing may reach the application as a cross-thread
queued call. That is the property that makes the use-after-free impossible.

Built and run out of tree with Qt 6.10.2, all three cases pass. Rebuilding only the shim from
the pre-fix source and running the same test binary fails the first case with exit code 4
("app-owned timers = 0"), and the two negative cases still pass against both versions — so the
failure is specifically about who owns the quit, not about the old shim being broken in general.
The pass and the failure were both repeatable.

**The race itself was not reproduced, and no attempt is made to claim otherwise.** A separate
attempt was made to fire the pre-fix use-after-free directly: a purpose-built host under
AddressSanitizer that deletes a heap-allocated `QCoreApplication` at the exact millisecond the
shim's thread wakes, run two hundred times with jitter. It produced zero AddressSanitizer
reports. That is not evidence the bug was absent — the window is a few instructions wide and
cannot be hit on demand. It is the reason the fix had to be structural and the reason the
regression test asserts the ownership invariant instead of trying to win the race.

### Checked carefully in this area and found sound

Recorded so the next reader does not repeat the work: the destruction order of every object in
`main.cpp` was traced against every pointer held to it; the `QPointer` discipline in
`AppController`, where every asynchronous callback captures one and returns early if it reads back
null; the duplicate-connection handling in the two injection setters; the out-of-order-reply guard
in the notifier, walked through four interleavings; the refresh generation guard that stops a
superseded reply or a failed request from retiring a live Dev Session; and the credential and
host-key chain, where secrets are captured by value and cleared on use and a passphrase has no
code path into password authentication.

Because `docs/SPEC.md` was edited during this round, every specification citation in the area's
files was re-checked against the current document. The edits touched sections 3.3, 7.5, 8.1 and
19; no comment in this area cites any of those, and the section numbering did not move, so the
corrections made in AC5 and AC6 still match the document. No further change was needed.

# AS — application state

## AS1 — Turning automatic reconnect off during a user's own connect left the client stuck on "Connecting" forever

**Files:** `src/app/SessionBootstrap.cpp` lines 493–520; `src/app/SessionBootstrap.h` lines
147–157 and 497–503.

**What the code did before.** `SessionBootstrap` brings the remote session up: it probes the
endpoint, performs the SSH handshake, and attaches the two data channels. It also owns an
automatic-reconnect ladder that retries after a session dies. `setReconnectEnabled(false)` turns
that ladder off. Its old body unconditionally stopped the pending retry timer, aborted whatever
attempt was running, and — only if the state happened to be "reconnecting" — moved to
"disconnected".

The abort routine does not distinguish "a retry the ladder started" from "a connect the user
clicked Connect for". Both kinds of attempt spend most of their time parked in a nested event
loop: the connect pre-flight (up to 5 seconds) or a provisioning step (up to 3 minutes).

**Why that is wrong.** The connect routine deliberately does **not** relabel a cancelled attempt
as failed, on the reasoning that whoever cancelled has chosen the end state. That is correct for
the deliberate-disconnect path, which sets "disconnected" itself, and it is the only caller for
which it is correct. When `setReconnectEnabled(false)` cancelled a user's connect, nobody set an
end state at all: no session, no error signal, no retry armed, and the state stuck on
"Connecting" for the rest of the process, with a spinner that never resolves. This is reachable —
the setter is public, non-blocking API, and both nested loops keep the event loop turning on
purpose, so a timer or any queued action can land inside the window.

**What changed.** A flag records whether the running attempt is a ladder retry (set by the
reconnect timer) or a user connect (cleared by the connect routine). `setReconnectEnabled(false)`
now aborts only a retry, and when it does it sets "disconnected" itself, which is correct because
the rung is gone and nothing will re-arm it. Turning the ladder off while a rung is merely armed
behaves exactly as before. The header documents the boundary.

**Test.** New: `TstSessionBootstrap::disablingReconnectDuringAUserConnectLeavesItAlone`. It parks
a real connect in the production pre-flight against a local server that never answers, flips the
switch 30 milliseconds in, and asserts the connect still reaches its own conclusion, that no
retry was armed, that a subsequent connect still works (so the object is not wedged), and that
reconnect really is off. Against the pre-fix code the state is "Connecting" where "Failed" is
expected.

## AS2 — A cancellation flag could outlive its attempt and permanently disable reconnection

**File:** `src/app/SessionBootstrap.cpp` lines 603–610.

**What the code did before.** The cancellation flag means "the attempt currently running was
cancelled; unwind without wiring, failing or retrying". It is cleared in exactly one place: the
top of the routine that *starts* an attempt. The scheduler that arms the next retry returns
immediately when the flag is set. The handler for a live session dying tore the session down,
told the user, reset the attempt counter, and called the scheduler without touching the flag.

**Why that is wrong.** If the flag is still set when a live session dies, the loss path unwires
both channels, reports the error, and then arms nothing. The object is left claiming to be fully
wired while both of its devices have been destroyed. Nothing can recover it, because that same
handler only acts from the wired state, so a second loss is ignored too. AS1 was one route into
this state; AS3 is another.

**What changed.** The loss handler now clears the flag before scheduling, with a comment naming
the failure mode. The invariant is simple: losing a live session is by definition not a cancelled
attempt.

**Test.** None added — this is a defence-in-depth guard behind AS1's route. Every existing
loss-path case still passes, which is what confirms the clear is not itself a behaviour change.

## AS3 — A cancellation arriving between provisioning steps was ignored, and the next step burned its whole budget

**File:** `src/app/SessionBootstrap.cpp` lines 808–823 and 1316–1324.

**What the code did before.** Bringing up a bare server runs up to three remote scripts in
sequence: inspect the server, install the remote service, inspect again to confirm. Each one
opens a throwaway SSH channel and waits in a nested event loop until the remote closes it or the
time budget elapses — 15 seconds for an inspection, **180 seconds** for an install.

The abort routine cancels a parked attempt by setting the flag *and* quitting the loop it is
parked in. Between two steps there is no loop of ours, so only the flag gets set. The
script-runner did not look at the flag on entry: it opened a channel, ran a remote command, and
waited. Separately, the wiring routine never re-checked the flag after provisioning returned, and
provisioning reports a cancellation as failure only when one of its own steps failed — a
cancellation landing between steps leaves it reporting success.

**Why that is wrong.** Two consequences. First, the user asks to disconnect during a first
connect, and the client answers by running a command on a machine it has just been told to leave
alone, freezing the window for up to three more minutes before noticing. The interruptibility
this area was built for does not cover the gaps between steps. Second, and worse: with
provisioning reporting success, the wiring routine would go on to open both channels, attach both
consumers and announce the session as wired — resurrecting the very session the user just tore
down, and leaving the cancellation flag set on a live wired session, which is exactly AS2's
input.

**What changed.** The script-runner now returns "cancelled" before touching the connection pool if
the flag is already set. The wiring routine re-checks the flag immediately after provisioning
succeeds, before anything is executed.

**Test.** None added. Driving a cancellation into the exact gap between two steps needs a seam
that does not exist, and the existing test seam short-circuits the nested loop entirely, so a
substitute cannot reproduce the timing. All fifteen existing provisioning cases and both
cancellation cases still pass, which covers the "does not change the ordinary paths" half.

## AS4 — Integer overflow in the pane-label counter could hand out a label a visible pane is already wearing

**File:** `src/app/SessionLayouts.cpp` lines 79–110, 213–239 and 596–604.

**What the code did before.** Every pane in a region carries a label like `viewer-3` or
`terminal-2`. The number is chosen as the larger of a counter stored in a local settings file and
one more than the highest number already present in the layout tree; after a split, the counter is
stored as the chosen number plus one.

Neither input belongs to this client. The layout tree is fetched from the remote server and other
clients write to it too. The counter is read out of a hand-editable settings file. Both were used
in "plus one" arithmetic with no bound.

**Why that is wrong.** With either input at the maximum value a 32-bit signed integer can hold,
"plus one" is signed overflow — undefined behaviour, and in practice a wrap to the most negative
value. The wrapped value is stored as a negative; the settings store then applies its documented
floor and repairs it to 1; and the next split therefore mints `viewer-1`, the label of a pane that
is still on screen. This was confirmed end to end: with the fix reverted, two consecutive splits
over such a store mint the *same* label. Two panes wearing one label is confusing on its own, and
it is also what makes a stale "resolve this terminal by its slot label" answer plausible again —
the class of bug the pane-identifier scheme exists to close.

**What changed.** A ceiling of one billion was introduced: far above any real Dev Session, and far
enough below the integer maximum that every addition here is representable. Labels above the
ceiling are ignored when scanning the tree. A *stored* counter above the ceiling is treated as
corrupt and the code falls back to the tree — deliberately not clamped to the ceiling, because the
ceiling is a fixed point and clamping would make every later split mint the identical label. (That
version was written first and the new test caught it.) The stored value is capped so the next read
never has to treat it as corrupt.

**Test.** New: `TstSessionLayouts::anAbsurdPaneCounterCannotHandOutALabelAlreadyOnScreen`. It seeds
the counter at the integer maximum through the production interface, loads a one-pane layout,
splits twice, and asserts each minted label is a well-formed positive suffix, that neither is a
label already on screen, that the two do not collide with each other, and that the published tree
contains each pane identifier exactly once. Against the pre-fix arithmetic, the two minted labels
are identical.

## AS5 — The settings writer accepted values its own reader refuses

**Files:** `src/app/UiStateStore.cpp` lines 127–137; `src/app/UiStateStore.h` lines 85–88.

**What the code did before.** The reader for the pane-label counter enforces a documented floor: a
stored value that is absent, non-numeric or below 1 reads back as 1. The writer applied no floor at
all — it dropped an empty Dev Session identifier and otherwise stored whatever it was handed,
including zero and negatives, and then flushed it to disk.

**Why that is wrong.** A check applied in one place and missing in its twin. The two halves
disagree about what the store contains: a caller stores zero, the reader silently repairs it to 1,
and the counter has quietly forgotten every label already spent — which is a duplicate pane label,
the one thing the counter exists to prevent. It also spent a synchronous disk write (which the
surrounding code goes out of its way to avoid elsewhere) on a value that can never be read back.
And it made AS4's wrapped negative *look* like a stored value rather than a rejected one.

**What changed.** The writer now returns without writing for a value below the floor, mirroring the
reader exactly, and the header documents that a write below 1 is dropped for the same reason a read
below 1 is repaired. No current caller changes behaviour.

**Test.** None added; this is a hardening of an internal invariant with no observable change for
any current caller. AS4's test defends the end-to-end property it supports.

## AS6 — One test wrote the developer's real application settings on macOS and Windows

**File:** `src/app/tests/tst_liveshell.cpp` lines 337–360.

**What the code did before.** The suite redirects its settings storage to a throwaway directory so
it never touches the developer's own configuration. On Linux it does that with an environment
variable. On every other platform Qt ignores that variable, so the fallback branch simply used the
real native configuration location and wrote there. The stated reason is sound: the live cases
launch child processes that must address the same store, and Qt's process-local test-mode switch
cannot be inherited by a child.

**Why that is wrong.** The reasoning only covers the *live* cases. One case in this file needs no
live fixture and therefore runs in the default suite on every machine — and its very first
statement writes region widths through the production settings store. So on a macOS or Windows
developer machine, a plain test run silently overwrote that developer's stored sidebar and terminal
widths and then moved them around further. The sibling gate `tst_coldstart` already draws exactly
this line in its own entry point; this file did not.

**What changed.** The non-Linux branch now enables Qt's test mode when there is no live fixture —
precisely the condition under which no child process is spawned, so process-local isolation is both
safe and correct. The live path is untouched. A comment explains where the line falls and
cross-references the sibling gate.

## AS7 — A test comment denied two features that exist

**File:** `src/app/tests/tst_coldstart.cpp` lines 1243–1251.

**What the code did before.** A step of the cold-start walkthrough carried a comment saying that
the persisted layout tree "carries only pane identifiers" and that "the shipped user interface has
no open-a-file affordance at all".

**Why that is wrong.** Both halves are false. The layout node type **has** a URL member, it *is*
persisted, and there is a dedicated method to record it. The shipped interface **does** have the
affordance: the directory view reports a file being opened, the pane relays it upward, and the main
window feeds it straight into the layout manager. Worse, the very next step of this same test
asserts that the restored layout matches — which only works *because* the URL is persisted. A
reader trusting the comment would conclude that half of the next step's guarantee is impossible.

**What changed.** The comment now says what is actually true: the test bypasses the user interface
because the shipped affordance needs a directory pane already showing the file's parent —
navigation this gate has no business rehearsing — and it hands the region the same node shape the
main window's own fallback uses, carrying the URL that the next step relies on.

## AS8 — Wrong specification section cited for the two-browser-profile design

**File:** `src/app/tests/tst_liveshell.cpp` lines 881–885.

**What the code did before.** The comment explaining why the relaunched application binary must not
be given a particular Chromium flag said "the viewer stack creates two by design (SPEC 7.2)".

**Why that is wrong.** Section 7.2 of `docs/SPEC.md` is "Arbitrary Websites". The section that
requires two separate browser profiles — one sandboxed for external sites, one privileged for the
application's own pages — is 7.3, "Browser Profiles". A reader chasing the citation lands on the
wrong requirement.

**What changed.** Corrected to 7.3, with the section title and the actual split spelled out so the
citation is checkable without opening the specification. Every other specification citation in the
owned files was judged individually and verified against the headings; all the rest were correct as
written.

## AS9 — An out-of-range stored port number had no test coverage

**Files:** finding in `src/app/ServerProfiles.cpp` lines 76–108 and 197; test added at
`src/app/tests/tst_serverprofiles.cpp` lines 722–806.

**The routed premise, and its correction.** This was routed in from the application-core reviewer,
who reported that saved server profiles never range-check the TCP port number, so a hand-edited
value of 70000 would silently become 4464 through a narrowing conversion at the one place that
reads the field. That reviewer was right to decline to add a second validation site in someone
else's area — but the premise turned out to be false for the current code. Both sides already
validate: the save path rejects anything outside 1 to 65535, and the load path substitutes the SSH
default of 22 when the stored value fails the same range check. The narrowing conversion at the
consumer is therefore already safe, and **no production change was made**; adding one would have
been exactly the duplicated rule this class avoids.

**What was genuinely wrong.** The *coverage*. The only test touching this seeded the port as a
non-numeric string, which converts to zero and is caught by the **lower** bound alone. The upper
bound and the negative case had no test at all. Deleting the upper-bound check therefore reproduced
precisely the reported 70000-becomes-4464 bug **with a fully green suite** — which is how a defect
that does not exist today gets introduced tomorrow. The legal boundaries were also unpinned, so a
"clamp everything to 22" regression would have gone unnoticed too.

**What changed.** New test: `TstServerProfiles::outOfRangeStoredPortsAreRepairedNotTruncated`. It
hand-writes six profiles in the documented on-disk layout — 70000, −1, 65558, 0, and the two legal
boundaries 65535 and 1 — then asserts that the four bad ones read back as 22 and the two legal ones
read back unchanged; that every port a consumer can see survives the narrowing conversion unchanged,
which is the exact property the consumer depends on; that the repair is written back on the next
save, so no out-of-range value is left in the file for an older build to pick up; and that the save
path still refuses the same values, so the load path is repairing hand edits rather than covering
for this class's own writer. With the upper-bound check removed, the test reports 70000 where 22 is
expected.

## AS10 — The cold-start walkthrough compared terminal output that now legitimately contains colour codes

**File:** `src/app/tests/tst_coldstart.cpp` — `paneText()`, lines 658–682.

Found by the coordinator's live run, *after* `SSH2` was fixed and after the round's first commit. It is a
consequence of `SSH2`, so it is recorded here rather than being folded into it silently.

**What the code did before.** The cold-start gate is the full first-run walkthrough against a real server. One of
its steps proves the terminal pane's shell opened in the Dev Session's checkout rather than in the user's home
directory: it types a command that prints a marker and the working directory, scrapes the pane's accumulated output
for that marker with a pattern that captures everything up to the end of the line, trims the whitespace, and
compares the result against the expected path.

**Why that is wrong.** It assumed the captured line is plain text. That held only because of the very bug `SSH2`
fixed: the pane used to negotiate a plain `xterm` terminal, so a shell that colours its prompt mostly did not
bother. Now that the pane really gets `xterm-256color`, the shell writes colour and erase-to-end-of-line control
sequences in among its output, and they land on the same line as the value being scraped. The comparison then failed
with the right path followed by an escape sequence — an assertion failure that says nothing about the property under
test, on a gate whose whole purpose is to be trusted.

This is worth stating plainly rather than dressing up: the fix was correct and the test was fragile, and the
fragility was invisible for exactly as long as the bug was present.

**What changed.** `paneText()`, the one helper every step scrapes through, now strips terminal control sequences
before returning: escape-bracket sequences, operating-system commands, and the two-character escapes. Deliberately
not a terminal parser — a comment says so — it only has to stop escape bytes reaching a comparison. Nothing in this
gate is testing terminal rendering; every assertion wants the text the remote printed. No assertion was changed and
no other step was touched.

**Verification.** The cold-start gate was re-run on its own after the change and passes, and the whole live gate was
then run twice consecutively, 13/13 both times. The failure was also traced to its cause rather than assumed: the
captured value was the correct path followed by an erase-to-end-of-line, a green and a bold sequence, which is a
coloured prompt being redrawn.

## AS11 — A re-entrancy guard in the profile store could never fire, and was removed in favour of the rule it was silently enforcing

**Files:** `src/app/ServerProfiles.cpp` — the guarded branch in `persist()` and the invariant that
replaced it; `src/app/ServerProfiles.h` lines 229–232, the deleted member.

**What the code did before.** The server-profile store writes to disk under a `QLockFile`, so two
processes cannot interleave a merge. Alongside that it carried a boolean member, `m_persisting`,
set on entry to `persist()` and cleared after the unlock, with an early return if it was already
set. The intent was to stop a save that somehow re-entered `persist()` from deadlocking against a
file lock its own call stack already holds.

**Why that is wrong.** The previous round examined this branch, agreed it was unreachable, and kept
it anyway, on the reasoning that it is "a correct and cheap safety net against a future emit moving
inside the locked region". Under the stricter bar that is not good enough: it is a branch no test
can reach and no reader can evaluate, and its cheapness is not the point.

The reachability was re-derived from the source rather than taken on trust. The region between
`tryLock()` and `unlock()` is exactly `mergeAndWrite()`, which calls `QSettings::sync()`,
`readStoredProfiles()`, `writeEntry()` and `restrictPermissions()` — no signal emission, no
callback, no nested event loop. The one signal the class emits, `saveDegraded(...)`, is emitted
after `lock.unlock()` **and** after the guard had already been cleared. So even on the single
re-entrant path that genuinely exists — a handler for `saveDegraded` that calls back into the store
— the guard was observed as false. It was false at every entry to `persist()`, always.

**What changed.** The member and its early-out branch were deleted. In their place the property the
guard was insuring is written down as an enforced invariant on `persist()`: nothing between
`tryLock()` and `unlock()` may emit a signal, run a callback, or spin an event loop. The comment
spells out the consequence of breaking it — `QLockFile` is not recursive, so a nested save would
wait out the full 1.5-second acquisition budget against a lock held by its own call stack and then
report that "another process" holds it, about itself — and states the placement rule for future
code: anything new that emits belongs after the unlock too. The header carries a one-line pointer
at that statement.

**Test.** New: `TstServerProfiles::aSaveDegradedHandlerMayMutateTheStore()`. It holds the merge lock
from a live `QLockFile`, so every save takes the degraded path and therefore really does emit;
connects a `saveDegraded` handler that calls `addProfile()` back into the store; and asserts the
nested save completes, that both profiles survive a reopen, and that the whole sequence finishes
inside a bound a self-deadlocked wait could not meet (two sequential 1.5-second timeouts, asserted
under 15000 ms).

**This test does not fail against the pre-fix code, and cannot.** That is stated plainly rather than
rounded up. Removing dead code is not a behaviour change. Rather than assume that, the guard was put
back verbatim — both halves — and the suite rebuilt and re-run: the case passed, exactly as it does
without the guard. That run *is* this item's evidence. Identical behaviour with and without the
guard is precisely the proof that the guard was unreachable. The only test that could have failed
beforehand would be one asserting on source text, which the house rules forbid. What the new test
buys is coverage of the *reachable* re-entrancy path — the one the written invariant is about —
which previously had none.

## AS12 — Closing the last pane of a one-child split left a blank slot nested in the layout tree

**Files:** `src/app/SessionLayouts.cpp` lines 61–100 and 833–853; `src/app/SessionLayouts.h` lines
157–163 and 329–333; test in `src/app/tests/tst_sessionlayouts.cpp`.

**What the code did before.** A region's panes are arranged as a tree: leaves are panes, branches
are splits holding an ordered list of children plus a matching list of size ratios. Closing a pane
removed its leaf and then applied a one-level repair: a branch left holding exactly one child was
replaced by that child.

**Why that is wrong.** The parser accepts a split with exactly one child — `SplitNode::tryFromJson`
(`src/models/SplitTree.cpp:130`) rejects only an *empty* child list — and the layout row is stored
on the server, where other clients write to it too, so this client cannot assume it never receives
one. Removing that only child left the branch with zero children, which is not one, so the repair
did not fire. A branch with no children serialises as a leaf with no pane identifier: a blank slot
in the region that no pane can occupy and nothing short of a reload clears.

The previous round saw this and left it, calling the outcome cosmetic and the recursive fix out of
proportion. The outcome is a permanently unusable slice of the user's region, and the fix is
contained.

**What changed.** The single-level repair in `SessionLayouts::dropLeaf()` was replaced with a
recursive one. A new file-local helper, `dropLeafFrom(SplitNode&, const QString&)`, returns
`NotFound`, `Removed` or `Emptied`; `dropLeaf()` dispatches to it and turns an emptied *root* into
the placeholder empty node that an emptied region is supposed to keep. On the way back up a branch
left with one child is replaced by that child, as before, and a branch left with no children reports
`Emptied` so its own parent drops it in turn. Ratio removal is now index-guarded rather than
assuming the ratio list and the child list are in step. The guarantee is therefore total: the root
is the only place an empty node can survive. The contracts on `closePane()` and `dropLeaf()` were
updated to say so.

**Test.** New: `TstSessionLayouts::closingTheOnlyChildOfAOneChildSplitLeavesNoBlankSlot()`. It uses
two shapes because they fail differently: in the viewer region the one-child split is a *sibling* of
two real panes, so its removal must leave those two and their ratios untouched; in the terminal
region one-child splits are nested two deep, so emptiness must propagate more than one level before
the root collapses onto the survivor. Both the published tree and the `workspace.setLayout` payload
sent to the server are asserted.

Against the pre-fix body of `dropLeaf()`, rebuilt and run, the test fails with the blank slot
visible in the actual value:

```
FAIL!  : closingTheOnlyChildOfAOneChildSplitLeavesNoBlankSlot() Compared values are not the same
   Actual   : {"children":[{"paneId":"viewer-1",...},{"paneId":"viewer-2",...},
                           {"paneId":"","type":"leaf"}],"ratios":[1,2,3],...}
   Expected : {"children":[{"paneId":"viewer-1",...},{"paneId":"viewer-2",...}],
               "ratios":[1,2],...}
```

## AS13 — The layout publisher deep-copied the whole tree on every republish, including every ratio-drag frame

**Files:** `src/app/SessionLayouts.cpp` lines 308–368 and 547–556; `src/app/SessionLayouts.h` lines
270–277.

**What the code did before.** One entry point, `setTree(int index, SplitNode tree)`, took the tree
**by value** and then moved it into the slot. Almost every internal caller passed the slot's own
current tree — that is, the object the function was about to overwrite.

**Why that is wrong.** The previous round defended the by-value parameter with "the copy is what
makes the subsequent move safe, since moving the member onto itself would be a self-move". That
reasoning is true of the shape it was written for, but the shape *is* the problem. The by-value copy
is a full deep copy of the split tree — every node, every string, every ratio vector — and it ran on
every ratio-drag frame, every pane-URL echo, every split, every close and every terminal republish.
