The self-move it was defending against only exists because the function insisted on being handed a
tree it already owned. The right correction is not to change the parameter's category but to stop
asking republishing callers for a tree at all.

**What changed.** The one entry point was split into two:

* `setTree(int, SplitNode)` and `setTreeQuietly(int, SplitNode)` — *adopt* a new tree. Still by value
  plus move, which is correct here: these callers genuinely hand over a tree they own.
* `publishTree(int)` and `publishTreeQuietly(int)` — *republish* the slot's current tree. No
  parameter, so there is nothing to copy and no self-move to avoid.

Every caller was migrated. `setRatios`, `splitPane`, both terminal-mint callbacks,
`bindTerminalPaneRow`, `closePane` and `setPaneUrl` now call the parameterless form; `saveTree()`
moves its parsed tree instead of copying it (`parsed` is no longer `const`). The only remaining
`setTree` call is `applyLoadedTree`'s `setTree(index, std::move(next))`, which is a genuine
adoption. No internal caller passes the member any more.

**Test.** None added, deliberately. This is a pure refactor with no observable contract of its own:
pre-fix and post-fix behaviour are identical by construction, so there is nothing that could fail
beforehand, and a test written here would be testing plumbing, which the house rules forbid. What
covers it is the existing `tst_sessionlayouts` suite, which exercises every migrated caller and
passes unchanged. That is coverage of the callers, not new coverage of this change.

## AS14 — A terminal-pane creation the server never answered blocked that region's writes for the rest of the session

**Files:** `src/app/SessionLayouts.cpp` lines 241–244 and 723–818; `src/app/SessionLayouts.h` lines
147–153, 190–205, 318–321 and 351–352.

**What the code did before.** Splitting a terminal pane inserts a leaf immediately and asks the
server, over JSON-RPC, to create the matching terminal row; the leaf is only complete once that
answer arrives and supplies its server-side identifier. While any leaf is still waiting, the class
deliberately refuses to write the region's layout back to the server — writing a tree containing an
identifier-less terminal leaf would persist a pane no client can ever resolve. That refusal is
load-bearing and documented. What it lacked was an end: if the request was simply never answered,
the leaf stayed pending forever and the region's writes stayed blocked for the rest of the session.

**Why that is wrong.** The previous round left this, reasoning that the connection object fails
every pending request when the transport closes, so the callback always arrives, and a timeout here
would duplicate that mechanism in the wrong layer. The first half is true; the conclusion does not
follow. That mechanism only sees a peer that has gone **silent**. Its detector is
`kDefaultHeartbeatIntervalMs` (15000 ms) times `kDefaultHeartbeatMisses` (4), roughly 60 seconds of
total silence, and `src/remote/CodeharbordClient.h:105` states that there is deliberately **no**
per-request timeout at all. So the case it cannot see is precisely the one that matters here: a
connection that stays perfectly healthy while this one request goes missing.

**What changed.** The mint is now bounded.

* New production API `setTerminalMintTimeoutMs(int)` / `terminalMintTimeoutMs()`, with
  `static constexpr int kDefaultTerminalMintTimeoutMs = 90000`. Passing `0` disables it, matching
  `SessionBootstrap::setConnectTimeoutMs(0)`.
* `mintTerminalPaneRow()` arms a `QTimer::singleShot` beside the RPC. The answer and the deadline
  share a `std::make_shared<bool>` settle flag, so whichever arrives first wins and the other is a
  no-op, and the timer's lambda repeats the same `QPointer`, generation and Dev-Session-identifier
  guards the reply callback already used.
* Both failure outcomes funnel through one new private method, `abandonTerminalMint(paneId,
  reason)`: report the reason, drop the half-made leaf, republish the tree, persist. The error-answer
  path was already doing exactly this; extracting it means the timeout and the error answer cannot
  drift apart.
* The contract comment on `splitPane()` now names the third failure mode and the unblocking.

The shape was taken from the codebase rather than invented: a named `constexpr int k...TimeoutMs`
with a comment justifying the number (`SessionBootstrap::kInspectTimeoutMs`, `kProvisionTimeoutMs`,
`kDefaultConnectTimeoutMs`, `Notifier::kExpireTimeoutMs`), a settable override that is production
API rather than a test seam (`SessionBootstrap::setConnectTimeoutMs`, where `0` means off), and
`QTimer::singleShot` with a `QPointer` self-guard (`AppController.cpp:814`).

**Why 90 seconds.** The number is chosen to sit **above** the transport's own silent-peer detection
at roughly 60 seconds, not below it. That ordering is the whole point: when the peer really has gone
silent, the transport notices first, fails the pending request itself, and its better-worded error
is the one the user sees. This timeout only fires in the case the transport cannot see. Were it set
below 60 seconds it would pre-empt the transport and report a vaguer error for a failure the
transport diagnoses properly. It is not a latency budget for the operation; it is the bound that
stops one unanswered creation disabling a whole region's writes. The comment on the constant says
exactly this.

**Test.** New: `TstSessionLayouts::aTerminalMintThatIsNeverAnsweredStopsBlockingTheRegion()`. It
splits a terminal pane, reads the `workspace.createTerminalPane` frame off the fake server and never
answers it, then asserts in order: that the region is correctly unwritable while the leaf is pending
(the behaviour being *bounded*, not removed); that the reported error names the pane and says the
creation was never answered; that the half-made leaf is taken back out, so no identifier-less
terminal leaf can reach the server; that the corrected tree is written; and that a subsequent
ordinary `setPaneUrl` also reaches the server, so the region really is writable again rather than
merely error-reported. The budget is compressed to 2000 ms through the production setter, for the
same reason `tst_sessionbootstrap` compresses `setConnectTimeoutMs`: 90 seconds of wall clock has no
place in the default suite.

With the `QTimer::singleShot` block removed (the setter kept so the file still compiles), the test
fails:

```
FAIL!  : aTerminalMintThatIsNeverAnsweredStopsBlockingTheRegion() Compared values are not the same
   Actual   (errorSpy.count()): 0
   Expected (1)               : 1
```

No error, no drop, no write — the region stays blocked, which is the bug.

## AS15 — A hand-edited server profile with a blank host or user loaded as a usable profile

**Files:** `src/app/ServerProfiles.cpp` lines 191–221; `src/app/ServerProfiles.h` lines 197–203;
test in `src/app/tests/tst_serverprofiles.cpp`.

**What the code did before.** Saved server profiles live in a settings file the user can edit by
hand. The save path's `sanitize()` refuses to store a profile missing a host or a user. The read
path did not check either field, so a hand-edited or truncated row with a blank host or user loaded
like any other profile and appeared in the list the user picks from.

**Why that is wrong.** The previous round declined this on the grounds that "dropping rows on read
is the opposite of what the merge contract promises". That objection does not survive inspection.
The merge contract exists so that this client does not lose *another writer's profiles* — and a row
with no host is not a profile. `SshConnectionPool::connectToHost(host, port, user)` needs all three
values, and since `sanitize()` already refuses to store such a row, it cannot have come from this
client. It is a server entry that can only fail at the moment the user selects it.

**What changed.** `ServerProfiles::readStoredProfiles()` now reads and trims `host` and `user` at the
top of the row loop, **immediately beside the existing port range check from `AS9`**, and skips a row
where either is blank; the trimmed values are what goes into the row map. This is deliberately the
one existing read-side normalisation site and not a second validation site: `sanitize()` still owns
the save side unchanged, and `readStoredProfiles()` is the only read path — both `load()` and
`mergeAndWrite()` go through it. Adding a second place that decides what a valid profile is would be
the duplicated rule that `AS9` was careful to avoid. The header contract was updated.

Skipped rather than repaired, unlike the port: the port is repairable *because* it has a documented
default, so substituting 22 for a missing value invents nothing. Host and user have no such answer.
As with the port repair, the skip is written back by the next save, so no unusable row is left in
the file for an older build to pick up.

**Test.** New: `TstServerProfiles::blankHostOrUserRowsAreNotProfilesAndAreDroppedOnLoad()`. It
hand-writes four rows in the documented on-disk layout — one usable, one with the `host` key
deleted, one with a whitespace-only host, one with a blank user — plus an active-server selection
pointing at an unusable one. It asserts that only the usable row loads; that the other three are
absent both from the profile list and from lookup by identifier; that the selection naming an
unusable row is dropped like any other dangling selection; and that after the next save the file's
`servers` group contains exactly one child group, so no orphan keys are left behind.

With the skip disabled, the test fails:

```
FAIL!  : blankHostOrUserRowsAreNotProfilesAndAreDroppedOnLoad() Compared lists have different sizes.
   Actual   (namesOf(store.profiles())) size: 4
   Expected size: 1
```

## AS16 — Six recursive tree walks looked unguarded, and nothing said why they are safe

**File:** `src/app/SessionLayouts.cpp` lines 32–39.

**Routed in from the models reviewer,** who reported six recursive walks over the split tree in this
file with no depth limit of their own.

**What was actually the case.** The analysis was correct as far as it went, and both ends were
verified here. `SplitNode::kMaxDepth` is 256 (`src/models/SplitTree.h:72`) and it is enforced by
`tryFromJson` on the way in and by `tryToJson` on the way out. Every `SplitNode` that reaches this
file has passed through one of them, and the one walk that runs over generated output,
`markLegacyLeaves`, only ever sees `tryToJson` output. So the walks cannot be driven into stack
exhaustion.

**What changed.** No guard was added: a second depth limit here would duplicate an invariant that
already holds, and duplication is how two copies of a rule get to disagree with each other. Instead
a comment at the head of the anonymous namespace where the walks live names `SplitNode::kMaxDepth`
and gives the argument, so the next reader does not have to re-derive it and does not add the
redundant guard.

**Test.** None. Comment only; no behaviour changed.

## AS17 — A comment described the editor as a pane type of its own, contradicting the viewer-pane model

**File:** `src/app/SessionBootstrap.cpp` lines 1351–1353.

**What the code did before.** A comment describing what a wedged daemon looks like to the user
called the symptom "an editor pane stuck on 'saving…'".

**Why that is wrong.** There is no editor pane. The application has viewer panes; a viewer pane may
be showing a text editor. Naming an "editor pane" invents a pane type that does not exist and
contradicts the framing every other description of the layout uses, which is the kind of drift that
leaves a reader unsure which model is the real one.

**What changed.** The comment now reads "a viewer pane whose text editor sits on 'saving…'". Comment
only; `tst_sessionbootstrap` was rebuilt and re-run afterwards, 45 passed.

# SSH — the SSH layer

## SSH1 — The 15-second connection timeout was switched off for anyone who has an SSH config file

**Files:** `src/ssh/SshConnectionPool.cpp` lines 588–645; documentation at
`src/ssh/SshConnectionPool.h` lines 130–137.

**What the code did before.** CodeHarbor opens SSH connections *synchronously on the user
interface thread* — the whole window is frozen until the connection succeeds or gives up. To bound
that freeze the code sets the SSH library's timeout option to 15 seconds. It did that **after**
parsing the user's personal OpenSSH configuration file, and it *skipped the setting entirely*
whenever that file existed at all. The stated reason: the file might carry the user's own connect
timeout, the library offers no way to ask whether one was read, and overwriting the user's choice
would be rude.

**Why that is wrong.** The mere *existence* of the file was treated as "the user configured a
timeout". Almost every developer has that file, for host aliases and key paths; almost none of them
set a connect timeout in it. For all of those users **no timeout was set at all**, and three things
followed.

First, the 15-second bound simply did not apply. Second, and confirmed by reading the installed
library's own source, when the timeout option is unset the library resolves its internal default to
*infinite* for channel writes — so writing to a remote peer that has stopped reading blocks
**forever** on the calling thread. The channel device's own comment claims the opposite ("each write
is bounded by the session timeout"), so the code's stated safety property was false for the common
case. Third, the workaround was unnecessary anyway: the library's configuration parser *does* set
the timeout option for a `ConnectTimeout` directive, so a value set beforehand is simply overwritten
by the user's.

Both claims were measured against the installed library rather than assumed. With the value pre-set
to 6 seconds and the config saying 2, the connect gave up after 2.00 seconds — the user's value
wins. With the value pre-set to 2 and the config silent, it gave up after 2.00 seconds — our value
survives. End to end through the real connection pool against a black-holed address: with a config
file that has no connect timeout, 15.02 seconds (the bound now applies; before the fix, none did);
with `ConnectTimeout 2`, 2.00 seconds.

**What changed.** The timeout is now set immediately after the host option and **before** the
configuration file is parsed, and the conditional and its flag were deleted. A user's own setting
now overrides ours because the parser runs later; everyone else keeps the bound. The class
documentation describes the new ordering and why it is correct.

**Test.** No automated test — reproducing it needs a black-holed network endpoint and a 15-second
wall-clock wait, which does not belong in a unit suite. Verified by the two measurement tables
above, produced by a standalone program driving the real connection pool.

## SSH2 — Remote terminals ran with the wrong terminal type, so everything was in 16 colours

**Files:** `src/ssh/SshConnectionPool.cpp` lines 1187–1197; `src/ssh/SshChannelDevice.cpp` lines
259–271; documentation at `src/ssh/SshConnectionPool.h` lines 374–382 and
`src/ssh/SshChannelDevice.h` lines 60–66.

**What the code did before.** Opening an SSH channel (one independent stream inside the single SSH
connection) went through the pool. If the caller labelled the channel as a terminal channel, the
pool immediately asked the server for a pseudo-terminal using the library's convenience function.
Afterwards the channel device noticed a terminal had already been negotiated and therefore only sent
a window-resize request, deliberately not a second terminal request — because the SSH protocol
allows exactly one such request per channel.

**Why that is wrong.** That convenience function is literally defined as "request a pseudo-terminal
of type `xterm`, 80 by 24". The terminal type is hard-coded. The application asks for
`xterm-256color`, and because the one allowed request had already been spent by the pool, that ask
was silently dropped — only the geometry could still be applied. Every remote program that reads the
terminal type to decide its colour capability — tmux, vim, coloured directory listings, anything
built on the ncurses library — therefore believed it was on a 16-colour terminal, while the terminal
emulator in the pane is fully 256-colour capable. There was no error, no warning, and no way for the
caller to find out.

**What changed.** Pseudo-terminal negotiation was removed from the pool entirely; the channel kind is
now purely a label. The channel device unconditionally issues the single pseudo-terminal request,
with the requested type and size, for every channel kind. The pool's channel-opening function has
exactly one caller in the whole repository, so this is a complete cutover with no compatibility
shims. Both header comments were rewritten to state the new contract.

**Test.** New: `TstLiveSsh::ptyChannelRunsWithTheRequestedTerminalType`. It opens a terminal channel
asking for `xterm-256color`, runs a tiny shell command on it that prints the terminal type, and
asserts the remote echoed `<xterm-256color>`. This lives in the live gate because only a real remote
shell can observe which terminal type was actually negotiated. **The coordinator confirmed this test
really executes and passes against the fixture server** — this was the specific case verified by
running the binary directly rather than trusting a summary line.

## SSH3 — On Windows the private-key fallback pointed at a filename that cannot exist, and never looked at the user's real keys

**File:** `src/ssh/SshConnectionPool.cpp` lines 394–414.

**What the code did before.** The routine that builds the list of private key files to try takes, in
order: the key path saved in the server profile; the key named in the user's SSH config, obtained by
asking the library; and only if *both* produced nothing, a scan of the user's `.ssh` directory for
the three standard key names.

**Why that is wrong.** The second step never produces nothing. The library seeds every new session's
identity list with its own defaults, stored in *template* form using placeholder tokens it expands
much later. Confirmed against the installed library: asking a fresh session returns the literal
string `%d/id_ed25519`, where `%d` stands for "the .ssh directory" and is not expanded yet. So the
list was never empty, which skipped the third step entirely, and the unopenable template path was
then handed to the library's key-import function, which fails.

This list has exactly one consumer: the fallback used when the machine is Windows *and* the SSH agent
socket points at Windows' named-pipe agent, which the library cannot talk to. That fallback is the
only thing standing between such a user and "cannot authenticate at all" — and it was operating on a
single unopenable filename, with the user's actual key never tried. Both the plain-key rung and the
passphrase-protected-key rung were dead on that platform whenever no key was explicitly configured.

**What changed.** A value returned by the library is now dropped if it still contains one of the
library's `%` escapes, with a comment explaining why. A real absolute path from the user's SSH config
is still accepted, and when only the library's unexpanded defaults are present the directory scan
runs as it was always meant to.

**Test.** No automated test: the routine is a file-local helper needing a live session object, and its
only consumer is compiled only on Windows. The library behaviour it depends on is pinned by the probe
output quoted above.

## SSH4 — A corrupted line in the known-hosts file was silently repaired and then trusted

**File:** `src/ssh/KnownHosts.cpp` lines 15–40.

**What the code did before.** OpenSSH can store host names in the known-hosts file in hashed form, so
the file does not reveal which machines you connect to. The stored token looks like
`|1|<encoded salt>|<encoded hash>`. To decide whether such a line refers to the host being connected
to, the code decoded the two parts and recomputed the hash. It used Qt's *lenient* base64 decoder.

**Why that is wrong.** Qt's lenient decoder **silently discards any character outside the base64
alphabet**. Confirmed directly: a clean token and the same token with a stray hyphen inserted decode
to the identical result under the lenient decoder, while the strict decoder refuses the dirty one. So
a known-hosts line whose hashed token had been corrupted — by a bad edit, a truncated sync, a mangling
text tool, or deliberately — still resolved to a real host name, and its host key was then treated as
the trusted key for that host. OpenSSH itself rejects such a line outright, so the two tools disagreed
about which lines in the user's file count. This is a security-relevant file, and every other base64
decode in the same file and in the host-key verification path already uses the strict decoder for
exactly this reason — so it was also one strict check with a lenient twin.

**What changed.** Both decodes now use Qt's strict decoder and the function reports "no match" if
either refuses. The line is still preserved verbatim when the file is written back; it is the user's
file.

**Test.** New: `TstKnownHosts::malformedHashedHostTokenNeverMatches`. It builds a genuine hashed
token, asserts the intact token matches so the fixture is sound, then inserts a stray character into
the hash, then into the salt, then tries outright garbage — asserting all three yield "unknown host"
rather than "match", and that each corrupt line still survives being written back out. Confirmed to
fail against the lenient decoder, where the first case reports "match".

## SSH5 — The read pump kept writing to its own memory after emitting signals that are allowed to destroy it

**File:** `src/ssh/SshChannelDevice.cpp` lines 417–449.

**What the code did before.** The timer-driven loop that pulls bytes off an SSH channel emits, in
order, a channel-error signal, then possibly a failure signal, then a data-ready signal, and finally a
stream-finished signal. Between and after those emissions it reads and writes its own member
variables.

**Why that is wrong.** Qt signals run their handlers synchronously, and any handler may destroy the
object that emitted the signal. That is not hypothetical here: the session bootstrap's unwire routine
runs from inside this device's own stream-finished signal and tears the device down — its comment says
so explicitly and it uses a deferred delete rather than an immediate one *specifically* to dodge this.
In other words the hazard is already known and is currently avoided only by every consumer remembering
to be careful. A consumer that used an immediate delete, or a future one wired to the data-ready
signal where the danger is far less obvious, would make this loop write to freed memory the moment the
handler returned. The JSON-RPC client already defends itself against exactly this pattern with a
`QPointer`; this device did not.

**What changed.** The loop now takes a `QPointer` to itself before the emission sequence and guards the
remaining steps on it. A `QPointer` is cleared automatically when its object is destroyed, so the
function stops touching itself the instant a handler deletes it. This matches the convention already
used in the JSON-RPC client.

**Test.** No automated test: constructing the failure needs a live SSH channel delivering bytes *and* a
consumer that deliberately misuses immediate deletion, i.e. a test that asserts against knowingly
wrong caller code. The change is provably behaviour-preserving when nobody deletes — the pointer is
non-null, so every branch is taken exactly as before.

## SSH6 — The authentication-method query got the right answer for its error case by accident

**Files:** `src/ssh/SshConnectionPool.cpp` lines 498–520; documentation at
`src/ssh/SshConnectionPool.h` lines 283–289.

**What the code did before.** After the handshake the client asks the server which authentication
methods it will accept. The library returns a bit mask, or the value −1 if the query itself failed. The
code cast the result to an unsigned integer and then compared against zero to detect "the server did
not tell us".

**Why that is wrong.** It is not wrong today, and that is the problem. Reinterpreting −1 as unsigned
yields a value with every bit set, which happens to make every method look "offered", which happens to
be the same answer the deliberate "we were not told, try everything" fallback gives. So the error case
lands on correct behaviour purely by a two's-complement coincidence, through a path nobody wrote on
purpose and no test covered. The day a method bit is added that this client must *not* assume is on
offer, the coincidence stops holding and the failure is silent.

**What changed.** The function now tests the **signed** value first and takes the documented fallback
explicitly, with a comment saying why the signed test is deliberate. The cast to unsigned moved below
that point, where the value is known to be a genuine bit mask. The header documentation previously
mentioned only the zero case and now covers both.

**Test.** The existing fallback test was extended with an assertion that the error value reports all
three methods available, plus a comment recording that this used to be an accident. A stale comment in
the same test was corrected.

## SSH7 — The known-hosts parser recompiled a regular expression once per line

**File:** `src/ssh/KnownHosts.cpp` lines 218–232.

The parser splits each line into fields on runs of whitespace, and constructed a fresh
regular-expression object **inside the per-line loop**. Constructing one compiles the pattern. A real
known-hosts file on a developer machine or a shared build box runs to hundreds or thousands of lines,
and the file is re-parsed on every connection attempt — pure wasted work of the kind that hides in a
profile. The pattern is now compiled once, as a function-level constant, with a comment; Qt documents
its regular-expression type as safe for concurrent read-only use. No test: a pure performance change
with no behaviour difference, and the existing 32 cases (including tab and carriage-return separators)
pin the splitting behaviour.

## SSH8 — The diagnostic transcript's size cap was untested for a single oversized line

**File:** `src/ssh/tests/tst_sshlogrouter.cpp` lines 129–156.

**What was missing.** The routine that appends to the retained diagnostic transcript caps it at 64
kilobytes and prepends a single "earlier diagnostics discarded" marker. The existing test drove it with
4000 small lines. Nothing covered the case where **one single line is itself larger than the whole
budget** — which a hostile or broken server can produce directly, because the SSH library quotes
server-supplied strings such as the identification banner into its log lines.

**Why that matters.** The cap exists so a misbehaving server cannot make CodeHarbor hold an unbounded
amount of attacker-chosen text in memory for the rest of the session. A plausible reimplementation —
"drop whole leading lines until the transcript fits" — satisfies the existing test perfectly and
retains a single huge line whole, defeating the cap entirely.

**What changed.** New test:
`TstSshLogRouter::oneOversizedLineIsTruncatedRatherThanRetainedWhole`. It appends one line of twice the
limit, bracketed by markers, and asserts the transcript is exactly the limit plus the discard marker,
starts with exactly one marker, ends with the tail marker, and no longer contains the head marker —
truncation drops from the front, so the newest text survives. It then appends an ordinary line and
asserts there is still exactly one marker.

## SSH9 — Three tests in this directory used the graphical entry point; the other two did not

**Files:** `src/ssh/tests/tst_knownhosts.cpp` line 689, `tst_sshauth.cpp` line 329,
`tst_sshlogrouter.cpp` line 579.

Qt offers two entry-point macros for tests: one that creates a graphical application object when the
target links Qt's GUI module, and one that never does. Two files in this directory used the
non-graphical one; the other three used the graphical one. None of these tests needs a graphical
environment — this whole module links only Qt's core and test libraries. Today the graphical macro
happens to expand to a plain non-graphical application, so the two spellings behave identically. But
the choice is made by a preprocessor symbol that appears the moment anything in the link line pulls in
Qt's GUI module; on that day the three would start demanding a display and fail on headless continuous
integration, while their two neighbours kept working. Two spellings for one intent, differing only by
accident. All three switched to the non-graphical macro.

## SSH10 — Two comments asserted that a state is unreachable, and it is reachable

**Files:** `src/ssh/SshConnectionPool.cpp` lines 527–534; `src/ssh/tests/tst_sshauth.cpp` lines 262–265.

**What the code did before.** The comment on the authentication-result classifier said that the
library's "the server has more challenge questions for you" result "is consumed by the
keyboard-interactive loop before this classifier ever sees a terminal result". The matching test
comment said "this client never requests that method".

**Why that is wrong.** Both were left behind by the change that added keyboard-interactive support. The
client *does* request that method now, and the challenge-response loop can exit with that result still
in hand in three real situations: the credential callback supplied no answer, the library rejected an
answer, or the 32-round safety cap was reached. In all three the value is handed straight to the
classifier. The handling is correct — it becomes "refused" — but a comment that asserts a state is
unreachable is exactly the kind of thing that gets someone to "simplify" the handling away.

**What changed.** Both comments were rewritten to describe the three ways that result really does
arrive and why each is a refusal.

## SSH11 — A channel label that no longer changed any behaviour was still threaded through thirteen files

**Files:** `src/ssh/SshConnectionPool.h` (the enumeration, formerly lines 153–160; the channel-opening
function, now line 409); `src/ssh/SshConnectionPool.cpp` line 1273; `src/ssh/SshChannelDevice.h`;
`src/ssh/SshChannelDevice.cpp` lines 17–18, 213 and 265–270. Outside this area, twenty construction
sites in thirteen files, listed below.

**What the code did before.** Every caller that opened an SSH channel had to say what kind of channel
it wanted, choosing from an enumeration on the connection pool. Before SSH2 that choice decided
whether the pool asked the server for a pseudo-terminal. After SSH2 moved that request into the
channel device, the value did nothing: the device stored it in a member, the member's only use was to
pass it back to the pool's channel-opening function, and that function's first statement marked the
argument unused.

**Why that is wrong.** The previous round recorded this as a pure label and left it alone, reasoning
that it is part of the class's public interface, that it documents caller intent, and that it is
useful in diagnostics. The last of those is not true, and the other two do not survive it. A search of
the whole repository found no `switch` on the enumeration, no log message that prints it, no test that
asserts on it, and nowhere that stores it; the pool never recorded it anywhere. The only occurrences
of the device's member were its declaration, its initialisation and the single call that handed it
back to a function that ignored it. Every other occurrence in the tree — twenty of them — was an
argument being supplied at a construction site. A parameter that no code path can read does not
document intent; it invites the next reader to believe the choice matters.

**What changed.** The enumeration, its Qt registration, the device's member and the parameter were all
deleted. The channel-opening function is now `openChannel()` and the device's constructor is
`SshChannelDevice(SshConnectionPool*, QObject* = nullptr)`. Two comments that described behaviour "for
every channel kind" were reworded. The removal rippled outward: the argument was dropped at all twenty
construction sites in thirteen files outside this area — `src/app/SessionBootstrap.{h,cpp}` (where the
kind was also a parameter of `openChannelDevice`, now `openChannelDevice(command, role)`),
`src/app/tests/{tst_sessionbootstrap,tst_coldstart,tst_livereconnect,tst_liveshell}.cpp`,
`src/terminal/TerminalFactory.cpp`,
`src/terminal/tests/{tst_liveterminal,tst_liveterminalfactory}.cpp`,
`src/agent/tests/tst_liveagent.cpp`,
`src/editor/tests/{tst_liveeditor,tst_liveeditorreconnect}.cpp`,
`src/viewers/tests/tst_liveviewers.cpp` and this area's own two test files. Two further references were
handed to the agents who own those files rather than edited here — one in `docs/PLAN.md` and one in
`src/terminal/TerminalController.h` — and both owners confirmed the change applied. A final repository-wide
search for the enumeration's name returns nothing.

**Test.** None, and none is possible: this is a deletion with no behaviour attached, so there is no
pre-fix failure to reproduce and no test was invented to imply one. The proof obligation is that
nothing depended on it, which a compiler discharges — the name no longer exists, so any surviving use
is a build error. The five test targets in this area were built out of tree, and each of the thirteen
out-of-area files was compiled with its real build flags; all succeeded.

## SSH12 — Destroying a connection pool told the user interface its state had changed, from inside the destructor

**Files:** `src/ssh/SshConnectionPool.cpp` lines 266–284; `src/ssh/SshConnectionPool.h` line 455 and the
comment at the `sessionClosing()` declaration.

**What the code did before.** The pool's destructor called the ordinary `disconnectFromHost()` routine.
That routine is written for a live object: it closes the SSH session and then routes the state change
through the pool's normal state setter, which emits `stateChanged()` and, because the diagnostic
transcript is cleared on the way, `diagnosticLogChanged()`. It also emits `sessionClosing()`. All three
therefore ran while the object was being destroyed.

**Why that is wrong.** The previous round called this a genuine hazard and left it alone, on the grounds
that one of the emissions is load-bearing and that fixing it properly would mean restructuring teardown
ownership across another area. The second half is what turned out to be avoidable; the first half is
correct and the fix keeps it. The three signals are not alike:

* `stateChanged()` and `diagnosticLogChanged()` are notifications for Qt properties, which means a
  handler can be a QML property binding that reads the pool back. Making a binding re-read an object
  that is halfway through being destroyed buys nothing at all — the value it would fetch describes a
  pool that will not exist a moment later.
* `sessionClosing()` is different. The channel device's constructor connects it to the device's own
  channel-closing slot, and it is the only mechanism that makes a device which outlives its pool drop
  its channel handle before the pool frees the underlying session. Remove it and those handles are used
  after the memory behind them is gone.

**What changed.** The destructor no longer calls `disconnectFromHost()`. It sets a new `m_destroying`
flag, closes the session directly, and then corrects its recorded state by assigning the member rather
than going through the state setter — so neither property notification is emitted during teardown.
`sessionClosing()` is kept, and was made safe to emit rather than removed. Three properties, written
into the header next to the declaration, are what make it safe: it is emitted from the body of the
most-derived class's destructor, so every member and the `QObject` base are still alive and the object
still behaves as a pool; the session-closing routine detaches the session handle before emitting, so a
handler that somehow re-entered teardown would find nothing left to close; and the new `m_destroying`
flag makes the three re-entrant routes a handler could take refuse outright — connecting to a host
(line 607), disconnecting (line 840) and opening a channel (line 1275) all return immediately while the
pool is being destroyed. Without that last part a handler could allocate a session that nothing would
ever free, or reinstate the very `stateChanged()` emission this finding removes. The flag is declared
outside the conditional compilation guard so it exists in builds made without the SSH library too.

**Test.** New file and new build target, `src/ssh/tests/tst_sshconnectionpool.cpp`, test
`destroyingAPoolAnnouncesTheSessionButChangesNoProperty`. It first drives a handshake to `127.0.0.1`
port 1 with an empty home directory — refused immediately by the local networking stack, with no server
involved — which leaves the pool in its error state. That matters: a pool destroyed while already
disconnected has no state change to make, so it would emit nothing either way and prove nothing. The
test then watches all three signals across the pool's destruction and requires none of them to fire.
Reverting only the destructor body to its previous `disconnectFromHost()` call makes it fail, reporting
one state change where zero are expected.

The offline test can only cover the half that used to be wrong on every teardown. `sessionClosing()`
fires only when there is a live session to close, which needs a real server; that specific teardown
path was not covered in the final pass because the live-gate prerequisites were unavailable.

## SSH13 — Releasing a logging route from the wrong thread was caught only by an assertion, which release builds remove

**Files:** `src/ssh/SshConnectionPool.h` lines 94–127 and 153–159; `src/ssh/SshConnectionPool.cpp`
lines 124–156 and 178–197.

**What the code did before.** Diagnostic lines from the SSH library are steered to whichever component
asked for them by a small router. A component takes a route, the route is pushed onto a per-thread
stack, and lines emitted on that thread go to the innermost route's sink. The route object's destructor
is the normal way to release it. Releasing checked that the route was on the current thread's stack with
an assertion; in a release build assertions are compiled out, so the check disappeared and the code fell
through to decrementing a process-wide counter and returning. The route object then finished being
destroyed while a raw pointer to it stayed on the other thread's stack.

**Why that is wrong.** The previous round left this alone, reasoning that there is no way to repair the
situation from the wrong thread because the owning thread's storage is unreachable. That reasoning is
half right, and the wrong half is the part that mattered. It is true that the owning thread's saved
library verbosity setting cannot be restored from another thread; that is left as it was, and the
threading contract now says so, because a stale verbosity level is a diagnostic annoyance and not a
safety problem. But the dangling pointer is a memory-safety bug, and it is entirely repairable: the
route's sink kept receiving lines through freed memory on the owning thread, which is the hazard the
assertion was supposed to prevent and which release builds shipped anyway.

**What changed.** Two changes. First, the thread stack no longer holds a raw pointer to the route. The
route owns a shared pointer to a small entry holding the sink and an atomic "active" flag, and the stack
holds a copy of that same shared pointer, so the entry outlives the route and a route destroyed on the
wrong thread cannot leave a pointer into freed memory. The route also records the identity of the thread
that created it. Because the entry type is private to the route, the stack moved from a file-scope
variable into a private per-thread member of the router, alongside a private routine that sweeps out
inactive entries. Second, the assertion became a check that runs in every build. The entry is
deactivated first, so routing to it stops no matter which thread ends the route; then, if the caller is
not the owning thread, the router issues a warning and returns without touching either thread's state.
Dispatch walks the stack backwards to the innermost entry that is still active, so a wrongly released
route is skipped, and the owning thread sweeps the inert entry away the next time it takes or releases a
route. The cost is one shared pointer per route and one atomic load per line dispatched.

**Test.** `src/ssh/tests/tst_sshlogrouter.cpp`,
`releasingARouteFromTheWrongThreadStopsItRoutingAnyway`. A worker thread takes an outer and an inner
route and emits a real library log line; the main thread then commits the violation by releasing the
inner route; the worker emits two more lines. The test requires the warning to be issued, the inner
sink to have seen exactly the one line emitted while it was genuinely live, both later lines to have
gone to the outer route, and the process-wide route count to have decremented exactly once — no second
decrement when the owning thread later drops the same route. Reverted to the previous semantics, where
a wrong-thread release changes nothing but the counter, the test fails with all three lines delivered to
the released route's sink, which is precisely the read through a dead pointer that the fix removes.

## SSH14 — A channel that had reached the end of its remote stream still read as "no data yet"

**Files:** `src/ssh/SshChannelDevice.cpp` lines 80–96, plus the extracted helper at line 449 and lines
179 and 326; `src/ssh/SshChannelDevice.h` lines 95–101.

**What the code did before.** The channel device is a Qt input/output device. When a read was requested
and nothing was buffered, it returned 0 — "nothing available right now" — regardless of whether the
remote side had finished sending. Qt's contract for this kind of device is that 0 means "try again
later" and −1 means "there will never be any more".

**Why that is wrong.** The previous round left this alone on the grounds that Qt permits either answer
for a sequential device and that consumers key on the stream-finished signal instead. Permitting either
answer is not the same as either answer being right: with 0 returned forever, a caller written to Qt's
own contract — read until it returns a non-positive value that distinguishes end from emptiness — cannot
tell a finished stream from an idle one, and requiring every consumer to subscribe to a signal instead
is a private convention layered on top of a standard interface.

**What changed.** A read with an empty buffer now returns −1 when the remote stream has finished and 0
when the channel is still live. Bytes that are already buffered are still handed over first, exactly as
before.

Changing a return value from 0 to −1 is only safe if no caller reads meaning into the difference, so
every in-tree consumer of this device as a Qt input/output device was audited first, and the audit found
none. Every one of them uses the read-everything and bytes-available calls rather than the raw read:
`src/remote/CodeharbordClient.cpp` (lines 203, 331, 579, 723), `src/agent/AgentStatusMonitor.cpp` (lines
62, 71), `src/terminal/TerminalController.cpp` (lines 138, 154), `src/app/SessionBootstrap.cpp` (line
862), and the live tests. Qt's read-everything call stops on a non-positive result and keeps whatever it
has already collected, so buffered bytes cannot be lost by the new −1; when the buffer is empty it
yields an empty result, which is what those call sites already handle. The terminal controller
additionally checks that the device is readable, which is unaffected because the device deliberately
stays open after the end of the stream.

Supporting change: the block that announces the end of the read channel exactly once was duplicated in
three places and is now one protected helper, called from the read pump and from the channel-closing
routine in both build configurations. The destructor still latches the flag directly, because it must
record the end without emitting anything.

**Test.** `src/ssh/tests/tst_sshchanneldevice.cpp`, `aFinishedStreamReadsAsMinusOneNotZero`, using a
small subclass that opens the device and exposes the end-of-stream helper — the one state a test without
a server cannot otherwise reach, since only a real channel's end of stream sets it. The helper is
protected rather than test-only because the higher layers already fake channels by subclassing this
class the same way. The test asserts that a read is 0 while the stream is live; that after the stream
finishes the signal fires once, the device is still open, a read is −1, reading everything yields
nothing and the device reports itself at its end; and that finishing a second time neither re-announces
nor changes the read answer. Reverting the single return to 0 makes it fail.

## SSH15 — Refusing an unknown host key left the user with a failed connection and no reason for it

**Files:** `src/ssh/SshConnectionPool.cpp` lines 363–410 and line 841; `src/ssh/SshConnectionPool.h`
lines 389–397.

**What the code did before.** When a server presents a host key that is not in the known-hosts file, the
pool asks the application, which asks the user. If the user declines, the pool abandoned the connection
attempt with a state change and a line in the diagnostic transcript, and no error message.

**Why that is wrong.** The previous round judged this intentional, reasoning that the user had just
pressed "reject" in a dialog that told them exactly what they were rejecting. The prompt and the failure
are not the same moment. The pool abandons the attempt and the controller then retries the whole
connect, following the same non-blocking pattern the passphrase and password steps use, so the state
change the user eventually sees is not adjacent to the dialog they answered. Every other outcome on this
code path reports itself — a changed key, an unreadable key, an unknown key with nobody available to ask
— and the last of those was added in an earlier round for exactly this reason: the caller and the user
saw a change to the error state and no explanation anywhere but the diagnostic transcript. The declined
case was the one remaining hole in that argument.

**What changed.** Two parts. The known-hosts policy was split out of the host-key verification callback
into a separate routine taking the host, port, key type and key bytes, declared in the header and
compiled in both build configurations because it needs nothing from the SSH library. Verification now
does nothing but pull the key off the session and delegate; this is also what makes the behaviour
testable at all, since the callback itself can only be reached from inside a completed handshake. Then
the "user declined" branch emits an error naming the host and the key type, like every other refusal on
the path already did.

**Test.** In the new `src/ssh/tests/tst_sshconnectionpool.cpp`,
`aDeclinedUnknownKeyIsRefusedWithAnExplanation` requires exactly one error, whose text names both the
host and the key type, and requires the key not to be left trusted. Reverting just the new emission
makes it fail with no errors where one is expected. The split also allowed the rest of the policy, which
had no coverage without a server before, to be tested: a trusted key accepted silently, a changed key
refused and reported, an unknown key with nobody to ask refused and reported, an accepted unknown key
trusted and written to the file, and a non-default port looked up and stored in OpenSSH's bracketed
form.

## SSH16 — A partial success from the no-op authentication method was discarded, so a later failure gave the wrong advice

**File:** `src/ssh/SshConnectionPool.cpp` lines 947–979.

**What the code did before.** Authentication starts with the SSH protocol's `none` method — a request
that asks the server what it will accept and occasionally succeeds outright. The result of that request
was compared against two raw library constants and otherwise dropped. A server answering "partial
success" — meaning `none` counted as one factor and more are required — fell through as if it had
refused.

**Why that is wrong.** The pool keeps a list of the methods that have already partially succeeded, and
that list is the switch deciding which of two completely different explanations the final failure
message gives. With `none` never recorded, a server that accepted it as a first factor and then ran the
client out of remaining methods produced the explanation about ssh-agent and identity files — advice
about a key exchange that never took place.

**What changed.** The `none` result is now classified through the same routine every other
authentication step uses. A transport error is still checked first, because that is a dead connection
rather than a refusal; a grant returns; a partial success appends `none` to the recorded list and writes
a diagnostic line; a refusal falls through to the remaining methods exactly as before.

**Test.** None at this seam, and this is a deliberate statement rather than an omission: the behaviour
is fixed but cannot be tested where it lives. Reaching the line needs a server that answers "partial
success" to a `none` request, meaning one configured with `none` as a genuine first authentication
factor. OpenSSH cannot express that — its authentication-methods setting has no token for `none` — so
even the live gate against a real server cannot produce it; it would need a purpose-built SSH server
written to stand in for sshd. The recording itself is a private member update inside the authentication
routine, which only runs in the middle of a handshake, so there is no offline seam short of that server.
Turning the failure-message routine into a standalone function so that the *consequence* could be driven
directly was considered and rejected: the test would hand that function the very list the fix produces,
so it would prove nothing about the fix, and it would trade a cohesive private method for a long
parameter list. What is covered is the classifier the fix now routes through:
`tst_sshauth::partialIsClassifiedApartFromSuccessAndFailure` pins the library's partial-success constant
to the partial outcome.

### Verified in this area and found correct

Recorded so the check is not repeated. This area was searched for any dependence on the viewer pane's
framing — handlers treated as pane types, an "editor pane", or `http`/`https` routing — and there is
none. The word "handler" occurs three times in this area and every one of them means a Qt slot.

### The library version constraint

The standing project rule is that libssh is pinned to 0.12.2, with a workaround that applies only to
0.12.0. The version-conditional code matches that intent: it compares only the leading release token of
the reported version for exact equality with `0.12.0`, so 0.12.1, 0.12.2, 0.12.10 and every 0.11.x are
excluded, and the mitigation is applied on that one release only. An existing test already pins every
one of those cases including the `0.12.10` prefix trap. No change needed.

# RPC — the JSON-RPC client

## RPC1 — A reply that also carried a method name was accepted as a successful answer

**File:** `src/remote/CodeharbordClient.cpp`, the line-processing routine; fix at lines 463–481.
Contract text it violated: `src/remote/CodeharbordClient.h` lines 177–183.

**What the code did before.** Every line the server sends is one JSON object. The client decides what
to do with it by looking at two members: a `method` name, and an `id` — the number the client stamped
on the request it is waiting for. The rules of JSON-RPC are: a message with a method and *no* id is a
one-way notification (for example "a watched file changed") and is emitted as such; a message with an
id and no method is a response and is routed back to the callback of the request that minted that id.

Notifications were handled correctly. But a malformed message carrying *both* a method and a valid id
fell through into the response path, was looked up in the pending table, and then only its result and
error members were inspected. If such a message also carried a result, the client handed that result
to the waiting caller as a normal success, with no warning at all.

The class's own documentation promised the opposite, in as many words: a message carrying a method and
an id "is never dispatched here: it is routed on its id like any response, which fails the matching
caller … and always emits a protocol warning".

**Why that is wrong.** A message carrying a method name is by definition not a reply — it is either a
request aimed at the client, which this client does not implement at all, or a corrupted response.
Either way its result is not an answer to the request that happens to share the id. A buggy or hostile
server emitting an id-bearing message with a method name and a result would resolve that pending
request with a payload that was never a reply to it. In this application such payloads flow straight
into the editor's read and write handling, so a caller would act on file content or a revision token it
never asked for, with nothing in the log. The existing test only covered the case where the malformed
message had *neither* a result nor an error, which landed in a different branch that happened to fail
the caller — so the hole was invisible.

**What changed.** Immediately after the pending entry is looked up and removed, and before any result or
error is inspected, the code now checks for the presence of a method member. If one is there it emits a
protocol warning naming the offending response and fails the caller's callback once with the standard
internal-error code and the message "malformed response: carries a method member". The result is never
delivered.

**Test.** New: `TstRpcClient::responseCarryingMethodIsNeverAResult`. It issues a real request, then feeds
a reply with the matching id plus both a method and a result, and asserts the callback fired with an
error rather than a success, the delivered value is null rather than the bogus payload, no notification
was emitted, at least one protocol warning was emitted, and the pending count dropped to zero.

## RPC2 — An error code that was numeric but not a whole 32-bit integer was silently rewritten to zero

**File:** `src/remote/CodeharbordClient.cpp` lines 500–520.

**What the code did before.** When the server answers with a failure it sends an error object with a
numeric code and a text message. The client validated only that the code was *a JSON number at all*,
warning if it was not, and then converted it with Qt's integer accessor.

That accessor does not clamp or truncate: if the JSON number is fractional or outside the range of a
32-bit integer, it silently returns its default value, which here is zero. So an error code of one
trillion reached the caller as code zero with no warning, because one trillion is a perfectly good JSON
number and passed the "is it a number" check.

**Why that is wrong.** Zero is indistinguishable from a server that genuinely sent zero, and zero is also
what the client's own "malformed error object, best effort" path produces. So a code the client could not
represent was laundered into one that looks deliberate. Callers in this codebase branch on the exact
number — the editor compares against the revision-mismatch code −32001 to decide whether a save collided
with someone else's edit — so a mangled code takes the wrong branch with nothing in the log to explain
it. The neighbouring id decoding already guards against exactly this hazard using a two-default probe;
the error code did not.

**What changed.** The validity check now probes the code with two different defaults, which disagree only
when the number is fractional or out of range. That condition joins the existing checks, so such a
response emits the existing "error object missing code or message" warning. The best-effort behaviour is
unchanged — the code still ends up zero and the callback is still failed, so no caller can hang — but the
loss is now reported instead of silent. A comment explains the two-default technique, mirroring the
wording already used for the id.

**Test.** New: `TstRpcClient::errorCodeOutOfIntRangeWarns`. It feeds an error response with a code of one
trillion and asserts the callback is failed, the message survives intact, the code is the best-effort
zero, and exactly one protocol warning was emitted. Without the fix the warning count is zero.

## RPC3 — Two places could use the client after a warning handler destroyed it

**File:** `src/remote/CodeharbordClient.cpp` lines 284–300 and 725–732.

**What the code did before.** The protocol-warning signal is public. Qt emits signals synchronously, and
a handler is free to do anything — including delete the client. The suite's own
`callbackDeletingClientMidDispatchIsSafe` proves consumers really do delete this object from inside its
own dispatch.

Two places emitted a protocol warning and then called a member function on themselves. The first is the
send path: when a write to the transport succeeds only *partially*, a truncated frame is on the wire and
the byte stream is unrecoverable, so the code emitted the warning and then called the teardown routine.
The second is the heartbeat tick: when the peer has been silent for the configured number of intervals,
the code emitted a "treating transport as dead" warning and then called the same teardown routine.

**Why that is wrong.** It is a latent use-after-free, reachable the moment anyone wires the
protocol-warning signal to logic that tears the session down — which is a completely natural thing to
connect a protocol-error signal to, and these two are precisely the "the connection is dead" cases a
consumer would want to react to. Nothing in the shipped code connects to that signal outside tests today,
so it is a trap left armed rather than an active bug. The rest of this file already defends against
exactly this: the read path takes a `QPointer` to itself before its own warning emission and bails if it
goes null. These two were the outliers.

**What changed.** Both sites now follow the file's existing pattern, taking a `QPointer` before the
emission and checking it before the teardown. Everything after that point is a local variable, so the
failure callback is still invoked exactly once and the request id is still returned even if the client is
gone. No new include was needed.

**Test.** None added. Driving this needs a warning handler that deletes the client from inside a
short-write or heartbeat-death path; the fixture would then have to survive its own client vanishing
mid-call, and the assertion would be "no memory-error report", which this suite cannot express
deterministically. The change is a strict narrowing of an unguarded window to the same pattern already
used three lines away.

## RPC4 — Reconnecting from inside a failure sweep announced the connection twice and drained the wrong stream

**File:** `src/remote/CodeharbordClient.cpp` lines 193–219.

**What the code did before.** Rebinding the client onto a new byte stream must fail every request still in
flight, because the request identifiers it minted mean nothing to a different server process. The order
was: rewire the signal hooks, fail all pending callbacks, read whatever the new stream already had
buffered, then emit the "transport bound" signal that tells consumers the connection is up again and they
should re-register their server-side state.

Those failure callbacks run *synchronously, inside* the rebind function, and the file's own comments
acknowledge that the natural reaction to a transport error is to reconnect — that is, to call the rebind
function again. When one did, the nested call performed a complete bind of its own: rewire, sweep, drain,
and announce. Control then returned to the *outer* call, which carried on using the client's current
stream — now the one the callback bound, not the one the outer call was asked to bind. It drained that
stream a second time and announced a second time.

The drain had a related gap: a queued end-of-stream, or an unframed line past the 16-megabyte cap, can
close the connection *during* the drain. The outer call did not re-check, so it would announce "the
transport is up and usable" immediately after the closed signal had already been emitted for it.

**Why that is wrong.** The "transport bound" signal is documented as the hook where consumers re-establish
server-side state, and the specific case that forced it into existence is file watching: the daemon keeps
subscriptions in a per-process table, so every bind makes the editor re-issue its watch. Announcing twice
for one bind means duplicate subscriptions on the server — the second watch identifier is the one the
client keeps, so the first is leaked for the life of the connection, delivering events nobody will ever
cancel — plus duplicate re-read work. Announcing after a close advertises a usable transport that is
already dead, so the consumer's first request fails immediately and it has no idea why. Both are reachable
from a plausible and explicitly anticipated consumer behaviour.

**What changed.** After the pending sweep, the rebind now checks whether the client's stream is still the
one it was asked to bind; if a swept callback rebound or detached, that nested call has already done the
drain and the announcement for whatever it bound, so the outer call stops rather than acting on a stream it
no longer holds. After the drain it re-checks both that the connection is not closed and that the stream
has not changed, so a drain that ended the connection, or a callback dispatched by the drain that rebound,
suppresses the now-false announcement. Both checks carry comments explaining the scenario, and the
pre-existing "no stream" early exit was folded into the first check.

**Test.** New: `TstRpcClient::rebindFromASweptCallbackAnnouncesBoundOnce`. Three scripted streams: the
client is bound to the first with one request in flight whose failure callback binds the third. The test
then binds the second. It asserts the callback ran exactly once, the surviving stream is the third (the
callback's bind is the one that stands), and the bound signal fired **exactly once** — then round-trips a
real request on the third stream to prove it works. Against the pre-fix source the signal count is 2.

## RPC5 — The read loop's re-entrancy safety rested on a comment, and the reason given for keeping it was measurably false

**Files:** `src/remote/CodeharbordClient.h` lines 255–288, `src/remote/CodeharbordClient.cpp` lines
355–404 and 434–442, plus the buffer-reset points at lines 130–132, 391–394, 422–424 and 631–633.

**The previous round's reasoning was wrong, and correcting it is the point of this entry.** The earlier
report recorded this as "the read buffer is compacted once per frame, which is quadratic in frames per
read", and then argued the obvious fix must not be applied because of a re-entrancy trap. The verdict —
leave the loop working the way it works — was defensible. The stated reason was not: the loop was never
quadratic.

**What the code did before.** The client reads bytes from the transport into one growing buffer and cuts
them into frames at newline characters. After each frame was dispatched, the loop removed that frame's
bytes from the front of the buffer, so the next search for a newline always started at offset zero.

**Why that is wrong.** Removing bytes from the front of an array *sounds* like it must copy everything
behind them, which would make N frames in one read cost time proportional to N squared. That is the claim
the earlier report made. It was measured this round and it does not hold on Qt 6: the container behind
`QByteArray` has a front-erase fast path that only advances its data pointer, and `QByteArray::left()`
deep-copies rather than sharing a reference, so nothing forced a copy of the remainder either. A
standalone benchmark of the two disciplines with no JSON parsing in the way (60-byte frames, optimised
build) showed both are linear — quadrupling the frame count costs about 3.2 times the time on the old
code — and the new discipline is roughly 2.4 times faster on the buffer work alone: at 80 000 frames,
three runs each, 28/29/29 ms before against 12/15/10 ms after. That is a constant factor, not a change
of complexity.

Nor is it a visible speedup in the running program. Timing the delivery of a 20 000-frame chunk end to
end, six runs each on a loaded machine, gave 91/100/91/89/119/115 ms before against 88/90/140/118/139/124
ms after — minima 89 and 88, medians inside each other's noise. **No speed improvement is being claimed.**

The same conclusion was reached independently for the copy of this loop in `AgentStatusMonitor`, by
probing the buffer's data pointer across a front removal on the same Qt version.

What *was* real is the hazard the earlier report identified: a response callback may spin a nested event
loop, which re-enters the reader while the outer loop still has unconsumed frames. That hazard was held
in check only by the shape of the code and a paragraph of prose in a report, which the next person to
edit this function has no reason to have read.

**What changed.** The loop now tracks where it is with two members rather than by shrinking the buffer:
`m_readPos`, the first unconsumed byte, and `m_scanOffset`, how far past it the search for a newline has
already looked. The buffer is compacted once, at the end of the read, by a new `compactReadBuffer()`.
Three properties make that safe against re-entry, and each is now stated in the header next to the
members:

* The position lives in members, so a nested read advances the very cursor the outer loop re-reads on its
  next iteration. No frame is dispatched twice, whichever nesting level reaches it.
* No local variable holds an index across a dispatch. The newline position is recomputed at the top of
  every iteration, and `m_readPos` is advanced *before* the frame is handed to the caller.
* The frame handed out is a deep copy, not a slice sharing the buffer's memory. A shared slice would be a
  live reference into a buffer that re-entrant code is free to append to and reallocate.

The cursor is reset everywhere the buffer is dropped: when a new transport is bound, when a close is
latched mid-chunk, when an oversize line is discarded, and when the transport closes. The
16-megabyte unframed-line cap is now checked *after* compaction, so it measures the genuine unframed
remainder rather than a buffer that still physically contains frames already delivered; behaviour is
unchanged, because under the old per-frame removal the delivered bytes were gone by then too.

The comments in the header and the source were rewritten to record the measurement instead of repeating
the quadratic claim.

So the change was kept on correctness grounds, not for speed: it is not measurably worse, and the
re-entrancy hazard is now pinned by a test rather than by a paragraph in a report.

**Test.** Two new tests, and the evidence around them is unusual enough to state precisely.

* `TstRpcClient::reentrantFeedFromACallbackDispatchesEveryFrameOnce` — one read delivers three responses;
  the first response's callback issues a fourth request and delivers its answer synchronously, so a
  nested read runs while the outer loop still has two frames of its own chunk unconsumed, against a
  buffer the nested read has just appended to. It asserts four callbacks, each label exactly once, zero
  protocol warnings, and a pending count of zero.
* `TstRpcClient::aBurstOfFramesInOneReadIsRoutedOnce` — 20 000 frames in one read, each routed once, no
  warnings; then a frame split across two deliveries, to show the deferred compaction leaves a partial
  frame held on its own rather than spliced onto a megabyte of already-dispatched bytes. It carries no
  wall-clock assertion on purpose: as the numbers above show, a timing bound the old code also passes is
  a flake, not a test.

**Neither test fails against the pre-fix source**, because the pre-fix source was correct — that is the
whole point of this entry. What they were checked against instead is the *wrong* fix the earlier report
warned about, built for exactly this purpose: the new source with the cursor demoted from members to
locals and one compaction at the end. Against that version the re-entrancy test fails with one protocol
warning where zero are expected (the nested reader re-dispatches an already-consumed frame, whose id is
no longer in the pending table, producing an "unknown id" warning), while the burst test passes. So the
first test pins the hazard, and the second pins the framing; neither is evidence of a pre-existing
failure, and none is claimed.

## RPC6 — The public pending-request count narrowed a 64-bit size to a 32-bit integer

**Files:** `src/remote/CodeharbordClient.h` lines 118–126, `src/remote/CodeharbordClient.cpp` lines 677
and 690.

**What the code did before.** `pendingCount()` returned `int`, produced by an explicit cast from the size
of the hash table of in-flight requests, which is a 64-bit `qsizetype`.

**Why that is wrong.** The earlier round left this alone on two grounds: two billion in-flight requests
would exhaust memory long first, and widening the return type is a public interface change that ripples
into consumers. The first is true; the second turned out to be empty. A search of the whole tree finds
exactly two callers, both tests — `src/remote/tests/tst_rpcclient.cpp` and
`src/app/tests/tst_livereconnect.cpp` line 296 — and both compare against integer literals, which compile
unchanged against the wider type through Qt's generic comparison template. There is no production caller.
So the change costs nothing, and an explicit cast that quietly discards the high half of a size is exactly
the kind of thing a later reader has to re-derive as safe.

**What changed.** `pendingCount()` returns `qsizetype`, and the internal `outstanding` variable is a
`qsizetype` rather than a cast to `int`. The header comment records that the value comes from a hash
size and is therefore 64-bit.

**Test.** New: `TstRpcClient::pendingCountIsNotNarrowedToInt`. It is a compile-time assertion that the
return type is `qsizetype`, plus a runtime round trip through the accessor. Two billion pending entries
cannot be built in a test, so the contract is pinned where it actually lives, in the type. Compiling the
new test file against the pre-fix client fails with

```
error: static assertion failed: pendingCount() must not narrow QHash::size() to int
```

which is a build failure, not a runtime one — the honest form of evidence for a type-level contract. The
17 existing comparisons in the same file build and pass unchanged.

## RPC7 — Re-arming the heartbeat reset the silence measurement, so a silent peer could go undetected forever

**Files:** `src/remote/CodeharbordClient.cpp` lines 29–36, 711–722 and 756–766;
`src/remote/CodeharbordClient.h` lines 177–184 and 342–347.

**What the code did before.** `enableHeartbeat(interval, tolerance)` sends a periodic ping and declares the
transport dead once the peer has been silent for `tolerance` intervals. Calling it again while it was
already running retired the probe currently in flight — leaving it in the pending table and in the set of
probe ids — zeroed the count of missed intervals, and restarted the timer from zero.

**Why that is wrong.** The earlier round recorded this as unbounded growth of two internal collections and
left it alone, reasoning that a retired probe is still physically on the wire so it must stay in both
collections, and that production enables the heartbeat exactly once. The first half of that reasoning is
correct and was not touched. The rest understated the problem in two ways.

First, this is not only a slow leak. Because re-arming also zeroed the miss counter and restarted the
timer, a consumer that re-enabled the heartbeat more often than the interval reset the silence measurement
every time. The heartbeat was on, and would never declare anything dead — silent-peer detection, the
entire reason the feature exists, was disabled outright. That is an active bug, not a trap.

Second, nothing bounded how many retired probes could accumulate. Each re-arm against a peer that answers
nothing stranded one more entry in the pending table and one more in the probe set, reclaimable only by
losing the transport.

**What changed.** Two independent fixes.

* Re-enabling the configuration that is already running is now a true no-op: if the timer is active and
  both the interval and the tolerance equal the running ones, `enableHeartbeat()` returns without
  re-arming. A genuine configuration *change* still falls through and re-arms, because the old cadence has
  to be replaced.
* The number of outstanding probes is capped at four. The heartbeat tick now treats "a probe is already
  outstanding **or** four probe ids are already unanswered" as "a probe is on the wire", and counts the
  interval as a miss instead of minting a fifth id.

The cap does not weaken detection. Four unanswered probes is already conclusive evidence of silence, the
interval still counts as a miss, and any byte arriving from the peer still zeroes the miss counter through
the read path, so a slow-but-live peer cannot be killed. Detection gets faster, not slower.

An existing test, `retiredProbeAnswerDoesNotClobberTheLiveProbe`, drove retirement by re-enabling with an
identical configuration, which is now the no-op. It was changed to re-arm with a genuinely different
configuration, which exercises the same "a retired probe answers late" scenario it was written for; its
comment now says why.

**Test.** Three new tests, all of which fail against the pre-fix source:

* `reEnablingTheRunningHeartbeatIsANoOp` — eight re-enables of the running configuration issue no probe
  at all, and the original probe is still the live one, shown by answering it and seeing the next one
  released. Pre-fix: one probe issued where zero are expected.
* `repeatedlyReEnablingCannotSuppressSilenceDetection` — the purpose case. Interval 20 ms, tolerance 3,
  re-enabled every 10 ms for 600 ms against a peer that answers nothing: the transport must still be
  declared dead exactly once and the pending caller failed. Pre-fix: the closed signal never fires.
* `reArmingAgainstASilentPeerCannotPileUpProbes` — twelve real re-arms (a different tolerance each time)
  against a silent peer strand at most four probes, and the public pending count still reports zero
  caller requests. Pre-fix: twelve re-arms leave twelve probes stranded in flight.

## RPC8 — The daemon's new resource-limit error code had no counterpart in the C++ header

**Files:** `src/remote/RpcTypes.h` lines 33–46, `remote/test/rpc-mirror.test.ts` lines 206–227.

**What the code did before.** The daemon gained an implementation-defined error code,
`RPC_RESOURCE_LIMIT = -32003`, meaning "the parameters were valid and nothing was applied, but the server
refuses to answer at this size". It is raised when a directory listing would exceed 15 MiB and when a file
watch would pass 512 live subscriptions. The C++ header that mirrors the wire protocol did not declare it.

**Why that is wrong.** `RpcTypes.h` is the single place where the client records the protocol's constants,
and a cross-language test enforces that the two sides agree. A code present on one side only is drift, and
drift in this file is what the test exists to catch. The refusal also matters to this client specifically:
without it, an 18-megabyte listing would arrive as a single line past the client's 16-megabyte cap, and
exceeding that cap does not fail one reply, it drops the whole transport.

**What changed.** `inline constexpr int kResourceLimit = -32003;` was added alongside the existing
revision-mismatch and database-busy codes, following the same naming and comment style, with a comment
recording why the refusal matters on this side. No client behaviour changed: the client has no special
case for the database-busy code either, and both server messages are written to be read by a person, so
the text reaches the user verbatim through the generic error path.

**Test.** New case in `remote/test/rpc-mirror.test.ts`, "the resource-limit error code matches between
TypeScript and C++", following the two existing error-code cases: it reads the constant out of the C++
header, compares it to the TypeScript value, asserts it lies in the reserved −32000..−32099 band, and
asserts it differs from both other codes. With the header constant commented out the case fails with
"RpcTypes.h must declare kResourceLimit"; restored, the file's 14 tests pass.

### Cross-check against the daemon

Every wire name in `src/remote/RpcTypes.h` was compared against the daemon's own definitions: the seven
file methods, the three tmux methods, the twenty-one workspace methods, the two watch notifications, the
ping method and `server.info` are all present and byte-identical on both sides. The error codes match on
both sides (−32001 revision mismatch, −32002 database busy, and −32003 resource limit as of RPC8). Ping
and `server.info` are answered where the header comments claim. The null-result shape the C++ tests assume
for nothing-to-return handlers is real. An existing test already enforces this set equality in both
directions, so drift is guarded.

### Test totals for this area

`tst_rpcclient` finishes at 59 passed, 0 failed, 0 skipped, against a baseline of 53 passed before this
round. `remote/test/rpc-mirror.test.ts` is 14 passed, 0 failed. All builds and runs for this area were
done out of tree against Qt 6.10.2; nothing was built in the shared development tree.

# TM — terminals

## TM1 — Changing the workspace server left old identity lookups running and cached their answers under the new server

**File:** `src/terminal/TerminalFactory.cpp` lines 143–166 and 249–263.

**What the code did before.** The terminal factory asks the server "what tmux session name belongs to this
pane?", caches the answer, and keeps a list of panes waiting on each outstanding question. The setter that
tells it which workspace server it is talking to cleared the *cache* — with a comment explaining that a
name minted on one server is meaningless on another — but did nothing about the questions still on the
wire. The callback for such a question captured only the lookup key, not which server it had been asked of,
so whenever the reply arrived it was written into the cache unconditionally.

**Why that is wrong.** Two failure modes. The first is a wrong-server name: a pane asks against server A,
the setter switches to server B, the reply lands and inserts server A's tmux session name into the cache.
Every later attach for that key skips the round trip and uses it, so the pane attaches to a tmux session
belonging to a workspace the user is no longer looking at — or, because the attach command is "attach to
this session or create it", silently *creates* one under a foreign workspace's name. That is the
server-only-naming rule broken through the back door. The second is a stranded pane: the lookup function
documents that returning true guarantees an answer will be delivered, and the setter was the one path that
could break that promise, leaving panes showing "resolving…" forever with no retry.

This is reachable, though narrowly: in practice a server change is usually accompanied by a transport swap
whose sweep fails outstanding requests, but nothing orders that sweep before the setter.

**What changed.** The setter now, after clearing the cache, takes a snapshot of the outstanding lookup keys
and finishes each one through the existing "the lookup failed, stop waiting, offer Retry" path, with the
