reason "the workspace server changed while this terminal was being resolved". The snapshot is required
because finishing a lookup removes it from the waiting table and a waiter is free to react by starting a new
one. Separately, the lookup callback now captures which server the question was asked of and returns
immediately if the server has changed, so a late reply can neither poison the cache nor deliver to waiters
that have already been answered.

**Test.** None added. The lookup needs both a connected SSH pool and a live workspace database; this test
file has neither and there is no injection point for a substitute. Adding one purely for the test would be a
new abstraction. The existing 24 cases still pass.

## TM2 — Detaching could unbind a pane that had just been re-attached from inside its own teardown

**File:** `src/terminal/TerminalFactory.cpp` lines 408–440.

**What the code did before.** Detaching disconnected the device's signals, closed the channel, unbound the
controller, deleted the device, and then walked a live pane to the disconnected state. Closing the channel
ends the read stream, which makes the controller emit a state change, which reaches the QWebChannel bridge
and then the terminal pane's user-interface code — and anything running there is free to call back into this
same factory.

**Why that is wrong.** If that code re-enters attach on *this* pane, the nested attach opens a fresh channel
and binds it. Control then returns to the middle of the outer detach, which throws away the brand-new
binding — leaving a live but orphaned channel and a pane that will never receive another byte — and walks
the freshly attaching pane to disconnected. This file *already* documents exactly this re-entrancy hazard
twice, for two other lookups in the same class; the controller binding was the one place unguarded.

Reachability was checked: today the pane's state handler only clears a flag, and the attach path is guarded,
so nothing currently re-enters. This is therefore latent, not active — but it is one line of user-interface
code away (an automatic reconnect-on-drop handler is the obvious future change).

**What changed.** After closing the channel, the function records whether the controller is still bound to
the device it came here to release, always schedules that device for deletion, and only performs the unbind
and the disconnected transition when the binding is still its own. On the normal path that is always true, so
behaviour is unchanged.

**Test.** None added. Reproducing the re-entrancy needs a real SSH channel device, which needs a connection
pool, and the device is not substitutable at this seam.

## TM3 — The terminal page permanently lost flow-control credit for any batch that decoded to no text

**File:** `src/web/terminal/src/writer.ts` lines 70–93.

**What the code did before.** The writer receives two things: decoded text, and the number of raw bytes that
text was decoded from. The page must report those bytes back to C++ once the terminal emulator has drawn
them, because that is what allows C++ to send the next batch — a credit mechanism that stops a runaway remote
process from filling the browser's memory. When the decoded text was empty, the function returned early and
the byte count was silently discarded.

**Why that is wrong.** A batch legitimately decodes to no text: C++ flushes on a size or time threshold and
will cut a multi-byte character in half, so a flush can consist entirely of the first two bytes of a
three-byte character. The C++ side handles this correctly, carrying the byte weight forward onto the next
batch that *does* produce text — and that is exactly why the page must not throw such a weight away. C++ has
already charged those bytes against the pane's 512-kilobyte credit window; if the page never acknowledges
them, the window shrinks permanently. Repeated over a long session with a lot of non-ASCII output, the credit
reaches zero and the pane stops receiving output altogether.

Today the only caller skips the call when the decode is empty, so the two halves happen to be safe *in
combination*. But this is the exported, documented interface, the two halves of the accounting live in
different languages, and the asymmetry — C++ carries forward, the page discards — is exactly the kind of thing
that becomes a real leak the moment either side is touched.

**What changed.** The byte weight is now accumulated before the empty-text check, mirroring the C++
carry-forward. An empty batch still never becomes an empty chunk handed to the terminal emulator, which an
existing test pins, and closing still zeroes the count.

**Test.** New: "the byte weight of a text-less batch is carried onto the next real chunk". It writes an empty
batch worth 2 bytes and asserts nothing reaches the terminal and the backlog is empty; then writes a one-byte
character, acknowledges, and asserts the callback reported **3** — all three bytes, exactly once. It then
writes one more byte and asserts the carry was not double-counted. Fails on the old code, which reported 1.

## TM4 — Missing coverage for the scrollback eviction scan's direction and its bound

**File:** `src/terminal/tests/tst_terminalcontroller.cpp` lines 911–959.

**What was missing.** When the 2-megabyte rolling output buffer overflows, the controller does not cut at the
raw overflow point — that would land in the middle of a multi-byte character or an escape sequence, and the
buffer is later replayed verbatim into the terminal emulator. Instead it scans *forward* from the overflow
point, up to a 4-kilobyte window, for a line feed (a byte no escape sequence can contain), and failing that
skips continuation bytes to reach a character boundary. Two properties of that scan were untested: that it
starts at the overflow point rather than at the start of the buffer, and that the 4-kilobyte window actually
bounds it.

**Why that matters.** Both are silent-failure properties. If the line-feed search were ever changed to start
from the beginning, it would find a line feed *before* the cut, remove fewer bytes than the overflow required,
and leave the buffer permanently above its cap — the memory bound the whole mechanism exists for would stop
holding, with no visible symptom. If the window were dropped, a stream that offers no resume point (a
full-screen redraw with no line feeds) would scan the whole buffer and evict *everything*, wiping the user's
retained scrollback on a single overflow. Neither is caught by the two existing cases.

**What changed.** Two sub-cases were added to the existing test. The first fills the buffer with two line feeds
— a decoy ten bytes *before* the overflow point and the real resume point ten bytes *after* it — and asserts
the buffer ends at the expected size and, explicitly, at or below the cap; a search from the beginning would
leave it above. The second fills the buffer entirely with continuation bytes and no line feed, so the boundary
scan can never terminate on its own, and asserts exactly the window's worth of bytes were dropped and the
newest bytes are untouched; without the bound the buffer would be emptied.

## TM5 — An unused include in a shipped header

**File:** `src/terminal/TerminalFactory.h`. The header included Qt's vector container. Nothing in the file uses
it — the waiting list is a list and the maps are hash tables. A header include is paid for by every file that
includes it, and a stale one is a false signal about what the class depends on. Removed.

## TM6 — Seven unused includes across two test files

**Files:** `src/terminal/tests/tst_terminalfactory.cpp` and `src/terminal/tests/tst_terminalpage.cpp`. Seven
includes with no corresponding use anywhere in either file, verified by searching for each identifier and
finding only the include line. Same cost as TM5, plus in a test file it misleads a reader about what the test
touches — the file-system includes in the terminal-page test suggest file-system work, of which there is none.
Removed.

## TM7 — Test state held as a member for no reason

**File:** `src/terminal/tests/tst_terminalpage.cpp`. A member variable was assigned in one test, compared
against the expected value on the very next line, and cleared later in the same function. It was never read by
any other test. A member is shared, mutable state across a test object whose cases run in declaration order, so
putting single-function state there invites a later test to read a value it did not set; here it also obscured
that the assignment and the clear were pure noise around one comparison. Collapsed to a single comparison and
the member deleted.

## TM8 — A styling rule for a terminal state that cannot exist

**File:** `src/web/terminal/index.html`. The page's status strip is styled per state, and there was a rule for
a "reconnecting" state. The strip's text is written verbatim from the C++ state names, whose complete set is:
unloaded, opening channel, attaching tmux, ready, disconnected, error. The model layer states explicitly that
session-level progress — connecting, authenticating, reconnecting — is tracked once for the whole application
and deliberately *not* mirrored onto a pane. So the selector can never match. It is stale styling that implies
a pane state which does not exist, which is exactly the kind of thing that makes someone add one. Removed and
replaced with a comment enumerating the six values the attribute can actually hold.

## TM9 — A wrong character could be drawn when an overflow threw away the second half of a character whose first half had already been sent

**Files:** `src/terminal/TerminalController.cpp` lines 213–289, `src/terminal/TerminalController.h` lines
230–253 and 263–266, `src/terminal/tests/tst_terminalcontroller.cpp`,
`src/terminal/tests/tst_terminalfactory.cpp` lines 732–780.

**What the code did before.** The controller collects the bytes arriving from the remote shell and hands them
to the page in batches, cutting a batch wherever a size or time threshold happens to fall — including in the
middle of a character that takes more than one byte to write. The page-side decoder in
`ch::TerminalBridge::onFlushReady()` is stateful, so on its own that split is harmless: the decoder keeps the
leading bytes and finishes the character from the following batch.

When the pane is hidden, or the page is behind on acknowledgements, batches are not sent but appended to a
2-megabyte rolling buffer instead, and when that buffer overflows the oldest bytes are evicted.

**Why that is wrong.** The two halves of a split character can end up in two different places with two
different levels of protection. The leading bytes may already have been sent, charged against the credit
window and consumed by the decoder, while the continuation bytes are still only in the rolling buffer. An
overflow then evicts those continuation bytes, and the decoder — which is still holding half a character —
completes it from whatever bytes survive the eviction. The result is one wrong glyph, a U+FFFD replacement
character, at the top of the replay the user sees when the pane comes back.

The previous round's stated reason for leaving this alone was that fixing it meant resetting the decoder when
an eviction happens, which would need a new signal from the controller to the bridge — a whole new mechanism
for a cosmetic outcome. That reasoning assumed the fix had to live at the eviction end. It does not.

**What changed.** The fix reuses the mechanism the file already has rather than adding a second one. The
eviction already refuses to cut inside a byte sequence: it moves the cut forward to a point no sequence
straddles. The flush now applies the same rule at the other end of the buffer.

A new helper, `incompleteTrailingUtf8()`, reports how many trailing bytes (0 to 3) are the start of a
character whose continuation bytes have not arrived yet. `flush()` chops that fragment off the batch and
leaves it in the pending buffer, so **no batch ever ends inside a multi-byte character** — whether that batch
is sent or retained. The decoder therefore never carries state from one batch into the next, so there is no
half-character for a later eviction to orphan. This costs no latency: those are exactly the bytes the decoder
would have withheld anyway, and there are at most three of them. A pending buffer consisting of nothing but a
character head produces no flush at all until the rest arrives.

The other half of the rule is deliberate: bytes that are not legal UTF-8 at all are never held back. A
terminal carries binary — `cat` of a JPEG, for instance — and a byte that can never be completed into a
character would otherwise sit in the pending buffer forever and stall the stream. So `incompleteTrailingUtf8()`
returns 0 for plain ASCII, for a character that is already complete, and for anything that cannot be the start
of a valid character (leading bytes 0xF8–0xFF, or continuation bytes with no lead). Those go out immediately.

The escape-sequence rule that TM4 covers is untouched. The only bytes ever held back are UTF-8 leading bytes
in the range 0xC0–0xF7 and their continuations, which cannot occur inside the plain-ASCII body of a CSI or OSC
escape sequence; and holding them is not destructive in any case — nothing is dropped, the order is preserved,
and the emulator's parser is stateful across writes.

**Test.** Two new cases in `tst_terminalcontroller.cpp`.
`flushBoundariesNeverSplitAMultiByteCharacter()` covers four situations: an ordinary two-bytes-of-three split;
a pending buffer holding only a character head, which must produce no flush; non-UTF-8 bytes (`FF FE 80`),
which must go out immediately rather than be held; and the retained path, where a hidden pane's rolling buffer
likewise never ends mid-character.
`anEvictionCannotOrphanHalfOfACharacterInTheDecoder()` reproduces the whole scenario end to end: a batch that
would have ended two bytes into a euro sign is sent, the pane is hidden, the third byte is retained, a
2-megabyte flood evicts it, and the pane comes back. Every sent batch is then fed through one stateful
`QStringDecoder`, exactly as the bridge does, and the decoded result must contain no replacement character.

`bridgeDecodesUtf8SplitAcrossFlushes()` in `tst_terminalfactory.cpp` was updated because it pinned the *old*
contract — that the controller emits the half-character and the bridge carries its weight. Its first half now
pins the new controller contract; its second half still pins the bridge's own carry-forward, which is TM3's
property, by driving the flush signal directly, since that is the bridge's actual seam.

Both new cases were run against the pre-fix controller sources and both failed:
`flushBoundariesNeverSplitAMultiByteCharacter()` on a compared-value mismatch, and
`anEvictionCannotOrphanHalfOfACharacterInTheDecoder()` on the replacement character being present in the
decoded output — that is, the wrong glyph was observed, not just inferred. After the fix the controller test
binary reports 35 passed, 0 failed, and the factory test binary 25 passed, 0 failed.

## TM10 — A factory method callable from QML returned an object with no owner, which QML may delete while a pane is still using it

**Files:** `src/terminal/TerminalFactory.cpp` lines 52–59, `src/terminal/TerminalFactory.h` lines 52–54,
`src/terminal/tests/tst_terminalfactory.cpp` lines 104–123.

**What the code did before.** `TerminalFactory::create(QObject* owner = nullptr)` did
`new TerminalController(owner)`. A caller that took the default got back a `QObject` with no parent.

**Why that is wrong.** It is more than a memory leak. `create()` is marked `Q_INVOKABLE`, so QML can call it,
and Qt's QML engine gives a returned `QObject*` that has no parent **JavaScript ownership** — the QML garbage
collector is then free to destroy it. That object is a live pane's controller: its flush timer, its 2-megabyte
rolling buffer, its transport connections. It could be collected at a moment nothing on the C++ side controls.
A C++ caller that took the default would instead simply leak it. Neither outcome is a decision this factory
should be leaving to its caller, and the file already knew that: `createBridge()`, immediately below, has
always used `owner ? owner : controller`.

The previous round left this alone on the grounds that it was the identical signature and default as the
editor's equivalent factory, so changing one of the pair would introduce a second convention. That reasoning
collapsed on inspection: `EditorFactory::create()` (`src/editor/EditorFactory.h` line 30,
`src/editor/EditorFactory.cpp` line 14) has the identical hazard rather than being a correct counter-example.
It was reported to the coordinator and routed to the editor area rather than edited from here.

**What changed.** `create()` now does `new TerminalController(owner ? owner : this)` — the same shape as
`createBridge()` — with the reasoning recorded in the comment, and the header now documents that the returned
object is never unparented. There is no production behaviour change: the only real caller,
`src/qml/TerminalPaneView.qml` line 139, always passes an owner.

**Test.** New `TstTerminalFactory::createOwnerlessControllerIsAdoptedByTheFactory()` calls `create()` with no
argument, asserts the controller's parent is the factory, and asserts through a `QPointer` that the controller
is destroyed when the factory goes out of scope. Against a build with only that one line reverted, it fails
with the controller's parent reported as null.

## TM11 — Replaying a hidden pane's retained output ignored the flow-control window and could push 2 megabytes at a page with room for 8 kilobytes

**Files:** `src/terminal/TerminalController.cpp` lines 291–373, `src/terminal/TerminalController.h` lines
93–96, `src/web/terminal/src/writer.ts` lines 26–30, `src/terminal/tests/tst_terminalcontroller.cpp`.

**What the code did before.** `releaseRetained()` handed the entire rolling buffer to the page as one batch.

**Why that is wrong.** The credit window exists to stop the client sending more than the page can draw: the
page acknowledges bytes as it renders them, and C++ must not have more than `kMaxUnacknowledgedBytes`
(512 kilobytes) outstanding. A full rolling buffer is 2 megabytes. Releasing it in one batch to a page that
still had, say, 8 kilobytes of credit left broke that 512-kilobyte bound by a factor of four — and did so
through the very mechanism that exists to enforce it, pushing the excess straight into the WebChannel and
Chromium queues that the window is there to keep bounded.

The previous round left this alone because splitting the replay would supposedly need "a second place that
decides where a safe cut lands", and deciding where a cut is safe is the thing the eviction goes to some
trouble to get right. That was avoidable: the eviction's cut logic can simply be shared instead of duplicated,
so there is now exactly one such place, not two.

**What changed.** The eviction's boundary search was extracted verbatim from `appendHidden()` into a
`resyncBoundary(buffer, from)` helper — the first line feed within the 4-kilobyte resync window, failing that
the first byte that is not a UTF-8 continuation byte, failing that `from` plus the window, and `from` itself
when `from` is past the end. `appendHidden()` now calls it and is byte-for-byte identical in behaviour, which
the untouched `hiddenEvictionResumesOnACleanBoundary()` test pins across all four of its sub-cases.

`releaseRetained()` now releases at most `kMaxUnacknowledgedBytes - m_unacknowledged` bytes, cutting through
that same `resyncBoundary()`. The remainder stays retained, in order, and the next acknowledgement releases the
next slice. Progress cannot stall: the guard at the top of the function ensures the allowance is at least one
byte, and `resyncBoundary()` never returns less than the offset it was given.

The escape-sequence boundary rule is not weakened. Unlike the eviction, this cut destroys nothing — both halves
reach the emulator's stateful parser in sequence, so even a split escape sequence is reassembled there. The cut
goes through `resyncBoundary()` anyway because preferring a line feed is the tidier split and costs nothing.
The effect on the overshoot is the point: it drops from up to `kHiddenBufferMaxBytes` (2 megabytes) to at most
`kHiddenResyncWindowBytes` (4 kilobytes, a couple of terminal lines).

Two pieces of documentation were made true again by the same change. `TerminalController.h` said that becoming
visible "replays the retained buffer once as a single batch"; it no longer does. The header comment in
`writer.ts` said the page-side backlog is bounded by the credit window plus one release batch — where a release
batch could be 2 megabytes; the bound is now roughly twice the window. Both were corrected. The TypeScript
change is comment-only: `tsc --noEmit` on the terminal page project is clean and the existing writer tests
still pass 10 of 10, with no behavioural test added because none is warranted for a comment.

**Test.** New `releasingRetainedOutputStaysInsideTheCreditWindow()` in `tst_terminalcontroller.cpp`, in two
parts. First: a full 2-megabyte buffer containing no line feed replays exactly one window's worth, the
outstanding-byte count lands on exactly `kMaxUnacknowledgedBytes`, the remainder stays retained, and draining
it under repeated acknowledgements reassembles byte-for-byte into what went in — nothing dropped, nothing
duplicated, order preserved. Second: a line feed ten bytes past the window is preferred, so the batch is the
window plus 11 bytes, and the overshoot is asserted to be no more than the window plus the resync window.

`acknowledgementsReleaseRetainedOutputInOrder()` was updated: it previously asserted the old contract, that
becoming visible releases everything retained at once as a single batch. It now asserts the credit-bounded
contract — one batch's worth acknowledged releases one batch's worth — and then drains the remaining four
batches in a loop, still asserting exact byte-for-byte reassembly and no re-delivery once the buffer is empty.
Its coverage is strictly increased, not relaxed.

Against the pre-fix controller both cases fail; the new one reports an actual first released batch of
2,097,152 bytes where 524,288 was expected.

### Checked in this area and found correct

Recorded so these are known to have been verified rather than skipped: server-only tmux naming (no client-side
invention remains anywhere in this area, and the only hole found was TM1); escape sequences are never split
destructively (the only destructive cut is the eviction, handled by the forward scan, now better tested;
everywhere else the emulator's parser is stateful across writes and the writer only ever concatenates); the
QWebChannel bridge's lifetime and thread affinity (the bridge holds its controller through a `QPointer`, every
page-callable method is null-guarded, and everything lives on one thread); acknowledgement accounting across a
page reload, traced through all four orderings of the relevant events; the decision not to reset the text
decoder on reload, which is correct because the byte stream is continuous; teardown while a write is in flight;
and the web bundle's staging-and-rename build script, traced through all three of its failure points.

Nothing in `src/terminal/` or `src/web/terminal/` claims ownership of a pane, intercepts `http`/`https` URLs,
or treats a handler as a competing pane type: a terminal pane is a leaf view in the layout, not a viewer-pane
handler. No contradiction with that framing was found.

Two edits in this area's files belong to other areas and were applied mechanically on their behalf: a comment
wording change in `TerminalController.h` line 143 handed over by the SSH area, and two call sites in
`TerminalFactory.cpp` lines 230–231 left on the old field type by the models area's identifier-type migration,
without which this area did not compile. The originating areas were told.

# ED — the editor

## ED1 — A background re-read could destroy the buffer of a save that was still in flight

**File:** `src/editor/EditorController.cpp`, the reload reply callback; guard added at lines 987–999.

**What the code did before.** The editor pane holds one buffer — the text the user sees and edits. Two
independent things can replace it. The first is a *system-initiated reload*: the daemon reports that the
file changed on the server, or the reconciliation step after a dropped connection runs, and the controller
re-reads the file and pushes the server's bytes into the page. The second is a *save*: the user presses the
save key, the page hands over its current bytes, and the controller writes them to the server. Both are
asynchronous round trips over SSH, so they can overlap.

The reload's reply handler had one protection: it remembered a counter of how many edits the controller had
been *told about*, and if that counter had moved while the read was in flight it threw the fetched bytes away
rather than overwrite the user's typing. It had no protection at all against a save being in flight.

**Why that is wrong.** That edit counter only advances when the *page* reports an edit, and the page
deliberately delays that report by 500 milliseconds. The save key beats that delay comfortably. So this
sequence is entirely ordinary: the buffer is clean; an external process writes the file; the watch
notification arrives and the controller starts a re-read; within the same few hundred milliseconds the user
types and saves, so the controller has never been told about those keystrokes and its counter has not moved;
and the re-read answers first.

Before the fix, that last step took the "nothing changed, install the bytes" path. It pushed the server's text
into the page — wiping the user's unsaved keystrokes — reset the revision baseline to the server's new
revision, cleared the dirty flag, and truncated the crash-recovery snapshot, which was the only other copy of
those keystrokes. The write's own reply then arrived and, because the file really had moved, was refused as a
revision conflict. The user's edits existed in exactly two places and both were destroyed, while the interface
showed a conflict notice for a save whose content no longer existed anywhere.

This is also an internal inconsistency: the twin path that reconciles after a reconnect already contains
exactly this rule, twice. The reload path simply never grew it.

**What changed.** A guard was added immediately after the existing counter check: a reload the system started
on its own now yields to an in-flight save, and the write's own reply — success, conflict, or error — decides
the pane's next state. Reloads the *user* explicitly asked for (the Reload button, or a page that reloaded and
lost its buffer) still install, because replacing the buffer is the request.

**Test.** New: `TstEditorController::aSaveInFlightIsNotClobberedByASystemReload`. It drives exactly the
sequence above and asserts no content reached the page, the guarded revision is still the one the save was
issued against, the pane still reports "saving", and no extra request went out — so the recovery snapshot was
not truncated either. It then answers the write with the real revision-mismatch payload and asserts the
conflict is reported honestly. Confirmed to fail on the unmodified controller, where one content push reaches
the page.

## ED2 — Clicking Reload on a save conflict could resurrect the edits it had just discarded

**File:** `src/web/editor/src/index.ts` lines 361–372.

**What the code did before.** When a save is refused because the file changed on the server, the page shows a
notice with Reload and Overwrite buttons. Just before building that notice it re-arms its delayed
crash-recovery snapshot timer, which is correct in itself: the buffer is still unsaved. The Reload button then
cleared the notice and asked the host to re-fetch the file. It did not disarm that timer.

**Why that is wrong.** Reload means "throw my local edits away and give me the server's version". The host
honours that by re-reading, replacing the buffer, clearing its dirty flag and truncating the recovery snapshot.
But if the user clicks Reload within 500 milliseconds of the conflict appearing, the armed timer fires during
the reload's round trip and reports the *pre-reload* buffer — the edits the user just discarded. Depending on
which round trip lands first the outcome is either harmless or genuinely wrong: the recovery snapshot on the
server ends up holding discarded edits, so the next time this pane opens this file it offers to restore work
the user explicitly threw away; and the host's dirty flag is raised for a buffer identical to the file, which
suppresses the automatic reload of a clean buffer on the next external change.

**What changed.** The Reload handler now cancels the pending snapshot before asking for the re-fetch, with a
comment noting that the save path cancels for exactly the same reason and that this path bypassed it.

**Test.** None added. The cancel operation is already covered by two existing cases. The button wiring lives in
the file that imports the whole editor runtime and needs a browser document, so it is not reachable from this
package's test harness; extracting it would be a restructure well beyond a bug fix.

## ED3 — A by-value parameter was copied instead of moved

**File:** `src/editor/EditorController.cpp` lines 137–139, with a new top-level include at line 11.

The constructor takes a string by value — the convention for "I want my own copy" — and then initialised the
member from it, making a *second* copy of the already-copied parameter. A needless allocation, and more
importantly the classic sign of a by-value parameter someone forgot to move. The sibling file does it correctly,
so the two were inconsistent. Changed to a move, with the required standard-library include added at the top of
the file rather than inside the function.

## ED4 — The same JSON value was converted to an object twice

**File:** `src/editor/EditorController.cpp` lines 349–364.

The reconnect reconciliation receives a file-status result as a generic JSON value and called the "convert to
object" accessor twice a few lines apart — once to derive permissions, once to read the revision. That accessor
is not free; it materialises a new object each time. Redundant work on every reconnect, and a maintenance
hazard, because two conversions of the same value invite a future edit to change one and not the other. Hoisted
to a single conversion with a one-line comment. No behaviour change.

## ED5 — A byte offset was narrowed from 64 bits to 32 in a test helper

**File:** `src/editor/tests/tst_liveeditorreconnect.cpp` line 315.

A search result was assigned to a 32-bit integer where the function returns a 64-bit size. It cannot bite at the
sizes this helper handles, but it is an implicit narrowing that some warning configurations flag, and its exact
twin in the sibling live test already uses the wider type. Two copies of the same helper that disagree is
precisely the drift that turns into a real bug when one of them is later pointed at a large payload. Corrected
to match the sibling.

## ED6 — A save's reply still rewrote the buffer after a same-path reopen or an explicit reload replaced it

**Files:** `src/editor/EditorController.cpp` — the guard at lines 741–764 and the matching one in the conflict
follow-up at lines 803–812; `src/editor/EditorController.h` lines 285–290.

Found after the round's first commit, by a review note pointing out that `ED1` had closed only one of the ways a
load and a save can collide.

**What the code did before.** A save is one network round trip. While it is in flight the pane can be handed a
completely different buffer by a *load* — either a re-read of the same file or a fresh open of it. The save's reply
handler defended itself against exactly one version of that: it captured the file path when the save was issued and
dropped the reply if the pane had since switched to a **different** file. Nothing else was captured.

**Why that is wrong.** The path check cannot see the case where the path stays the same, and there are three
reachable ways for a load to replace the buffer without changing it:

1. The user presses the page's **Reload** button while a save is in flight. That is explicitly allowed to replace
   the buffer — it means "discard my edits" — so it does.
2. The embedded editor page reloads (a crash, a refresh) while a save is in flight. Because the buffer is dirty at
   that moment, the controller deliberately **re-opens** the file rather than reloading it, so that the
   crash-recovery snapshot is offered back to the user. That is an `open()` of the same path.
3. A host re-opens the file the pane is already showing.

In all three the load finishes first and takes the buffer over: it adopts the server's revision as the new save
guard and pushes the server's bytes at the page. The write's reply then arrived and ran in full against that
freshly loaded buffer.

On success it re-baselined the revision to the one the *old* write produced — so the pane's save guard no longer
described anything on screen, and the next save would be refused as a conflict for no visible reason — cleared the
dirty flag for a buffer nobody had checked, and emitted `saved` for a save the page never asked for.

What it did to the crash-recovery snapshot differs by route, and the difference matters because the two `open()`
routes are the worse ones:

* Route 1 (`reload()`) truncates the snapshot itself as part of committing, so the save's success path merely
  repeated a truncation that had already happened.
* Routes 2 and 3 (`open()`) do **not** truncate it. They reset only their own local bookkeeping and then *re-probe*
  the snapshot, which is exactly how unsaved work gets offered back to the user. So on those routes the superseded
  save's success path truncated a snapshot the load had just re-probed — potentially while the "unsaved changes
  were recovered" offer was on screen, destroying the very work being offered.

On a conflict it was wrong in a third way: that path takes a second network hop to look up the current revision,
giving a load two windows to land in rather than one, and the outcome is a pane parked in "file changed on disk"
offering Reload-or-Overwrite over a revision mismatch that concerns bytes it no longer holds.

**What changed.** `save()` now also captures the controller's existing load counter — the same one every load reply
already checks itself against — and both hops of the save's reply handling drop the reply if it has moved. Silent,
like the neighbouring path check, because the load that superseded the save has already set this pane's state.

No new state was added. A first version of this fix introduced a second counter, bumped only where a load actually
*commits* to the buffer, out of a concern that the existing counter is bumped when a load *starts* — so a
system-initiated reload that starts during a save and is then discarded would bump it and wrongly swallow the
write's real outcome, which is the honest conflict `ED1` exists to report. That concern was tested and found
unreachable: every system-initiated reload site is gated on the buffer being clean, and `save()` marks it dirty for
the whole write, so a system reload cannot begin during a save at all. The second counter was therefore removed in
favour of the counter the file already had, and the invariant that makes that safe is written down at the guard,
together with what to do if a future change ever breaks it.

**Tests.** Two new cases in `src/editor/tests/tst_editorcontroller.cpp`.
`aSaveReplyIsDroppedAfterAnExplicitReloadTookOverTheBuffer` drives route 1 and answers the write
**successfully** — the worst case, because every branch of the success path rewrites pane state — then asserts no
`saved` was emitted, the revision and state are exactly as the reload left them, and no further request went out
(which is what proves the reloaded buffer's recovery snapshot was not truncated).
`aSaveConflictIsDroppedAfterASamePathReopenTookOverTheBuffer` drives route 3 and answers the write with a stale
revision carrying no revision of its own, so the controller would otherwise take its second hop; it asserts no
conflict, no error, no `saved`, and that the stat never reached the wire.

Both were confirmed to fail with the guard removed: the first reports one `saved` signal, the second catches the
superseded save chasing a conflict revision. `ED1`'s own case passes either way, which is what shows the two fixes
are independent rather than one masking the other.

## ED7 — A successful save could leave behind a crash-recovery snapshot holding older text

**Files:** `src/editor/EditorController.cpp` — `writeRecovery()` around lines 896–946, the new
`honourDeferredRecoveryClear()` and `clearRecovery()` at lines 960–990; `src/editor/EditorController.h` lines
212–216 and 322–348.

Found after `ED6`, by a review note. It is the same family of defect — an asynchronous reply acting on stale
knowledge — but on the recovery snapshot rather than the buffer.

**What the code did before.** While the user types, the page periodically reports its buffer and the controller
writes a snapshot of it to a per-pane file on the server, so unsaved work survives a crash. Two pieces of
bookkeeping describe that snapshot: the revision to guard the next write against, and a flag saying whether the
snapshot currently holds anything. **Both are only set when the snapshot write's reply lands.**

A successful save is supposed to retire the snapshot, because the file on the server now *is* the buffer, and it
does that with a revision-guarded truncate. But the truncate routine began by giving up when that flag said no
snapshot was known — a reasonable-looking check that saves a pointless round trip.

**Why that is wrong.** Between issuing a snapshot write and its reply, the controller does not yet know a snapshot
exists. A save that succeeds inside that window called the truncate, hit the "nothing was ever snapshotted" exit,
and did nothing. The snapshot write then answered and recorded a live snapshot — after the save that was supposed to
retire it.

If the user typed nothing between the report and the save, the snapshot and the file hold the same bytes, the next
open compares them, finds them equal and stays quiet. But typing again between the report and the save is
ordinary — the page's report is debounced by 500 milliseconds and the save key beats it — and then the surviving
snapshot holds text **older** than the file that was just saved. The next time the pane opens that file it therefore
offers to restore work the save had already superseded, and accepting the offer walks the buffer backwards over the
saved version. That offer used to be emitted into the void; `QM9` in this same round connected it to a real dialog,
which is what turns this from an invisible inconsistency into a wrong answer the user can act on.

**What changed.** The truncate routine no longer discards the request when it cannot act. If any snapshot write is
outstanding it records the intent, and the reply of the **last** outstanding snapshot write carries it out, guarded
by the revision that reply just supplied. A small helper owns that decision and is called from each path on which a
snapshot write chain ends while the pane still holds the file — including the stale-guard retry, which is kept
counted across its extra lookup hop so a truncate deferred during that hop is not decided on knowledge the retry is
about to replace. The one path that deliberately does not call it is a file switch, because re-opening resets the
recovery bookkeeping and any deferred truncate along with it, so nothing is owed to the file being left behind.

Four details are load-bearing. Each was arrived at by correcting a simpler attempt, and each is defended by its own
test that fails when only that rule is removed:

* **It is a count, not a flag.** Nothing serialises snapshot writes, so two reports in quick succession put two on
  the wire. With a boolean, the first reply would read "idle" while the second was still outstanding, truncate on its
  own revision, and then be overwritten by the write still in flight — putting the snapshot back after the save that
  asked for it to go.
* **A fresh report cancels a deferred truncate.** If the user types again *after* the save succeeded, those bytes are
  unsaved work the save never wrote, and the snapshot holding them must survive. Only a genuinely new report cancels;
  the internal retry that re-sends the same bytes after a stale-guard rejection deliberately does not.
* **The stale-guard retry stays counted across its extra lookup.** When a snapshot write is refused because a
  snapshot from an earlier session already occupies the slot, the controller looks up that slot's current revision
  and re-writes. That is two more round trips. If the chain stops being counted for the lookup, a save succeeding in
  that window finds no snapshot revision at all, drops the truncate request, and the re-write then leaves a snapshot
  of pre-save text behind a successful save.
* **Replies are stamped with a recovery generation, bumped by every `open()`.** The count and the deferred intent are
  shared pane state, so a reply belonging to a file the pane has since left must return before touching either.
  Otherwise the stale decrement releases the *new* file's deferred truncate early, guarded by the wrong revision and
  before the write it was waiting for has answered. This is the same stamping idiom the file already uses for loads
  and for watch subscriptions, rather than a new mechanism.

`open()` also resets the count and the pending intent, because a truncate meant for the file being left behind is not
owed to the new one, and the count is clamped at zero on decrement as a belt-and-braces measure.

**Tests.** Four new cases in `src/editor/tests/tst_editorcontroller.cpp`, one per rule above, each confirmed to fail
when its own rule is removed while the other three stay:

* `aSuccessfulSaveStillClearsASnapshotThatWasStillInFlight` — reports a buffer, saves newer bytes before that
  snapshot answers, answers the save first, asserts nothing is truncated yet (no revision exists to guard against),
  then answers the snapshot and asserts the truncate goes out with empty content guarded by the revision just
  supplied, exactly once. Without the deferral: the snapshot survives the save.
* `aReportAfterASaveCancelsTheTruncateThatSaveDeferred` — the opposite order: the save succeeds, the user types again
  while the first snapshot is still unanswered, then both snapshot writes answer oldest-first. Asserts no truncate is
  issued and the pane still reports unsaved changes. Without the cancellation: the deferred truncate deletes work
  typed after the save.
* `aDeferredTruncateSurvivesTheSnapshotRetryChain` — refuses the snapshot write first, so the retry chain parks on its
  lookup, and lands a successful save in exactly that window. Asserts nothing is truncated during the lookup and that
  the truncate runs after the re-write, guarded by the re-write's revision. Without the retry staying counted: the
  truncate is lost.
* `aStaleSnapshotReplyDoesNotDisturbTheNextFilesBookkeeping` — leaves a snapshot unanswered for one file, switches to
  another, gives the new file its own snapshot and a save that defers a truncate, then answers the *old* file's
  snapshot. Asserts that changes nothing, and that the truncate runs only when the new file's own snapshot answers,
  guarded by its revision. Without the generation stamp: the stale reply releases the truncate early.

## ED8 — Pressing the save key twice inside one round trip reported a conflict against the user's own save

**Files:** `src/editor/EditorController.cpp` lines 558–566, 728–751, 754–772, 780–792, 830–841 and 861–865;
`src/editor/EditorController.h` lines 196–200 and 302–329; tests at
`src/editor/tests/tst_editorcontroller.cpp` lines 2622–2805.

**What the code did before.** Every save sends one write to the server carrying the revision the buffer was
loaded at, and the server refuses the write if the file has moved on since. Pressing the save key twice inside
one SSH round trip put two writes on the wire carrying the *same* revision. The first one retired that
revision, so the second was refused, and the controller reported that refusal as an honest conflict: the pane
showed "File changed on disk" for a change made by nobody but this client, one moment earlier.

**Why that is wrong.** Nothing on the server changed. The user pressed one key twice, meaning one thing —
"make the file match my buffer" — and the two writes were raced against each other instead of being run one
after the other. The previous round recorded this as reachable but unfixable, on the grounds that each
candidate fix traded one problem for another: dropping the second save swallows a deliberate action, showing
"a save is already in progress" replaces a misleading notice with a noisy one, and holding an
outstanding-save flag in the page makes the save key dead for the rest of the session if any path ever fails
to deliver a final outcome. That reasoning treats the second save as something to accept or refuse. It is
neither; it is the same intent, and the only defect is the race.

**What changed.** Saves are now serialised per pane: at most one write is on the wire at a time.

* A save arriving while a write is in flight and carrying **identical bytes** *is* that write. It is not sent
  again — the reply already on its way answers both, and re-sending would only move the file's modification
  time and wake every other watcher of the file.
* A save carrying **different bytes** is real work. Its bytes are queued and issued by the in-flight write's
  reply, guarded by the revision **that reply produced** — the guard the second save needed and could not
  have known.
* The superseded write reports nothing at all: no success signal, no new revision baseline, no clearing of
  the crash-recovery snapshot, no state change. The file is not the buffer yet, so the pane stays in its
  saving state and the last write of the chain is the single outcome the page sees. This is the discipline
  `ED6` established — the operation that superseded you has already set this pane's state.
* A write that **fails** ends the chain and drops the queued bytes. Nothing is lost: the buffer is still
  marked unsaved, one honest notice is shown, and the notice's Retry and Overwrite buttons re-read the buffer
  at the moment they are clicked, so they send whatever is current.
* Opening a different file resets the chain and stamps it with a fresh generation number, so a write still on
  the wire for the file being left cannot release or consume the new file's chain.

No new prompt is shown to the user. A double press now produces exactly one save.

The flag lives in the controller rather than in the page, which is the shape the previous round preferred but
rejected. The hazard it cited — a path that never delivers a final outcome leaves the flag stuck on — does
not exist on this side of the seam: the JSON-RPC client guarantees that every pending callback fires, on a
reply, when the transport closes, when a transport is replaced with requests outstanding, and in its own
destructor (`src/remote/CodeharbordClient.cpp` lines 71–112, 191–199, 643–657 and 672–682). So the in-flight
flag cannot latch. The controller is also the authoritative half of the save guard, and any host can invoke
the save slot directly, bypassing a flag held in the page.

**Test.** Four new cases. `aSecondSaveOfTheSameBufferIsNotASecondWriteAndNotAConflict` drives the reported
scenario and asserts that no second write reaches the wire, that exactly one success is reported with the new
revision, and that no conflict or error is reported.
`aSecondSaveOfChangedBytesIsRewrittenAgainstTheRevisionItNowNeeds` asserts that the second save's bytes go out
only after the first reply, guarded by the revision that first write produced rather than the stale one the
page supplied, and that the superseded write reported nothing.
`aQueuedSaveIsDroppedWhenTheWriteItWaitedOnIsRefused` asserts that a genuine conflict — someone else really
did move the file — is still reported exactly once, and that the queued bytes are not fired at a server that
has just refused the write. `aSaveOnANewFileDoesNotJoinThePreviousFilesSaveChain` asserts the per-file reset:
a save on a newly opened file goes out immediately rather than queueing behind the previous file's
outstanding write, and that previous file's reply, when it lands, decides nothing. All four were confirmed to
fail against the pre-fix controller.

The fix was checked against the earlier findings in this area rather than assumed compatible with them. All
53 pre-existing cases still pass, including `ED1`'s, `ED6`'s two and `ED7`'s four. `ED6`'s guards now run
*after* the chain bookkeeping is released, which is the only correct order, since a superseded save is off
the wire whatever else is true of it. `ED7`'s snapshot truncation is deliberately not performed by a write
that has a queued successor, because the buffer is not on the server yet and the snapshot holding it must
survive; the final write of the chain truncates it as before. And `ED6`'s invariant that a system-initiated
reload cannot begin during a save — every such site is gated on the buffer being clean, and a save marks it
unsaved for the whole write — still holds, because the whole chain is one continuous unsaved window.

## ED9 — A file with a byte-order mark was saved back without it

**Files:** `src/web/editor/src/buffer.ts` (new, 45 lines); `src/web/editor/src/index.ts` lines 30–35, 256–259
and 300; tests at `src/web/editor/test/buffer.test.ts` (new, 8 cases) with
`src/web/editor/test/monaco-internals.d.ts` (new).

**What the code did before.** A byte-order mark is an optional three-byte prefix (the character U+FEFF) that
some tools put at the start of a UTF-8 file. The page asked the embedded editor for its text with the plain
`getValue()` call, in two places: the callback that supplies the buffer for both saving and the
crash-recovery snapshot, and the comparison that decides whether a freshly loaded file is already what the
buffer holds.

**Why that is wrong.** The previous round recorded this as nothing to fix, stating that a mark "survives being
set into the editor, and comes back out unchanged". That is not what the editor does. Read directly in the
installed `monaco-editor@0.52`: setting a value builds the text buffer through `createTextBuffer()`, and the
builder **strips a leading U+FEFF off the first chunk** and parks it on the buffer as a separate field,
deliberately not part of the text. The value accessor prepends that field back **only** when asked to
preserve it, and the editor widget's accessor defaults that option to off. Probed empirically before anything
was changed: feeding `"\uFEFFalpha\r\nbeta\r\n"` to the real buffer builder yields the mark held separately,
the line ending detected as carriage-return-plus-newline, and text with the mark gone.

So opening a file that had a mark and saving it back **wrote the file without its mark**, silently, and the
crash-recovery snapshot lost it too. The daemon is not at fault and was not changed: its read path decodes
with the mark deliberately left inside the content (`remote/src/files.ts` lines 119–126, with a comment
citing the specification's requirement that a save round-trips the exact bytes), and the C++ controller
carries the string through unchanged. The page was the only place the mark could be lost.

The other half of the previous entry was right. Line endings did round-trip already, because the accessor's
default line-ending preference is whatever the editor detected from the content it was given. But they
round-tripped by omission, with nothing written down recording the rule, and the same call was one argument
away from rewriting every line of every carriage-return-ended file on its first save.

**What changed.** Both halves now go through one documented helper. A new module `buffer.ts` exports
`bufferText(source)`, which asks for the value with the mark preserved and the line ending left as the file's
own. The empty string passed as the line-ending option is not a placeholder: the editor overrides the line
ending only for exactly `"\n"` or `"\r\n"`, so an empty string is how a caller asks for "whatever the file
uses" while still asking to keep the mark. The module header states the round-trip rule, why each half of it
is needed, and the one thing it cannot promise — a file with **mixed** line endings is normalised to whichever
kind is in the majority when the content is set into the editor, before any value accessor can see it. That
is inherent to editing in this editor and not something a value accessor can undo. The helper is a separate
module rather than a function inside the page entry for the same reason the language and recovery helpers
already are: the page entry pulls in the whole editor runtime as a side effect and cannot be loaded in the
test harness. Both call sites now use it, so a reload of an unchanged file with a mark is also correctly
recognised as unchanged instead of being pushed into the editor again.

**Test.** Eight new cases. The standalone editor cannot be constructed outside a browser, but the part that
actually decides this — the text buffer — has no dependency on a browser document, so the tests drive the
**real** buffer through a stub whose set-value and get-value bodies are the editor's own, with nothing between
those two calls and the buffer that is not also absent in the real page. The cases cover: carriage-return
endings survive; plain newlines are not promoted; a mark survives; the editor's default accessor is the one
that loses the mark (which pins the defect, so nobody "simplifies" the helper back); a mark and
carriage-return endings together; files with no trailing newline and empty files keep their shape; a
mark-only file; and a reload re-deriving both. **Five of the eight fail against the pre-fix accessor and
three pass either way** — the three that pass either way are the line-ending cases, which is the honest
result: that half was already correct and is now pinned rather than left to a default.

## ED10 — Language detection treated a backslash as a path separator, and the effect was not nil

**Files:** `src/web/editor/src/language.ts` lines 30–38; tests at `src/web/editor/test/language.test.ts` lines
42–57.

**What the code did before.** To pick a syntax highlighting language, the page took the part of the path after
the last separator, and counted both a forward slash and a backslash as separators.

**Why that is wrong.** Every path this page receives is a path on the remote Linux server, where a backslash is
an ordinary character inside a filename. The previous round left this alone and recorded the practical effect
as nil. It was not nil, in two shapes that this round records: a name whose extension sits *before* a
backslash, such as `app.js\notes`, was resolved on the wrong basename; and a name that merely *contains* a
recognised filename after a backslash, such as `odd\Dockerfile`, was claimed by that language although the
file is not one.

**What changed.** The basename is now taken after the last forward slash only, with a comment saying
Linux-and-only-Linux and giving the two shapes above.

**Test.** The existing case documented the intent that is now wrong, so it was split: the forward-slash half is
kept, and a new case covers `odd\Dockerfile` and `Dockerfile\odd` resolving to plain text, `back\slash.js`
resolving to JavaScript, `app.js\notes` resolving to plain text, and a Windows-shaped string treated as one
very odd Linux filename. With the old split restored, the new case fails on `odd\Dockerfile` and on the
Windows-shaped string; with the fix in place all twelve language cases pass.

## ED11 — Three comments described the text editor as its own kind of pane, which the specification does not define

**Files:** `src/editor/EditorController.h` lines 50–51 and 372–373; `src/editor/EditorFactory.h` lines 14–22.

**What the code did before.** Three comments spoke of an "editor pane" as a kind of pane in its own right. The
factory's header comment went furthest and *defined* the idea: "Per-pane EditorController factory … Each
editor pane must own its OWN controller".

**Why that is wrong.** The specification (`docs/SPEC.md` lines 682–684) says the text editor is one of the
viewer pane's handlers, not a separate kind of pane, and nothing in that section creates an "editor pane" as
a distinct concept. A comment that invents a pane type the rest of the system does not have sends the next
reader looking for something that is not there.

**What changed.** The two controller comments now name the viewer pane the controller serves. The factory
comment now says the text editor is one of the viewer pane's handlers, so "per-pane" means per *viewer* pane:
each viewer pane that reaches the text-editor handler owns its own controller, because a shared one would let
split panes clobber each other's path, revision and file state. The reason the comment gives is unchanged;
only the framing is corrected. Names that are merely shorthand rather than assertions were deliberately left
as they are — the QML file name, the factory's identifier and the library name are identifiers, not claims;
the bridge contract block describes the object the page talks to and says nothing about pane taxonomy; and
the test names that say "the pane" assert nothing about pane types.

**Test.** None. These are comments, so there is no behaviour to cover.

## ED12 — The editor's controller factory handed the user interface an object nobody owned

**Files:** `src/editor/EditorFactory.cpp` lines 16–23; `src/editor/EditorFactory.h` lines 32–34; test at
`src/editor/tests/tst_editorcontroller.cpp` lines 2924–2961.

**What the code did before.** The factory's `create()` method took an optional owner and passed it straight
through as the new controller's owner, so calling it without one produced a controller with no owner at all.

**Why that is wrong.** `create()` is callable from the user interface layer. When that layer calls a method
that returns an object with no owner, it takes ownership itself and may destroy the object at a moment
nothing in this code controls — while a live pane is still driving it. A C++ caller that took the default
would instead leak it. The previous round left this alone because the terminal factory had the identical
defect and consistency between the two was judged worth more than the fix. That is no longer the situation:
the terminal factory has now been fixed the same way, so this was the last one, and leaving it would have
made the two factories inconsistent in the unsafe direction. The symmetry argument now points at fixing it.

**What changed.** With no owner supplied, the factory itself becomes the controller's owner, with a comment
giving the reason and naming the terminal factory as the sibling with the same rule. The method's
documentation now states that behaviour.

One thing was checked rather than assumed: the pane identifier passed to `create()` can legitimately be
empty, and it has no bearing on ownership. It becomes the controller's crash-recovery key, read only when
building the snapshot path; empty means the pane has no stable key yet, so the snapshot is disabled rather
than shared unkeyed between panes, and the real identifier is pushed in later once the pane's layout
identifier settles. Nothing on that path touches object ownership.

**Test.** One new case, `anUnownedControllerIsKeptAliveByTheFactory`. No new build target and no user-interface
engine were needed: the existing controller test binary already links the library containing the factory, and
the defect is plain object ownership — the collection behaviour is only the consequence the interface layer
draws from a missing owner. The case asserts that a controller created with no owner is owned by the factory;
that this is still true after the real recovery identifier is supplied later; that a controller created *with*
an owner is still owned by that owner, so the fallback does not steal a real one; and, using pointers that
observe the objects' lifetimes from outside the factory's scope, that destroying the factory destroys the
orphan and **not** the one the pane owns. It was confirmed to fail against the pre-fix factory, where the two
owner pointers compared unequal.

## ED13 — A file with one bad byte in it was shown to the user as a wall of base64

**Files:** `src/remote/RpcTypes.h` lines 64–104 (new helper); `src/editor/EditorController.cpp` lines 598–618
and 1183–1196; tests at `src/editor/tests/tst_editorcontroller.cpp` lines 2965–3035 and 3037–3062.

**What the code did before.** When the daemon cannot decode a file as UTF-8 — its decoder is strict, so a
single invalid byte is enough — it answers with the file's exact bytes encoded as base64 and says so in the
reply (`remote/src/files.ts` lines 242–248). The controller handled that reply in two respects correctly: it
marked the buffer read-only, and it still showed the file rather than refusing to open it. But it passed the
**raw base64 text** to the page, undecoded. A user opening a file with one bad byte in it saw a wall of
base64 instead of their file.

**Why that is wrong.** A base64 reply is not a different kind of file. It is the same file, sent this way
because decoding it would have meant guessing. What the user should see is the file's bytes with the standard
replacement character wherever an invalid sequence is — which is exactly what the viewer side produced from
the identical reply: `ViewerModel::settleTextRead` decoded it. Two consumers of one wire format disagreed
about what it means, and the editor was the wrong one.

**What changed.** A new function `decodeFileContent()` in `src/remote/RpcTypes.h` takes a file-read result and
returns the text to show: the content unchanged for a UTF-8 reply, the base64-decoded text for a base64 reply,
and nothing at all **only** when a payload claiming to be base64 is not valid base64. Its body is the viewer's
decode unchanged — a strict base64 decode that refuses malformed input, then a UTF-8 conversion that turns
invalid sequences into replacement characters. It returns an optional value rather than reporting anything
itself, so each caller keeps its own error reporting.

Both of the controller's file-read replies now go through it, and both decode **before mutating anything** —
the open path decodes, then sets the revision, then derives read-only; the reload path decodes before touching
the revision, the unsaved flag or the crash-recovery snapshot. So a payload that cannot be decoded leaves the
buffer, its save guard and its recovery snapshot exactly as they were and settles the pane in its error state,
which is the honest answer for a server bug or a corrupted frame: nothing legible exists to show, and showing
the payload would be worse than showing nothing.

The instruction for this fix was to reuse the viewer's decode rather than write a second one, and that turned
out to be impossible: the viewer's text-read path, and the read-only text view that was its only consumer,
were **removed in this same round**, so the sibling to migrate no longer existed. The helper therefore has
exactly one caller today. That is a fact about right now and not evidence that nobody looked for a second one;
the function's own comment says so, at the owning area's request, so a later reader does not mistake its
location for proof of sharing. It sits with the reply shape it decodes, in a header both libraries already
include — there was nowhere else the two could ever have shared it, since the editor library deliberately does
not link the viewer library. No build-file change was needed. One other strict base64 decode, in the internal
URL handler, was deliberately left alone: it produces raw bytes for the browser to render (an image, a PDF)
and never text, so folding it in would mean decoding to text and re-encoding.

This does not disturb `ED9`, and the reason is structural rather than incidental: whether a buffer may be
written is derived from the reply's stated encoding, never from what the payload looks like
(`src/editor/EditorController.cpp` lines 630–633 and 1209–1212), that derivation runs before the content is
handed to the page, the save path refuses a read-only buffer outright (lines 741–744), and nothing clears that
flag except another load, which replaces the buffer. So a buffer that arrived base64 is never saved, and
`ED9`'s round-trip promise never has to hold for one. The new test asserts the save refusal directly, so this
is pinned rather than argued. It does not disturb the invalid-UTF-8 non-defect recorded below either: that
item is about what is written, and this fix changes only what is displayed.

**Test.** Two new cases. `aBase64ReadIsDecodedBeforeItReachesThePage` opens a file whose read answers base64
for bytes containing one invalid byte, and asserts the page is given the decoded text with a replacement
character and explicitly **not** the base64 string; that the buffer is read-only even though the file's
permissions allow writing; that saving reports an error and puts nothing on the wire; and then drives an
external change so the reload path answers base64 too, asserting that it is decoded as well and stays
read-only — both changed call sites in one case. `anUndecodableBase64ReadIsAReadFailure` answers with a base64
payload that is not valid base64 and asserts an honest read failure that does not half-apply: an error, no
content pushed to the page, the save guard still at the old revision, and nothing further on the wire. Both
were confirmed to fail against the pre-fix sources, the first with the raw base64 payload reaching the page.

### Investigated in this area and deliberately left alone

* **A claim that invalid UTF-8 could be corrupted on save was investigated and disproved.** The original
  suspicion was that the daemon might decode invalid bytes leniently, replacing each bad byte with a
  replacement character, so that saving the file back would write those replacements over the original bytes.
  The evidence was re-confirmed against the current daemon this round. The read path decodes in *strict* mode
  (`remote/src/files.ts` lines 125–129), so invalid bytes make the decode fail outright rather than being
  replaced; on that failure the reply switches to returning the file's exact bytes base64-encoded
  (`remote/src/files.ts` lines 244–248); and a reply in that form makes the client mark the buffer read-only
  (`src/editor/EditorController.cpp` lines 630–633), which its save path then refuses to write at all
  (lines 741–744). There is no lossy decode anywhere on the path, and the one non-UTF-8 outcome is a buffer
  the client already marks unwritable. Nothing to fix and nothing to test. The same read path is incidentally
  what makes `ED9` work end to end: it leaves a byte-order mark inside the content precisely so the mark
  reaches the client.

Verification for this area: the editor's C++ test suite finishes at **60 passing, 0 failing**, and the seven
new cases were confirmed to fail — and only those seven — when built against the pre-fix sources. The page's
TypeScript suite finishes at 31 passing, 0 failing, with a clean type check and a bundle that builds.

# VW — viewers

## VW1 — A file read that could not be sent answered before the caller learned its own request identifier, so the pane hung on "Loading…" forever

**Files (as the code stood when the defect was found — all of these were deleted later in this same round, see
VW15):** `src/viewers/ViewerModel.cpp` lines 131–192, `src/viewers/ViewerModel.h` lines 79–86. Root cause
observed in `src/remote/CodeharbordClient.cpp`; the victim is `src/qml/ViewerTextView.qml`.

**What the code did before.** Starting a read of a remote file returns a short string — a token — which is that
read's name. When the answer comes back the model emits a signal carrying the same token, and the pane compares
it against the token it is holding to decide "this answer is mine". The pane does this literally: it stores the
return value and ignores any reply whose token is not exactly that string.

The read is dispatched through the JSON-RPC client's send function. That function promises to invoke its callback
exactly once — but it does **not** promise to do so later. When it cannot put the request on the wire at all, it
fails the call *right there*, invoking the callback before it returns. It takes that branch when no transport is
bound, when the transport is closed, when the transport is not writable, or when the write came up short.

So in that case the whole chain ran inside the read function: the callback fired, the error signal was emitted,
and only *afterwards* did the read function return the token to its caller.

**Why that is wrong.** When the pane's error handler ran, its stored token was still empty, because the assignment
had not happened yet — the right-hand side had not returned. The pane therefore rejected its own error and stayed
in the loading state permanently: no content, no message, nothing to retry, and no further event will ever arrive
because the read is already settled and its bookkeeping entry already removed. The trigger is ordinary, not
exotic: any dropped SSH session leaves a live client object whose transport is gone, so opening a file in a viewer
pane after the connection dies wedges the pane. This is precisely the failure the function's own header comment
claimed was impossible — the comment only covered the "no client at all" case and missed the far more common
dead-transport case.

**What changed.** Every reply is now delivered through one deferred hop instead of straight out of the callback.
The reply handling was extracted into a private method (same logic: the cancellation check, the truncation check,
the decode and the signal), and the callback now only re-posts to it as a queued call with the model as the
context object — so if the model is destroyed first, the queued delivery is discarded rather than touching freed
memory. The header comment was rewritten to say *why* the deferral exists, naming the synchronous-failure branch.
Cancellation is not weakened: the handler still starts by removing the token from the in-flight set and bailing
out if it is not there, so a cancellation issued during the extra hop still suppresses the reply.

**Test.** New: `TstViewers::textReadOnADeadTransportStillReachesItsCaller`. It builds a client with no transport
bound — exactly the state that makes the send fail synchronously — and asserts that no error has been emitted at
the instant the read function returns (this is the assertion that fails on the old code); that the read is counted
as in flight at that moment; that the error then arrives carrying that read's own token and path; that no success
signal is emitted and the in-flight count returns to zero, so the deferral does not turn an unsendable read into a
permanent leak; and that a read cancelled before its deferred reply produces nothing at all.

**Note added later in this round.** The code this finding describes no longer exists. The read-only text view and
the model's whole text-read machinery — the read and cancel calls, the in-flight bookkeeping, the reply handler
this fix created and the two signals — were removed later in this same round; see VW15. This finding is **not**
retracted: the defect was real, a pane wedged on "Loading…" forever after a dropped SSH session, and it was really
fixed. What changed is that the code it was fixed in lost its last caller and was deleted, which is why the fix is
not in the tree at the end of the round. The durable half of the lesson does survive, in the JSON-RPC client's own
documentation of the branch where a call fails synchronously (`src/remote/CodeharbordClient.cpp`, header around
lines 96–120) — that is where the root cause always was, and every other caller still faces it.

## VW2 — `Makefile` opened as text; `makefile` opened as an unreadable binary

**Files:** `src/viewers/ViewerHandlerRegistry.cpp` lines 28–80 and 200–216;
`src/viewers/ViewerHandlerRegistry.h` lines 23–40.

**What the code did before.** The registry decides which kind of pane opens a file. For a remote file URL it
looked at the extension. Files with no extension and dotfiles have none, so a second table was matched against the
file's name. That table was written in the project's preferred capitalisation — `Makefile`, `LICENSE`, `README` —
and looked up case-**sensitively**. It was also consulted only when the extension was completely absent.

**Why that is wrong.** Three common failures. First, `makefile` and `MAKEFILE` — both spellings GNU make itself
searches for — missed the table entirely and fell through to the binary "download this" pane, hiding plain text
the user simply cannot read; same for `readme`, `license`, `changelog` and `GNUmakefile`. Second, the table was
missing names that are unambiguously text and turn up constantly: `Jenkinsfile`, `Vagrantfile`, `Rakefile`,
`Gemfile`, `Procfile`, `CODEOWNERS`, `.dockerignore`, `.npmrc`, `.nvmrc`, `.clang-format`, `.clang-tidy`,
`.gitmodules`, `.gitconfig`, `.vimrc`, `.pylintrc` and others. Third, because the name table only ran when there
was *no* extension, `Dockerfile.dev`, `Makefile.in`, `.env.local`, `.env.production` and `README.old` all had an
"extension" — `dev`, `in`, `local`, `production`, `old` — none of which means anything, so they went to the binary
pane too.

**What changed.** The duplicated "last path segment" logic was extracted into one helper, whose index variables
were also corrected from a 32-bit integer to the 64-bit type the underlying search actually returns. The table
moved into its own function, spelled in lower case and looked up after lower-casing, so matching is
case-insensitive, and the missing names were added. A stem rule was added: if the full name misses, the part
before the first dot is tried too, searching from the second character so a dotfile keeps its leading dot — that
is what makes `Dockerfile.dev`, `Makefile.in`, `.env.local` and `README.old` resolve as text. Finally, the
resolution order was changed so the extension is tried first and the name table is consulted only when the
extension resolved to "download". That is what keeps the stem rule safe: `archive.zip` and `photo.jpg` never reach
it, and `README.png` still opens in the image pane while `README.md` still renders as markdown. The trailing-slash
directory check still runs before either.

**Test.** The existing resolution table test was extended with two loops — eleven case-varied and newly added
names, six well-known-name-plus-suffix spellings — and four negative cases: `notmakefile.bin` stays a download, so
the stem rule cannot swallow arbitrary binaries; `README.png` stays an image and `README.md` stays markdown, so a
known extension still wins; and a path ending in `LICENSE/` stays a directory.

## VW3 — The extension table was missing formats a developer reads every day, and images the browser can display

**File:** `src/viewers/ViewerHandlerRegistry.cpp` lines 96–172.

**What the code did before.** The extension-to-pane map listed a generous but incomplete set of text extensions
and exactly six image extensions. Anything unlisted resolved to the binary "download this" pane.

**Why that is wrong.** Not a crash — a daily papercut with a wrong answer. Opening `main.qml`, `go.mod`,
`Cargo.lock`, `schema.proto`, `main.tf`, `deploy.nix`, `build.ninja`, `paper.tex`, `notes.rst`, `script.ps1`,
`lib.dart`, `mod.zig` or a systemd unit file showed a "binary file, download it" pane instead of the text that is
plainly there. For images, four more formats the embedded browser decodes natively could have been shown and were
not.

**What changed.** Around sixty text extensions were added, plus four image formats the browser really can decode.
Deliberately **not** added: three image formats the browser cannot decode, because routing them to the image pane
would produce a permanent broken-image icon, which is worse than offering a download. A comment at the image table
records that rule so the next person does not "fix" it by adding them.

**Test.** The existing table test was extended with a 28-extension text loop, the four new image extensions, and
two negative assertions pinning the decode-capability rule.

## VW4 — The internal-URL lookup accepted an address its own request handler refuses

**Files:** `src/viewers/InternalUrlSchemeHandler.cpp` lines 67–90; the twin check it disagreed with is at lines
188–196; documentation at `src/viewers/InternalUrlSchemeHandler.h` lines 56–61.

**What the code did before.** Remote files are shown to the embedded browser through opaque URLs of a fixed shape:
a custom scheme, then the fixed host name `file`, then a random identifier. The code that *answers* such a request
explicitly rejects any other host, with a comment explaining why: in a browser a different host is a different web
origin, and serving the same bytes under several origins hands a rendered page extra origins to play same-origin
games with.

The inverse function — which turns such a URL back into the remote file path, and is exposed to the user-interface
layer — checked only the *scheme*. It took the path component, stripped the leading slash, and looked the
identifier up. So a URL with the same scheme but a different host resolved happily to the real remote file.

**Why that is wrong.** A check applied in one place and missing in its twin. Nothing calls the inverse function
today, so there is no live exploit; the hazard is that the two halves of the subsystem disagree about whether a
given URL is legitimate, and the *accepting* half is the one exposed to the user-interface layer. Any future code
that validates a URL with it and then acts on the result would be validating against weaker rules than the handler
enforces.

**What changed.** The inverse function now rejects any URL of this scheme whose host is not `file`, matched
case-insensitively exactly as the handler matches it. Bare-identifier arguments, which the function also documents
support for, are unaffected. The documentation states the rule.

**Test.** The existing URL-mapping test was extended: two differing hosts must not resolve, and an upper-case
spelling of the correct host must still resolve, so the tightening does not accidentally become case-sensitive.

## VW5 — Wrong specification section cited on the external browser profile

**File:** `src/viewers/ViewerProfiles.cpp` line 54.

The comment read "arbitrary sites stay sandboxed (SPEC 7.2)". Verified against the document: section 7.2 is
"Arbitrary Websites" and describes the *content*; the requirement to use two separate browser profiles is 7.3,
"Browser Profiles". The comment is describing the two-profile split, so it pointed at the wrong section. Now cites
7.3, with a clause noting that 7.2 is what those sites *are*, so the neighbouring 7.2 citations still make sense.
The other citations in this area were judged individually and left alone — two of them genuinely concern untrusted
arbitrary web content and are correct as written.

## VW6 — Directory listings were sorted through a generic value type, paying four map lookups per comparison

**File:** `src/viewers/ViewerModel.cpp` lines 50–73 and 239–258.

**What the code did before.** The directory-listing reply handler built a list of generic key-value maps and then
sorted that list. The comparison function received two generic values, converted each to a map (a shared copy with
