an atomic reference-count round trip), then did two string-keyed tree lookups per side to get the entry kind, and
up to two more to get the name.

**Why that is wrong.** Not a correctness bug — the resulting order is right — but it performs four map
constructions and four to eight tree lookups per comparison, of which there are order *n* log *n*, on data that is
two plain strings. It is also harder to read than the thing it is doing. A directory with thousands of entries pays
for it on every listing.

**What changed.** A two-field structure and a free comparison function were added next to the file's existing
helpers. The handler now parses into a list of those, sorts it, and builds the generic list once afterwards. The
ordering rule is byte-for-byte the same — directories first, then case-insensitive name with a case-sensitive
tie-break — and the explanatory comment moved onto the comparison function where it belongs.

**Test.** New: `TstViewers::directoryListingIsSortedDirectoriesFirst`. There was **no unit coverage of the listing
order at all**; it was only exercised by the live gate, which is skipped by default. The new test feeds a
deliberately jumbled listing containing two directories, four files, a pair differing only in case, and an extra
field, and asserts the exact resulting name order, that each entry carries exactly the two keys the pane consumes
(the extra field must not leak through), and that an empty directory comes back as a successful empty listing
rather than an error.

## VW7 — An implicit narrowing in a public accessor

**File:** `src/viewers/ViewerModel.h` lines 99–102. A count was returned from a 32-bit integer function while the
underlying container's size is 64-bit. It cannot overflow in practice — it counts outstanding file reads — but it
is a silent narrowing that becomes a warning-as-error the day the project raises its warning level, and it is free
to state the intent. Wrapped in an explicit cast.

## VW8 — Nothing verified that the content type and character set actually reach the browser

**File:** `src/viewers/tests/tst_liveviewers.cpp` lines 160–166 and 662–699.

**What was missing.** The request handler picks a content type from the file's extension and, for textual types,
appends the UTF-8 character set — with a comment explaining that without it the browser falls back to a
locale-derived guess and a UTF-8 file with non-ASCII characters renders as unreadable mojibake. The unit suite
tests the type-picking function in isolation. The live gate loaded a real remote file into a real browser view and
read the text back out of the document — but only the text. Nothing anywhere checked that the header the handler
built was received and honoured.

**Why that is a gap.** The header could be dropped, overridden or sniffed away and every existing assertion would
still pass: the fixture file is pure ASCII, so a wrong character set is invisible in the rendered text, and a wrong
content type would still render as some kind of document. The garbled text this code exists to prevent would only
show up on a user's non-ASCII file.

**What changed.** The probe script the live test injects now also reports the document's content type and character
set, and the test asserts them. These are browser-side observations — the only place the delivered header is
visible. This runs in the live gate, which the coordinator executed: it passes.

## VW9 — An image or PDF a pane was still showing could stop working after 1024 other files were opened

**Files:** `src/viewers/InternalUrlSchemeHandler.h` lines 68–98; `src/viewers/InternalUrlSchemeHandler.cpp`
lines 67–86, 88–94, 107–135, 143–147 and 158–185; `src/viewers/ViewerModel.h` lines 54–71;
`src/viewers/ViewerModel.cpp` lines 141–149.

**What the code did before.** A remote file shown in the embedded browser gets an opaque internal address minted
for it, recorded in a table that maps the address's random identifier back to the real remote path. The table is
capped at 1024 entries; when it overflows, the least recently touched entry is dropped. An entry counts as touched
only when it is minted or when a request for it is answered.

**Why that is wrong.** A pane that finished loading its image never touches its entry again. The image, PDF and
binary panes each evaluate the internal address exactly once, in a binding that only re-runs when the pane's file
changes (`src/qml/ViewerImageView.qml` lines 22–24, `src/qml/ViewerPdfView.qml` lines 22–24). Nothing periodic
refreshes it. So once 1024 other files have been opened, that pane's address is evicted, and the next time the
browser needs the bytes again — a reload, a re-render, a restored view — the request is refused and the pane
breaks. This is an availability defect, not a theoretical one: the displayed document simply dies underneath the
user.

The obvious repair — treating "still on screen" as a touch — does not work here, because there is no moment at
which the pane says it is still on screen. Touching the entry when a request is answered comes too late: by the
time a reload's request arrives, the entry is already gone. Only an explicit "I am still holding this" statement
can express the requirement.

**What changed.** The table now supports pinning. `retain(id)` marks an address as in use and `release(id)` gives
it back; both are reference counted, so two panes showing the same file cannot unpin each other. Both are exposed
to the user-interface layer through the model as invokable methods. The rule for how a URL's identifier is
extracted — including the host check added in VW4 — was pulled into one helper, so minting, retaining and
releasing all apply the same rule instead of three copies of it.

The cap now governs the **unpinned** entries only: pinned entries are neither evicted nor counted towards the cap.
That choice is deliberate and the alternative is a bug. If pinned entries counted, then with the cap reached and
every entry pinned, the very next address minted would be the only unpinned one and would be evicted the instant
it was handed out — killing an address a pane had just been given, which is the same class of failure from the
other end. Eviction therefore walks *past* pinned entries rather than stopping at the first one it meets, so the
unpinned remainder stays bounded; a retain that is never released costs one entry, not the bound. `release()`
re-runs eviction, because the table may have been over the cap for the whole time the pin was held.

This changes who is responsible for releasing: the user-interface layer now has to. Until those panes are wired
up, nothing calls `retain()` and behaviour is identical to before, so there is no regression in the meantime. The
consumer half — connecting `ViewerImageView.qml`, `ViewerPdfView.qml` and `ViewerBinaryView.qml` — belongs to the
user-interface area and was routed there.

**Test.** New: `TstViewers::pinnedInternalUrlSurvivesEviction` (a pinned entry survives 20 files pushed through a
table capped at 4, keeps the *same* identifier, the unpinned remainder stays capped, and after release ordinary
traffic collects it) and `TstViewers::pinsAreCountedAndReleasable` (two holders, one release is not enough; a
freshly minted address is not instantly evicted by a table full of pins; a release with no matching retain is a
no-op rather than an underflow; retaining an unknown identifier or a wrong-host URL returns false and pins
nothing). With the old eviction policy re-injected, both fail:

```
FAIL!  : TstViewers::pinnedInternalUrlSurvivesEviction() Compared values are not the same
   Actual   (map.fileUrlFor(pinned)): Invalid URL:       Expected (shown) : file:///p/shown.png
FAIL!  : TstViewers::pinsAreCountedAndReleasable() Compared values are not the same
   Actual   (map.fileUrlFor(url)): Invalid URL:          Expected (shared): file:///p/shared.png
```

## VW10 — An oversized image or PDF failed with a blank page and no reason, because the browser interface cannot carry one

**Files:** `src/viewers/InternalUrlSchemeHandler.h` lines 159–204; `src/viewers/InternalUrlSchemeHandler.cpp`
lines 235–267 and 310–460; `src/viewers/ViewerProfiles.h` lines 49–56 and `ViewerProfiles.cpp` lines 88–93;
`src/viewers/ViewerModel.h` lines 162–177 and `ViewerModel.cpp` lines 110–126.

**What the code did before.** Files served to the embedded browser are capped at 8 megabytes read inline. When a
file is over the cap — or the request is refused for any of eight other reasons — the handler fails the browser's
request. The only way to fail such a request is a function that takes one value from a small fixed list of coarse
browser error codes and **no text at all**. The pane therefore showed a failed page with nothing on it. The text
pane, which does not go through this path, has always said "file is too large to display inline".

**Why the previous round could not fix it, and why that reasoning was right as far as it went.** The earlier pass
recorded this as unfixable because the interface genuinely cannot carry a reason, and that part is correct: there
is no text parameter anywhere on the failure call. The step it did not take was to stop trying to send the reason
*through* that interface.

Replying with a synthesised explanation document instead of failing was considered again this pass and rejected
for a concrete reason: `src/qml/ViewerBinaryView.qml` lines 105–124 performs downloads by navigating a hidden
browser view at the very same internal address. A successful reply carrying a plain-text "the file is too large"
body would therefore be **saved to disk as the file the user asked to download**.

**What changed.** The reason now travels out of band, alongside the failure rather than inside it. The handler
gained a typed `Failure` enumeration with one value per refusal reason (wrong method, unknown host, unknown or
evicted identifier, identifier that is not a remote file, empty path, no client, read error, too large,
undecodable payload), a pure static `failureMessage()` that turns a reason plus an optional server detail into a
sentence, and a `requestFailed(internalUrl, reason, message)` signal. All nine failure sites now emit it
immediately before failing the request, routed through one small local helper so no site can be forgotten. The
post-reply failure path guards the handler with a weak pointer, because that reply can land after the browser
profile owning the handler has been torn down.

The oversized case uses **the exact wording the text pane already uses** — "file is too large to display inline" —
so the one 8-megabyte rule reads identically in every pane.

To get that signal to the interface layer, the profiles object gained an accessor that creates the handler
independently of the browser profile, so the connection can be made before any profile is materialised; installing
the handler into the profile remains the profile's own job. The model forwards the signal as
`internalResourceError(internalUrl, message)`, holding the connection so that re-pointing the model at a different
profile drops the old forwarding instead of reporting twice.

The consuming half — having the image, PDF and binary panes listen for this and match the address against the one
they are displaying, the way `ViewerPane.qml` already matches paths — is in the user-interface area and was routed
there.

**Test.** New: `TstViewers::refusalMessagesExplainThemselves` (every reason has a non-empty and *distinct*
sentence; the too-large wording is pinned identical to the text pane's; a server-supplied detail is quoted; an
empty detail leaves no dangling colon) and `TstViewers::internalRequestFailuresReachQml` (the handler's report
arrives at the model's forwarded signal with address and message intact, and re-pointing the model does not leave
the old handler forwarding as well). With the pre-fix behaviour re-injected — the message collapsed to the single
indistinguishable value the failure call actually conveys, and the forwarding removed — both fail:

```
FAIL!  : TstViewers::refusalMessagesExplainThemselves() Compared values are not the same
   Actual   (seen.size()): 1        Expected (all.size()) : 9
FAIL!  : TstViewers::internalRequestFailuresReachQml() Compared values are not the same
   Actual   (failures.size()): 0    Expected (1)          : 1
```

## VW11 — An SVG rendered unstyled, because the security policy for active content also blocked its own style block

**File:** `src/viewers/InternalUrlSchemeHandler.cpp` lines 269–308 and 457; declaration at
`src/viewers/InternalUrlSchemeHandler.h` lines 188–196.

**What the code did before.** Content the browser renders as a document that could execute script — HTML, XML and
anything XML-based, which includes SVG — was served under the content-security policy `default-src 'none';
sandbox`. `default-src` is the fallback for every resource category the policy does not name explicitly, and one
of those categories is styles.

**Why that is wrong.** An SVG carries its own presentation: a `<style>` block, or `style="…"` attributes on its
shapes. Both are styles, both fell under `default-src 'none'`, and both were blocked. The file rendered with its
styling stripped and nothing on screen to say why. SVG is the most common file in this category that a user opens
here, so the policy's most visible effect was breaking legitimate rendering.

The previous round recorded this as a deliberate trade-off — loosening the policy would weaken the defence against
a document smuggling data out. That reasoning does not survive inspection, which is why it is fixed now rather
than kept. Allowing inline CSS costs nothing, for two reasons that can be checked rather than asserted. First,
inline CSS cannot execute script; script remains covered by `default-src 'none'`, and `sandbox` independently puts
the document in an opaque origin with no scripts and no forms. Second, every channel through which CSS could send
bytes anywhere — `background-image: url()`, `@import`, a downloaded font — is a **resource load**, and every one
of those categories is still unnamed by the policy and therefore still falls back to `default-src 'none'` and is
refused. Inline CSS with no ability to fetch anything has no outbound channel at all. The exfiltration defence is
unchanged.

**What changed.** The policy is now `default-src 'none'; style-src 'unsafe-inline'; sandbox`. `style-src
'unsafe-inline'` permits styles written inside the document and nothing else — in particular it does not permit
loading a stylesheet from anywhere. Building the response headers was also lifted out of the reply lambda into a
static function, which was necessary rather than cosmetic: the browser's request object cannot be constructed in a
unit test, so a free function is the only seam at which these security headers can be checked at all.

**Test.** New: `TstViewers::responseHeadersLetSvgStyleItself`. An SVG reply's policy must contain `style-src
'unsafe-inline'` **and** still contain `default-src 'none'` and `sandbox`, and must contain no `script-src` and no
`'unsafe-eval'`; a PNG reply gets no policy at all, since it is not rendered as a document; both carry the
no-sniffing header. With the old policy re-injected the test fails:

```
FAIL!  : TstViewers::responseHeadersLetSvgStyleItself()
   'csp.contains(QByteArrayLiteral("style-src 'unsafe-inline'"))' returned FALSE.
   (CSP blocks the SVG's own styles: default-src 'none'; sandbox)
```

## VW12 — A resolution outcome that no code path could ever produce, kept alive by circular reasoning

**Files:** `src/viewers/ViewerHandlerRegistry.h` lines 8–31; `src/viewers/ViewerModel.cpp` lines 40–49;
`src/viewers/tests/tst_viewers.cpp` line 734.

**What the code did before.** The registry that decides which pane opens a URL returns one value from a fixed list
of outcomes. One of them, "open externally", was never returned by any path in the registry. The switch that turns
an outcome into a pane kind carried a case for it anyway.

**Why that is wrong, and why the previous round's reason for keeping it does not hold.** The earlier pass checked
the specification, found the value listed there, and concluded it was a specified placeholder rather than dead
code. That reasoning is circular: the specification listed the value because the code had it. The specification
section describing resolution was corrected this session to state the algorithm explicitly — resolution is
browser-first, web navigation is the default, and a handler only takes a resource when it positively claims it —
in four steps (`docs/SPEC.md` lines 616–625). Those four steps between them produce exactly direct web navigation,
the internal HTML renderer, the text editor, the image viewer, the PDF viewer, the directory viewer and download,
plus an error outcome for an unknown scheme (`ViewerHandlerRegistry.cpp` line 218). Nothing produces "open
externally", and under a browser-first algorithm nothing can: handing a file to the desktop's own application is
an **action the download/metadata pane offers** — the specification's own table for that pane reads "Binary |
metadata and download/open actions" — taken *after* resolution has already settled on download. It is not
something a URL can resolve *to*.

**What changed.** The value was removed from the enumeration, with a comment recording why it is absent so it is
not put back. The switch that maps outcomes to panes dropped its case, and its comment now states the rule it
implements: a resource no handler claims becomes a download/metadata view, never a text buffer. A stale comment in
the test file was corrected.

One documentation defect was found and left to the documentation area: `docs/SPEC.md` line 637 still lists the
removed value among that section's possible resolution types. It was reported rather than edited here.

**Test.** Enforced by the compiler instead of by an assertion, deliberately. The switch over resolution outcomes
has no default case, so with `-Wswitch` any outcome that exists but is unhandled — or any use of a removed one —
is a compile error. That is a stronger guarantee than a runtime check and needs no new test. The existing
`TstViewers::viewKindStringsMatchQmlContract` continues to pin that download and error both map onto the binary
pane.

## VW13 — The request handler never said whether it supported partial reads, inviting a consumer to assume it did

**File:** `src/viewers/InternalUrlSchemeHandler.cpp` lines 268–276.

**What the code did before.** Every reply carried the whole file and said nothing about byte ranges. A client that
sends a `Range` header was simply given the complete document, which is the correct answer for a server without
range support — but the reply gave no indication that ranges were unsupported, so a range-capable consumer had no
way to know it should not try to seek.

**What changed.** Every reply now carries `Accept-Ranges: none`, which is the standard way to state exactly that.
This is accurate and costs nothing. It is not full range support — see the "left alone" note below for why range
support itself cannot be added at this interface.

**Test.** `TstViewers::responseHeadersLetSvgStyleItself` asserts `Accept-Ranges: none` on both an active-content
reply and an inert one (`tst_viewers.cpp` around lines 1409–1414). The recorded pre-fix failure for that test is
its first assertion, the content-security-policy one quoted under VW11, because with the pre-fix sources the whole
header-building function did not exist; the range assertion was never reached.

## VW14 — A cancelled file read held its payload alive for an extra turn of the event loop

**Files:** `src/viewers/ViewerModel.cpp` lines 205–213; comment at
`src/viewers/InternalUrlSchemeHandler.cpp` lines 389–398.

**What the code did before.** When a pane cancels a read that is already on the wire, the answer still arrives —
there is no way to call it off, see the "left alone" note below. On arrival the model copied the reply's payload
into a queued call (the deferred hop added in VW1) and only checked the cancellation set on the far side of that
hop, discarding the payload there. So a cancelled read's body — up to the 8-megabyte inline cap — stayed alive for
an extra turn of the event loop for no purpose.

**What changed.** The reply callback now checks the in-flight set *before* posting the queued call, so a read
already cancelled releases its payload immediately instead of one turn later. The check on the far side is kept
and remains the authoritative one, because a cancellation can also arrive *during* that turn — so VW1's deferral
guarantee is untouched. In the request handler, the equivalent early return for a request the browser has already
abandoned was documented to say exactly what it is doing and why, so the next reader does not have to work it out
again.

**Test.** None, and this is deliberate rather than an omission: the change alters *when* a buffer is released, not
any observable signal, count or ordering. The in-flight read count is already zero after a cancellation and no
signal is emitted either way, so there is nothing at this seam a test could assert. The four existing cancellation
tests (`cancelTextFileDropsOnlyItsOwnRead`, `cancelOfOneReadOfAPathLeavesTheOtherAlone`,
`aRefreshingPaneCancelsItsOwnEarlierReadOfOnePath`, `textReadBookkeepingDoesNotLeak`) pass unchanged, which is
what pins that the earlier exit did not weaken cancellation.

## VW15 — A read-only text view that nothing could ever open, and the whole model machinery behind it, removed

**Files:** `src/qml/ViewerTextView.qml` (deleted, 141 lines); `src/qml/CMakeLists.txt` line 36;
`src/qml/ViewerImageView.qml`, `src/qml/ViewerPdfView.qml`, `src/qml/ViewerDirectoryView.qml` lines 49–69,
`src/qml/RemotePath.js` line 10; `src/viewers/ViewerModel.h` and `.cpp`; `src/viewers/InternalUrlSchemeHandler.h`
and `.cpp` (two comments); `src/viewers/tests/tst_viewers.cpp`; `src/viewers/tests/tst_liveviewers.cpp`.

**What the code did before.** Alongside the code editor, this area carried a second, simpler pane for showing a
text file read-only, with its own read path in the model: a call to start a read, a call to cancel one, in-flight
bookkeeping keyed by a token, a reply handler, and a pair of signals for success and failure. The pane that
chooses a view for a resolved file reached this view only through its `default:` branch — the case for a
resolution outcome none of the explicit branches matched.

**Why that is wrong.** That `default:` branch was itself a defect, corrected in the user-interface area this
round: an unrecognised resource must stay with the browser and land on the metadata/download view, never fall
into a text handler. Once it was corrected, nothing in production could reach the read-only text view at all. It
has no *positive* match either, because the two outcomes that would be its natural input, plain text and markdown,
are a positive match for the code editor's handler (`src/qml/ViewerPane.qml` lines 592–594). Giving it one would
mean inventing a resolution outcome beyond the specification's vocabulary, which is a product decision, and the
specification as corrected is explicit the other way: the pane is a browser that delegates, the code editor is
*the* text handler, and no capability may assume it owns the pane. A second read-only text pane is exactly the
competing pane type that framing forbids. "Unreachable today" is not a reason to keep code under this round's
bar — the same bar already deleted a dead channel-kind enumeration and the dead resolution outcome in VW12.

**What changed.** The view and its whole stack were removed in one change: the QML file and its module entry, two
stale comment clauses in the image and PDF views, a reworded comment in the directory view (the load-bearing
conclusion — that a reply is matched against what *this* view asked for, from state set when the request went
out — survives; only the token comparison, which no longer has anything to refer to, is gone), a stale entry in a
helper script's caller list, the live-gate case and its fixture, and on the model side the read call, the cancel
call, the in-flight bookkeeping, the reply handler, the two signals, their members and the includes that became
unused. Two comments in the request handler that cited the removed methods now cite the editor's own size limit
and "the text handler" instead. The model's class comment now states outright that it has no text-read operation
and why, pointing at this finding, so someone searching for the removed method name has a thread to pull.

Before deciding, the two paths that might have needed the view were checked against the source rather than
assumed, and neither is lost. A text file over the 8-megabyte cap: the editor marks the buffer read-only on a
truncated read and **still shows the head of the file** (`src/editor/EditorController.cpp` lines 609–628), with a
comment saying that failing instead "would leave the pane blank for a file we can legitimately display" — the
removed view showed no content at all, just the sentence "file is too large to display inline". The content
survives; that sentence does not, on this path, and it is still used verbatim where it is genuinely needed, for
the image, PDF and binary panes, which cannot show a prefix of anything (see VW10). A file the server marks
read-only because strict UTF-8 decoding failed: the server switches the payload to base64
(`remote/src/files.ts` lines 242–248) and the editor marks the buffer read-only and still delivers the content
(`EditorController.cpp` lines 611–616), so it is a viewable read-only buffer, not a download. One real defect was
found during that check — the editor delivered the base64 text undecoded, which the removed model path had
decoded — and it was reported and fixed in the editor area, not here.

**This deletes the subject of VW1, which stands.** VW1 is not retracted. It was a real defect and it was really
fixed; the reason its fix is not in the tree at the end of the round is that the code holding it lost its last
caller and was deleted here. The durable half of that lesson survives where the root cause always was: the
JSON-RPC client's own note that a call can fail synchronously, which every other caller still faces.

One consequence of this removal is worth recording. The early release of a cancelled read's payload in VW14 had
two halves, and the model-side half went with the removed read call. The request-handler half is untouched and
still stands — and it is the half that matters, because that is the path carrying multi-megabyte images.

**Test.** Eight unit tests covering the removed read path were deleted with it, and so was the live-gate case:
tests for deleted code are not coverage. The one test that covered both the removed path and surviving ones,
`viewerModelWithoutClientReportsErrors`, was rewritten to cover the two surviving methods only. The unit suite is
25 passing, 0 failing, 0 skipped — 33 before this change, minus the 8 deleted — so the five tests added for the
other findings above are all still present and green. The live-gate file was verified to compile clean but, per
this round's instructions, not run. The QML linter reports no remaining reference to the deleted type in the three
touched QML files.

### Investigated in this area and deliberately left alone

1. **Byte-range support in the request handler — refused, on a technical ground rather than an effort one.** The
   *reading* half is implementable: the browser's request object exposes the request headers, so a `Range` header
   is readable, and the file service does take an offset and a length. The *replying* half is not. The entire
   public reply surface is "reply with this content type and this data", "fail with this error code", "redirect",
   and "add these headers" — there is **no way to set a status code**. A partial reply would therefore go out
   under the browser's implicit `200 OK`, meaning bytes 1000–1999 of a file would be delivered labelled as the
   complete file. That is strictly worse than today, where ignoring the `Range` header and returning the whole
   body under `200` is the correct answer for a server that does not support ranges. Adding this would require a
   status-carrying reply in the browser framework itself — upstream API, not code in this project. The honest part
   that *is* available was taken and is recorded above as VW13: the handler now declares that it does not support
   ranges.
2. **Cancelling a read that is already on the wire — the remaining half needs a wire-protocol operation that does
   not exist.** When the browser abandons a request, the read it started keeps running and its payload still
   crosses the SSH channel before being thrown away. There is no use-after-free and no reply to a dead request;
   the cost is bandwidth and memory. Two pieces are missing, both outside this area, and both are named precisely
   here so the work is well defined. First, the wire protocol needs a cancellation notification carrying the
   identifier of a still-pending request, plus a daemon-side contract that the file-read handler checks it,
   abandons the read, and answers the original identifier with a cancellation error instead of the payload —
   nothing in `src/remote/RpcTypes.h` or `remote/src/` has this. Second, the JSON-RPC client has no cancel entry
   point at all; its header documents only that a call's callback fires exactly once, and offers no way to retire
   a pending identifier. A cancel would need a method that both sends the notification and drops the local pending
   entry, and that client is another area. The in-area part — releasing a cancelled read's payload a turn earlier
   — was done and is recorded above as VW14; it is untestable at that seam, for the reason given there.
3. **Two model methods still report their "no client" failure synchronously**, unlike the read in VW1. The
   hypothesis that this is VW1 again was tested this pass and is disproved, so they are unchanged. VW1's defect
   was that the reply's *identity* — a token minted inside the call — had not been handed to the caller when the
   reply fired. Directory listing and path resolution are identified by **path**, which the caller composed before
   it called, so a synchronous reply is matched correctly. All three call sites were re-read this session and each
   sets its matching state *before* the call: `src/qml/ViewerDirectoryView.qml` lines 90–96 (handlers at 103–114
   match on the path); `src/qml/ViewerPane.qml` lines 264–292, which assigns the probe path before listing and
   matches it in the handler at 304–309, with nothing running after the call in the outer frame; and
   `src/qml/ViewerPane.qml` lines 235–246, which assigns its check state before resolving and matches it at
   317–324. The same holds for the dead-transport failure, which is the trigger that actually made VW1 bite: the
   fault has the same shape and both callers survive it. The asymmetry is pinned by an existing test —
   `TstViewers::viewerModelWithoutClientReportsErrors` asserts in one place that the text error is *deferred* and
   that the directory error is *synchronous* — so deferring these two would be churn that also breaks a pinned
   contract, for no defect.

# QM — the user interface layer

## QM1 — A terminal pane could attach to the previous Dev Session's shell, or strand itself forever

**File:** `src/qml/TerminalPaneView.qml` lines 88–106, 393–412 and 464–487.

**What the code did before.** Each terminal pane has to find out which remote tmux session it owns. That is a
question for the server, so the pane asks and waits; the answer comes back on a signal carrying only two things —
which controller it is for, and the session name. The pane recorded, in one variable, a string identifying the
"slot" it had asked about, and when an answer arrived it compared that string against the slot it currently
occupies, ignoring the answer if they differed.

Separately, if the user pointed the pane somewhere else while a question was still travelling, the retarget
routine cleared the "a question is outstanding" flag and immediately asked a **second** question.

**Why that is wrong.** Two bugs fall out of the combination.

The first is the wrong shell. Because the retarget asked the new question straight away, it also overwrote the
recorded slot with the NEW one. The old answer — still in flight, describing the OLD Dev Session's shell — then
arrived, found the recorded slot equal to the pane's current slot, passed the check, and was adopted. The pane
attached to the shell belonging to the Dev Session the user had just left, so two panes shared one remote shell:
precisely the failure the whole resolution mechanism exists to prevent. Reproduced by clicking one Dev Session in
the sidebar and another within the round-trip window, a few hundred milliseconds on a normal link.

The second is a permanent stall. In the cases where the mismatch check *did* fire, the handler returned **without
clearing the outstanding flag**. The attach routine refuses to do anything while that flag is set, so the pane
never asked again and never attached — and its own Retry button, which just calls the attach routine, could not
help either.

**What changed.** The pane now keeps **exactly one lookup outstanding**. A retarget no longer clears the flag and
no longer starts a second lookup; it sets a new "the answer coming back is about a slot we have left" marker. The
answer handler now clears the outstanding flag FIRST, so nothing can be stranded, and if the answer is stale — by
the new marker or by the existing slot comparison — it discards it and calls the attach routine to ask the
question the pane actually has now. Because only one question is ever in flight, an answer that carries no slot of
its own is unambiguous.

**Test.** New: `TstPaneIdentity::aTerminalPaneNeverAdoptsTheAnswerToASupersededLookup`. It drives a real terminal
pane against a new substitute factory, holds a lookup open, retargets the pane, delivers the old answer, and
asserts: no second lookup was started while the first was open; the old target was not adopted and no terminal
attached to it; the pane asked again for the new slot; and the new answer is adopted and attached.

## QM2 — A retargeted terminal pane could open its shell in the previous checkout

**File:** `src/qml/TerminalRegion.qml` lines 349–366.

**What the code did before.** When the region's Dev Session changes it pushes two values onto each pane: the
session identifier and the directory the shell should start in. It assigned the session identifier first and the
directory second.

**Why that is wrong.** Assigning the session identifier is what makes a pane drop its terminal and re-attach, and
the working directory only ever reaches the server as part of "create a tmux session in this directory", which
tmux honours **only when it creates the session**. So a pane that sees the new session identifier while still
holding the old directory creates its shell in the previous project's checkout, and the directory arriving one
line later can no longer move it. The main window documents this at length and pushes the pair the other way
round, which is what has been masking it — the region's own copy of the same push had the order backwards, so any
future caller that did not go through the main window first would hit it.

**What changed.** The two assignments were swapped so the directory is set first, with a comment pointing at the
main window's identical rule.

**Test.** None added: with the host's ordering in place the observable behaviour is unchanged, so a test would
assert internal ordering rather than a user-facing contract.

## QM3 — The viewer address bar kept the fragment the user typed, and then showed the wrong path after the first fix

**File:** `src/qml/ViewerPane.qml` lines 76–85 and 326–349.

**What the code did before.** The address bar shows the pane's current address. Because the field is editable the
pane pushes text into it rather than binding it, and it deliberately never pushed while the field had keyboard
focus, so it could not overwrite what the user was typing.

**Why that is wrong.** Pressing Enter in a text field does not take the keyboard away from it. A user typing a
name **relative** to the directory the pane is showing — `notes.md` in a listing of `/srv/repos/app/` — leaves the
field focused, so the pane opened `/srv/repos/app/notes.md` while the bar still read `notes.md`. The bar then
claimed the pane was showing a file with no directory, and the next Enter resolved that leftover fragment against
whatever directory the pane had moved to.

**What changed, and the second defect found during integration.** The push was extracted into a helper. A change
of the displayed path still pushes only when the field is unfocused (unchanged behaviour), but a change of the
pane's own address — which only ever happens because the pane navigated, i.e. because the user asked it to — now
pushes unconditionally.

The first version of that helper was wrong, and the coordinator's integration run caught it. It pushed a *bound*
property. That property is bound, through one intermediate, to the pane's address — and the new handler runs from
the very notification that refreshes those bindings. Whether they have already been refreshed when a given handler
runs is an ordering detail of the engine, and here they had not been: the handler read the previous value out of
the still-stale binding, so the bar showed the directory the pane had just left rather than the file it had just
opened. This is the same hazard a sibling file avoids by writing the equivalent value as a function instead of a
bound property. The final fix computes the pushed value with a small function — the same rule the bound property
states, applied to the freshly assigned address — instead of reading it off a binding. No assertion was changed.

**Test.** New: `TstPaneIdentity::theAddressBarShowsWhatARelativeNameResolvedTo`. It opens a directory, types a bare
file name, and asserts the pane opened the resolved absolute path AND that the still-focused field now reads that
same absolute path. This test failed in the coordinator's integration run, which is how the binding defect above
was found; it passes now.

## QM4 — Long names ran off the edge of the sidebar and covered the button that creates a Dev Session

**Files:** `src/qml/SessionRow.qml` lines 41–52 and 176–203; `src/qml/GroupHeader.qml` lines 36–50 and 87–108.

**What the code did before.** The Dev Session name, its repository subtitle and the group name are all drawn with
"cut this off with an ellipsis if it does not fit".

**Why that is wrong.** Eliding does nothing to a text item that has no width of its own, and none of these had
one: they sit inside layout containers that size themselves *to* their children rather than the other way round.
So the text grew to its full natural width and painted straight over the edge of the panel — and in the group
header, over the "collapsed" tag and over the "+" button that adds a Dev Session to that group, which is the only
route into the product's actual work. Every one of these strings comes off a remote server (session names,
repository paths), so lengths well past a 300-pixel sidebar are ordinary rather than adversarial.

**What changed.** Each label was given an explicit width derived from the space actually available: the session row
measures from its content row's own width, and the group header measures against a new value that is the left edge
of the "+" button when it is showing and the header's right edge when it is not. Both delegates also got a constant
natural width in place of the content-derived one, so that the new "label sized from the row" direction cannot
close into a circular binding if either is ever built without a parent, which is a documented fallback path.

**Test.** New: `TstSidebar::longNamesAreElidedInsteadOfOverflowingTheSidebar`. It builds a group and a session with
120- and 160-character names, then asserts that every visible text item inside the row and the header ends inside
the panel, and that the group name stops before its own add-session button. Asserted as geometry, because eliding
is the mechanism but staying inside the panel is the requirement.

## QM5 — The window title could push the Dev Session name under the window buttons

**File:** `src/qml/AppTitleBar.qml` lines 177–191 and 201–217.

**What the code did before.** The self-drawn title bar puts the application title and the current Dev Session side
by side in a row whose width stops short of the minimise, maximise and close buttons. Each of the two labels
measured itself against the **whole** row, as if the other were not there.

**Why that is wrong.** A row does not clip its children to its own width. With a long application title and a long
session name, the two together exceed the row and the session name is drawn on top of the window buttons — and on a
frameless window those buttons are the only pointer route to closing it.

**What changed.** The title now takes at most half the row once there is a session label beside it, and the session
label subtracts the title label's actual width from what it asks for.

**Test.** None added: an existing test pins the two labels' text, and a geometry assertion here would need a
font-dependent fixture to be meaningful.

## QM6 — The session row's context menu reached around the sidebar's own contract

**Files:** `src/qml/SessionRow.qml` lines 241–257 and 302–305; `src/qml/SessionsSidebar.qml` lines 354–377.

**What the code did before.** The sidebar states as a contract that it "owns every application call", and the group
header spells out why it routes its collapse through the sidebar rather than calling the application object itself:
a delegate is documented to stay usable with no host, and a bare lookup of the application object would break that
with a reference error. Every guard in the session row is written for the same "host may be null" contract — and
then its right-click menu (Rename, Duplicate, Move to top, Delete) called the application object directly.

**Why that is wrong.** Two twins that should agree and do not. The row's four mutating actions were the one place
in the panel that reached around the sidebar, so a standalone row — the configuration every other line of the file
is written to support — throws the moment its menu is used. It was also completely untested: nothing in the suite
ever opened that menu, so the entries could have been dead and no test would have noticed.

**What changed.** Four thin forwarding functions were added to the sidebar, beside the existing functions that
already own the application calls, and the four menu entries and the rename dialog now go through the host. Same
requests, same arguments, one owner.

**Test.** New: `TstSidebar::rowMenuActionsReachTheWorkspace`. It triggers Duplicate, Move to top and Delete on a row
and accepts the rename dialog, then asserts the exact four workspace calls reached the substitute application
object.

## QM7 — A pane header kept controls that had been removed from its action list

**File:** `src/qml/AppPaneHeader.qml` lines 68–94.

**What the code did before.** A caller hands the header a list of buttons; the header re-parents each one into its
layout row so it is drawn. The adoption loop only ever ADDED.

**Why that is wrong.** A list entry keeps the header as its parent whatever the list later says, so re-assigning
the list with a shorter or different one left the dropped controls sitting in the row — still drawn, still
clickable, still wired to whatever they were wired to. No caller re-assigns today, which is exactly what makes it a
trap: the property is public, documented as re-assignable, and the failure is silent.

**What changed.** The adoption pass now also un-parents anything in the layout row that the new list does not name.

**Test.** None added: no production caller re-assigns the list, so a test would have to construct the situation
purely to assert it.

## QM8 — A dead model-role requirement and a stale comment in the sidebar

**File:** `src/qml/SessionsSidebar.qml` line 11 and the group delegate. The group delegate declared a required
property, which is a contract with the model saying "this delegate cannot be built without this role", and never
read it. The file's header comment listed that role among the ones the sidebar consumes. Keeping a required
property that is never read makes the contract wider than it really is, and the comment then documents a dependency
that does not exist. Both removed. (An unrelated local variable with the same name elsewhere in the file is a
different thing and stays.)

## QM9 — Crash recovery was only half built: the user was never offered their unsaved work back

**Files:** `src/qml/EditorPaneView.qml` lines 239–374 and 394–405. Existing counterpart:
`src/editor/EditorController.cpp` line 683 and `src/editor/EditorController.h` line 142.

*Routed to this area from the editor reviewer, who owns the C++ half and correctly identified the missing part as a
user-interface decision.*

**What the code did before.** While the user types, the editor controller periodically writes a snapshot of the
unsaved buffer to a per-pane file on the server. When the same file is opened again it reads that snapshot back and
compares it with the file's current contents; a snapshot that differs means the application died holding unsaved
work, and the controller emits a signal carrying the recovered text. **Nothing anywhere connected to that signal.**
A search across the whole client found only the declaration, the emit, and two assertions in the controller's own
test. The editor pane did not handle it.

**Why that is wrong.** It is the entire point of the specification's unsaved-recovery section. The whole mechanism
ran — including an extra status request and a read on every single file open — and then the recovered text was
dropped on the floor. The user was never told their work had survived and had no way to get it back. Storing and
finding the snapshot without ever offering it is strictly worse than not having the feature: it costs two round
trips per file open and delivers nothing.

**What changed.** The missing user-interface half was added, using the module's own dialog and theming components
and no new visual vocabulary:

* The pane now tracks the revision the page was loaded at, taken from the same signal the page reads its content
  from.
* The recovery signal is handled: the recovered text is held, and a modal dialog titled "Unsaved changes were
  recovered" explains in plain words that unsaved changes to this file are not in the file on the server, and what
  each button does. It offers **Restore** and **Discard**.
* The offer is deliberately **withheld until the page actually holds the file**. An empty loaded revision means the
  controller is still holding the content back until the page reports itself ready; offering earlier would let a
  restore push text at a page with no handler attached, losing the very work being offered.
* **Restore** re-emits the content-loaded signal with the recovered text — the only route content has into the
  editor page, since the JavaScript bridge contract is fixed — using the revision the page already holds, so the
  next save's conflict guard is unchanged. It then reports the content upward, so the host knows the buffer no
  longer matches the server: that is what stops an external change from silently reloading over the restored work,
  and what keeps the snapshot alive until the user actually saves.
* **Discard** leaves the file as the server has it and leaves the snapshot alone; the controller already discards it
  on the next successful save.
* The dialog cannot be dismissed with the Escape key or by clicking outside it. Every other dialog in this module
  asks about something the user can redo; this one asks about work that exists in exactly one place.
* Switching the pane to another file clears the pending offer and the revision, so a previous file's unsaved work
  can never be restored into the new one.

Whether the fix belonged in the C++ controller instead was checked and rejected: the controller already publishes
everything needed — the recovered text on the signal, the revision on the load signal, and a method to mark the
buffer as differing from the server — and what was missing was purely the choice put in front of the user. No C++
was changed.

One mechanism is worth recording: restoring emits a C++-declared signal from the user-interface layer. That this is
possible was verified with an isolated probe rather than assumed.

**Tests.** Two new cases in `tst_paneidentity.cpp`, driven by a new substitute controller and no real editor page.
`theEditorOffersToRestoreARecoveredBuffer` asserts the offer does NOT appear while the page has not yet taken the
file; that it appears once the load completes; and that Restore pushes exactly the recovered text back into the
page, guarded by the revision the page already held, and marks the buffer as differing from the server.
`discardingARecoveredBufferLeavesTheEditorShowingTheFile` asserts Discard closes the offer, pushes nothing into the
page, and does not mark the file as changed.

## QM10 — Two comments cited the wrong specification section

**Files:** `src/qml/EditorPaneView.qml` lines 131–134; `src/qml/TerminalPaneView.qml` lines 581–584.

*Routed to this area from the build-and-documentation reviewer.*

Both comments explained why the view uses the privileged browser profile and cited "SPEC 7.2". Section 7.2 is
"Arbitrary Websites"; the requirement for two separate profiles is 7.3, "Browser Profiles". Both now cite 7.3 with
the section title spelled out. The other citations of 7.2 in this module were judged individually and left alone —
they genuinely concern untrusted arbitrary web content.

## QM11 — The directory listing accepted another pane's answer, and another pane's failure could paint over a good listing

**Files:** `src/qml/ViewerDirectoryView.qml` lines 49–69, 119–133, 138–144 and 146–162.

**What the code did before.** One shared object, `ViewerModel`, serves every viewer pane in the window, and
every pane listens on the same two notifications: "here is the listing of this directory" and "listing this
directory failed". Each view therefore has to decide, for every notification, whether it is the answer to its own
request. Two sibling views decided this in two different ways.

The text view records the token the model hands back when the read is issued, matches an arriving reply against
that recorded token, and clears the record once the request settles. The directory view recorded nothing at all:
when a notification arrived it recomputed a key from whatever URL it happened to be showing at that moment
(`if (path === root.remotePath(root.url))`) and accepted the notification if the paths matched. It did not
require a request to be outstanding.

**Why that is wrong.** This is the same class of defect as `VW1` in this report: a pane with no reliable way to
tell its own answer apart from somebody else's. Because the key was recomputed at reply time and no outstanding
request was required, a *settled* directory view still accepted traffic aimed at other panes. Two concrete
consequences: any other pane that listed the same directory had its answer land in this view as well, replacing
the rows under the user; and a failure raised for *another* pane's request for that directory painted an error
message straight over a listing that had loaded perfectly well.

**The previous round's stated reasoning was wrong, not merely its verdict.** The earlier entry framed the problem
as "path matching versus token matching", judged the weaker path matching "harmless today because a directory
listing is idempotent", and declined the fix because it "means touching the model's cancellation interface, in
another area". Both halves are wrong. Idempotence of the *listing* does not make the matching harmless, because
the same two notifications also carry *failures*, which are not idempotent — a failure belonging to someone else
destroys a good result. And no change to the model's cancellation interface was needed, because the defect was
never the choice of key.

**What changed.** The directory view was given the text view's discipline — remember what you asked for, clear it
when the request settles — while keeping the **path** as the listing's identity. A new
`property string requestedPath` is set when the request goes out; `reload()` clears it, then sets it and issues
the request; `ownsReply(path)` requires a non-empty recorded key **and** an exact match, the same shape as
`ViewerTextView.ownsReply()`; and both the success and the failure handler clear the key on settle.

Path won as the identity, rather than the text view's token, on evidence in the model itself.
`src/viewers/ViewerModel.h` lines 124–127 states it for the path-keyed calls: unlike a file read, the answer for
one path is the same for every pane that asks, so a second pane receiving the first pane's reply learns exactly
what it would have been told anyway. A read *must* be token-keyed because `cancelTextFile()` has to tell two
concurrent reads of the same file apart; a listing has no cancel and no per-listing bookkeeping to retire. Making
listings token-keyed would mean changing `listDirectory`'s signature and adding a `cancelDirectory` in
`src/viewers/` — a different area — to buy nothing the model does not already guarantee. `ViewerPane` matches its
own path-probe and repository-root replies by path for the same reason. The file now carries a comment spelling
this out, so that the two views are not "harmonised" the wrong way round later.

The earlier entry's second complaint — that the view "never cancels a listing it has abandoned" — is answered by
the same change. `reload()` drops the recorded key, which is what abandoning means here, and `ViewerModel` keeps
no per-listing state to retire: it has `m_liveTextReads` and nothing equivalent for listings. There is nothing
model-side to cancel and nothing leaks. That is recorded in the `reload()` comment.

**Test.** New: `TstPaneIdentity::theDirectoryListingTakesOnlyTheAnswerToItsOwnRequest` in
`src/qml/tests/tst_paneidentity.cpp` lines 1972–2029. It opens a directory pane whose client has no transport, so
the view's own listing fails immediately and the view is settled. It then emits a successful listing for that same
path and asserts the rows stay empty, and emits a failure for it and asserts the error text is unchanged. Finally,
with `requestedPath` set by hand to stand in for a request this fixture cannot hold open, it asserts that a
listing for a *different* directory changes nothing and does not settle the view, while the matching one fills the
rows, clears the loading flag and clears the key. Against the pre-fix source it fails at the first assertion:

```
FAIL!  : theDirectoryListingTakesOnlyTheAnswerToItsOwnRequest()
         'view->metaObject()->indexOfProperty("requestedPath") >= 0' returned FALSE.
         (the directory view keeps no record of what it asked for, so it matches replies
          against whatever its URL happens to be when one arrives)
```

## QM12 — Nothing bounded the wait for the "is this a directory?" probe, so the pane could stay busy for ever

**File:** `src/qml/ViewerPane.qml` lines 262–297.

**What the code did before.** When the user submits an address with no trailing slash, the pane cannot tell from
the text alone whether it names a file or a directory, so it asks the server to list it and marks its header busy
until an answer arrives. The only two things that cleared the busy state were the two reply handlers.

**Why that is wrong.** A reply that never arrives — a session dropped between request and reply, for example —
leaves the header busy on that address for ever, with no route out: re-submitting the same address just starts the
same unbounded wait again. A probe with no timeout is a pane that can hang.

**What changed.** A non-repeating `Timer` (`objectName: "viewerProbeWatchdog"`, `interval: 20000`) and a single
handler that arms it when the probe path becomes non-empty and stops it when the probe path clears. Arming from
the property change, rather than from the submit routine, is what makes it impossible for a future code path to
forget. When the timer fires the pane drops the probe and opens the address **as a file** — the identical action
the existing directory-failure handler takes — so the user gets an answer and the file's own view reports whatever
the real failure is.

This shape was chosen to match what the module already does rather than to invent a per-pane mechanism, which is
what the previous round asked for. `TerminalPaneView.qml` lines 183–204 already bounds its identity lookup exactly
this way: a non-repeating timer, a budget generously larger than one round trip, stopped from the one place every
settle path passes through. The same 20 seconds is used and the comment points at it.

**Test.** New: `TstPaneIdentity::theViewerPaneStopsWaitingForAProbeThatNeverAnswers` in
`src/qml/tests/tst_paneidentity.cpp` lines 1922–1970. It finds the watchdog, asserts it is idle with no probe
outstanding, shortens its interval to 60 ms, sets the probe path, asserts the watchdog is armed and the header
busy, then asserts the pane clears the probe, drops busy, disarms the watchdog and lands on
`file:///srv/repos/app/thing`. It also asserts that a probe cleared normally disarms the watchdog, so it cannot
fire later over whatever the pane has moved on to.

The test writes the probe path directly instead of typing into the address bar, and says why in a comment: this
fixture's client has no transport, so `ViewerModel::listDirectory` answers with a failure **synchronously**, and
the wait could never be observed at all. It is the wait, not the request, that was unbounded. Against the pre-fix
source:

```
FAIL!  : theViewerPaneStopsWaitingForAProbeThatNeverAnswers() 'watchdog' returned FALSE.
         (nothing bounds the path probe: a reply that never arrives leaves the pane busy
          on an address for ever)
```

## QM13 — Views reached for an injected object without checking it was there, in a file that would throw at load time if it were not

**Files:** `src/qml/ViewerWebView.qml` lines 11–18; `src/qml/ViewerImageView.qml` lines 12–17;
`src/qml/ViewerPdfView.qml` lines 11–16; `src/qml/ViewerBinaryView.qml` lines 14–19;
`src/qml/ViewerTextView.qml` lines 21–26; `src/qml/ViewerDirectoryView.qml` lines 37–42;
`src/qml/TerminalPaneView.qml` lines 133–136 and 589.

**What the code did before.** The C++ side installs an object named `viewers` into the QML engine as a *context
property* — a global name that exists only because the host put it there. Two files in the module reach for it
defensively and document why; seven others named it bare.

**Why that is wrong.** A bare name that the host has not installed is not a null value in QML, it is a reference
error. Three of the unguarded uses sit in bindings that run while the file is being loaded, so a file loaded
without `viewers` installed would throw there and then rather than degrade. The previous round reasoned that the
guard "would be dead code today", because those views are only ever created after the property has already been
used to classify the URL. That observation is accurate for the current call graph, but it is an argument about
reachability rather than about the contract, and the module already answers the same question the other way in two
places.

**What changed.** Every one of the seven files got the module's existing idiom, byte for byte the shape
`ViewerPane.qml` line 143 and `EditorPaneView.qml` line 63 already use:

```qml
readonly property var viewerModel: (typeof viewers !== "undefined") ? viewers : null
```

and every use of the property was routed through that handle. Deliberately no third idiom was introduced; the
`profile: … : null` fallback these views now take is not invented either — `EditorPaneView.qml` line 136 already
ships exactly that.

The previous entry counted three files (web, image and PDF — the three that touch `viewers` in a load-time
binding). All seven were done, because the defect is identical in each and fixing three of them would have
re-created the very inconsistency the item is about. `TerminalPaneView.qml` is not a viewer sub-view and was not
in the original item; it held the module's last unguarded `viewers` lookup and already guards its terminal factory
two lines away, so it was included.

**Test.** No standalone test for the guard, and that is deliberate rather than an omission: the guard's whole
purpose is that a load with `viewers` *absent* degrades instead of throwing, and every existing harness installs
it, so testing it would mean building a second QML engine with the property withheld — a test of the `typeof`
operator, not of this application. What is tested is that routing every use through the handle changed nothing
when the property is present: all five targets pass, including the image, PDF, binary and directory cases in
`tst_paneidentity` and `tst_uxshell::emptyViewerPaneExplainsItself`.

One measured side effect is recorded rather than hidden. `qmllint` 6.10.2 was run over every `.qml` file in the
module, with the module registered, and the same lint was run against a copy of the module at `git HEAD` in an
identical harness: no new warning and no new warning class. The count of unqualified-access warnings fell by six
overall, but rose by two in `TerminalPaneView.qml`, where the two new references sit inside a nested `Component`
that already makes eight identical unqualified references. Silencing those needs `pragma ComponentBehavior: Bound`
on the whole file, which changes identifier-capture semantics for every nested component in an 800-line file; that
was judged too large a change to make as a side effect of a null guard.

## QM14 — Guards on an application property that can never be null were removed rather than copied

**Files:** `src/qml/Main.qml` lines 104–115, 223, 246 and 528–529.

**What the code did before.** Three conditions in the main window tested `app.uiState` for null before using it;
two other uses of the same property, at lines 117 and 164–165, did not.

**Why that is wrong.** The previous round checked the C++ and got the fact right: `AppController.h` line 41
declares the property `CONSTANT`, and `AppController.cpp` line 60 constructs the object in the constructor's
initialiser list, so it cannot be null while the application object exists and cannot change either. The previous
round then declined to act, on the grounds that adding guards "would suggest a nullability that does not exist"
while "removing them is churn". The first half is right and is precisely why the second half does not follow: a
guard on a value that cannot be null is not defensive, it is misinformation, and it costs the next reader a trip
into the C++ to find out whether there is a case where it is null. Leaving three of them in place keeps that cost.

**What changed.** The three null tests were dropped, leaving the conditions that actually mean something
(`app.activeSessionId.length > 0`), and a single comment at the first use states the fact once. The two previously
unguarded uses are untouched and are now consistent with the rest. The genuinely nullable neighbour is called out
in the same comment and left alone: `app.layouts` is an injected `QPointer` (`AppController.h` line 252) and its
roughly twelve guards are real.

**Test.** None added. This removes a condition that is provably always true from three sites, with no observable
behaviour on either side of it, so there is nothing new to assert. The three code paths are exercised by existing
gates — `tst_qmlload::loadsRealApplicationTreeWithoutWarnings`, which loads the real `Main.qml` and fails on any
QML warning, so a type error on `app.uiState` would surface there, and the focus-reporting cases in
`tst_paneidentity`, which drive the changed handler. Both pass.

## QM15 — Hints popped up in the default light style inside dark application chrome

**Files:** `src/qml/AppToolTip.qml` (new); `src/qml/CMakeLists.txt` line 26;
`src/qml/AppPaneHeader.qml` lines 140–149; `src/qml/ViewerPane.qml` lines 511–521;
`src/qml/SessionsSidebar.qml` lines 589–601; `src/qml/GroupHeader.qml` lines 167–179;
`src/qml/SessionRow.qml` lines 102–114; `src/qml/ConnectSheet.qml` lines 523–531.

**What the code did before.** Six places showed a hover hint. Two of them spelled out a full tooltip of their own,
each with the same 23 lines of colours and each carrying a comment explaining why: the short attached form is
drawn by the Qt Basic style in that style's own light palette, so a hint about dark chrome arrives as a white box.
The other four still used the attached form, so the application had two different tooltip appearances.

**Why that is wrong.** It is cosmetic, but it is a real inconsistency, and the previous round's reason for leaving
it does not survive inspection: it declined because "an existing accessibility test asserts the attached tooltip
text of two of those buttons", so converting them means changing an accessibility assertion as well as four call
sites. That is a description of the work, not an obstacle to it.

**What changed.** A new shared component, `src/qml/AppToolTip.qml` (41 lines), holds the module's own content and
background — `Theme.text`, `Theme.surfaceSunken`, `Theme.border`, `Theme.radiusSmall`, `Theme.fontSizeSmall` —
with a 400 ms delay, 6-pixel padding, and plain-text formatting because a hint can repeat a name that came from a
remote server. It is registered in the module's `CMakeLists.txt` and follows the shared-chrome pattern the module
already uses for `AppScrollBar`, `AppSplitHandle`, `AppDialog` and `AppPaneHeader`. No new visual vocabulary was
introduced: the colours are literally the ones the two hand-written tooltips already used, now said once.

All six sites were converted to it. Positioning is unchanged — the new component inherits the Basic style's
default placement, centred above its parent, which is what the four attached sites had, and the two explicit sites
keep their existing offset overrides. The three sidebar sites carry an `objectName` so the accessibility
assertions can still read the hint back.

**Test.** New: `TstSidebar::hintsAreDrawnInTheApplicationsOwnPalette` (`src/qml/tests/tst_sidebar.cpp` lines
853–905). For the add-group button, an add-session button and a session row it asserts that the attached tooltip
text is now empty, so the Basic style has nothing left to draw; that the named `AppToolTip` exists and carries a
non-empty hint; and that the hint's own background is darker than its own text and below 0.5 lightness. The two
existing assertions in `addButtonsAreLabelledWithoutAPointer` were updated to read the new component's text
instead of the attached property. Against the pre-fix sidebar, group header and session row:

```
FAIL!  : hintsAreDrawnInTheApplicationsOwnPalette() Compared values are not the same
   Actual   (attachedString(owner, "ToolTip.text")): "Add a group"
   Expected (QString())                            : ""
FAIL!  : addButtonsAreLabelledWithoutAPointer() 'groupHint' returned FALSE.
         (the add-group button carries no hint)
Totals: 2 passed, 2 failed
```

## QM16 — The viewers area's reference-counted pinning of internal addresses had no consumer, so pinned entries would have leaked

**Files:** `src/qml/ViewerImageView.qml`; `src/qml/ViewerPdfView.qml`;
`src/qml/ViewerBinaryView.qml` lines 25–62, 165–182 and 214–235.

*The other half of this contract was added in the viewers area this pass. The user-interface half is what makes it
do anything at all.*

**What the code did before.** Three views display a resource through an internal address minted by the C++ side.
The viewers area added a pair of calls — retain an address, release an address — backed by a reference count, so
that a table entry in use cannot be evicted while a view is still showing it. Nothing in the user interface called
either one, so the mechanism did nothing.

**Why that is wrong.** A retain-and-release contract with no consumer is inert; wired up carelessly it is worse
than inert, because every retain that is not matched by a release pins a table entry that can never be collected.
Both ways out of a view have to release: navigating the view to a different address, and destroying the view. Miss
either and the table grows for the life of the session.

**What changed.** Each of the three views now retains when it acquires an internal address and releases on **both**
exits — address change and destruction. Signatures were read from `src/viewers/ViewerModel.h` lines 70–71 and
`src/viewers/InternalUrlSchemeHandler.h` lines 90–100 rather than guessed. Release is driven by a `retained` flag
rather than by testing whether the held address is empty, because retaining an address the table does not know
legitimately answers false, and the contract is exactly one release for each retain that answered **true**.

The image view was rewritten around this (115 lines): the held address is typed as a URL so both sides of every
comparison go through the same normalisation, plus the flag, a retarget routine, a release routine, and handlers
for address change, completion and destruction. The PDF view is its literal twin, with comments pointing at the
image view for the reasoning instead of repeating it. In the binary view the pending download address is pinned at
the click and released in the one routine reached from acceptance, refusal, navigating away and teardown. Two
details there are worth recording: the download is accepted **before** the release, because unpinning first would
put the address back at the head of the eviction queue while the download is still fetching it; and that routine
deliberately does not clear the hidden download view's own address, because it also runs during destruction — the
click handler clears it instead, which is separately load-bearing, since a retry after a refusal would otherwise
assign the address the view already holds, which is a no-op that starts no request.

**Test.** New: `TstPaneIdentity::theViewsThatHoldAnInternalAddressPinItUntilTheyLetItGo`
(`src/qml/tests/tst_paneidentity.cpp` lines 2031–2101), measured against the real
`ch::InternalUrlMap::shared().retainedCount()` rather than a substitute. It asserts that an image pins on display;
that navigating to another image releases the old address and pins the new one, so the count stays at one above
baseline and cannot drift; that swapping the handler out destroys the view and returns the count to baseline —
that is the destruction path, covered explicitly; then that clicking the Download button for real pins again, and
that closing that view with the download still pending returns to baseline. Both release paths are therefore
exercised. Against the pre-fix image, PDF and binary views:

```
FAIL!  : theViewsThatHoldAnInternalAddressPinItUntilTheyLetItGo()
         '(views = collect(pane, isPinningView)).size() == 1' returned FALSE.
```

## QM17 — A refused image or PDF showed a blank browser failure page instead of saying what went wrong

**Files:** `src/qml/ViewerImageView.qml`; `src/qml/ViewerPdfView.qml`; `src/qml/ViewerBinaryView.qml`.

*The other half of this contract was added in the viewers area this pass; as with QM16, the user interface had to
consume it before it did anything.*

**What the code did before.** The viewers area added a notification carrying an address and a human-readable
reason a resource was refused — an image or PDF over the size limit, for example. No view listened for it. The
embedded browser was left to render its own generic failure page, which says nothing useful and makes the pane
look half-loaded.

**Why that is wrong.** The user is told nothing. A pane that has definitively failed should explain itself.

**What changed.** The image and PDF views hold an error text, gained a routine that decides whether an incoming
refusal is about the address **they** are holding, and connect to the notification. This deliberately uses the
same matching discipline settled on in QM11: compare against the address the view is holding, never against a
recomputed key, and do nothing at all when the view is holding none. Once a refusal is known the embedded browser
view is hidden and a status label is shown in the same shape as the text view's existing status label — plain
text, the theme's danger colour, centred and wrapped. The binary view does the same and additionally clears its
pending download, so the Download button becomes usable again.

**Test.** New: `TstPaneIdentity::aRefusedInternalResourceSaysWhyInsteadOfShowingABlankPage`
(`src/qml/tests/tst_paneidentity.cpp` lines 2103–2141). It opens a PDF, reads the address the view is actually
holding, emits a refusal for a **different** address and asserts nothing changed, then emits it for the held
address and asserts the error text matches and that the status label is visible and contains the message. Against
the pre-fix views:

```
FAIL!  : aRefusedInternalResourceSaysWhyInsteadOfShowingABlankPage()
         '(views = collect(pane, isPinningView)).size() == 1' returned FALSE.
```

## QM18 — An unrecognised resource would have been handed to the read-only text handler

**File:** `src/qml/ViewerPane.qml` lines 584–598 and 625.

**What the code did before.** The pane picks a handler for whatever the address resolved to with a switch on the
resource kind. Its `default` branch returned the read-only text view.

**Why that is wrong.** The specification's section 3.3 makes a viewer pane a browser addressed by a URL that
delegates a resolved resource to a handler, and says an unrecognised resource stays with the browser; section 7.5
makes that the metadata-and-download view. Sections 8.1 and 3.3 say the text and editor handler is reached only on
a positive match. Falling back to a text handler is the opposite: it pours bytes nobody could classify into a view
that assumes they are text.

**What changed.** The `default` branch now returns the metadata/download view, with a comment recording two
things: that the branch is unreachable, because `ViewerModel::viewKind()` answers with exactly the seven kinds the
switch already names and the pane supplies the remaining one itself; and why it must nevertheless not be a text
handler. The component definition for the read-only text view, now unreferenced from this file, was removed from
it.

**One consequence was routed rather than decided here, and has since been resolved.** That change left
`src/qml/ViewerTextView.qml` with no positive match anywhere in production QML — text and markdown both go to the
Monaco editor handler. The file was **not** deleted here, because `src/viewers/tests/tst_liveviewers.cpp` still
exercised it directly, and whether it should gain a positive match or be removed is a viewers-area and product
question rather than a user-interface one. It was reported upward rather than guessed at.

The viewers area then removed it, and the removal is recorded as `VW15` in this report. Before deleting, they
checked that neither path that might still have wanted the view was lost: an oversized text file keeps its content
through the editor handler's read-only prefix, and a file that fails strict decoding still gets a viewable
read-only buffer rather than being turned into a download. So the change to the `default` branch above cost no
function. The deletion took the view's C++ backing with it — the model's text-read and cancel calls, the read
count and settle helper, the two text-read notifications and their bookkeeping, the eight unit tests covering
them, and the live-viewers case — and reworded the comments that referred to them, including the two in
`ViewerDirectoryView.qml`. Those two were deliberately reworded rather than dropped: the conclusion `QM11` rests
on — match a reply against what *this* view asked for, exactly once — is kept, without the comparison to a token
that no longer has anything to point at.

**Test.** No new test for the branch itself, because it is unreachable. The switch as a whole is exercised by
`tst_qmlload` and by `tst_paneidentity`, which pins that a `.txt` address still resolves to the editor handler
(`tst_paneidentity.cpp` line 762); the new pinning test drives the binary, image and web kinds through the same
switch.

## QM19 — The editor view's header comment described it as a kind of pane and cited a specification section that now says the opposite

**Files:** `src/qml/EditorPaneView.qml` lines 8–16; `src/qml/ViewerPane.qml` lines 5–10.

*The editor half of this was routed to this area from the build-and-documentation reviewer.*

**What the code did before.** `EditorPaneView.qml` opened with "Remote editor pane (SPEC 8.1)". Section 3.3 now
says a viewer pane is a browser addressed by a URL, and that a handler is something that browser delegates a
resolved resource to — so the editor is a handler, not a pane type of its own. `ViewerPane.qml`'s own header still
described the older framing as well.

**Why that is wrong.** A header comment that contradicts the specification it cites is worse than no comment: it
is the first thing the next reader takes as true, and here it would invite exactly the change the specification
forbids — an editor view that grows its own header, address bar and pane furniture, duplicating the chrome the
viewer pane already draws around it.

**What changed.** The editor file's header now calls it the **editor handler**, keeps the 8.1 citation, explains
that section 3.3 makes the viewer pane a browser and the editor one of its handlers, notes that the viewer pane
keeps its own header, address bar and outside-project marker around this view for that reason, and states plainly
that this file must never grow chrome of its own or assume it owns the pane. `ViewerPane.qml`'s header now states
the same framing from the browser's side: it keeps its own chrome, hands the resolved resource to a handler, no
handler owns the pane, and an unrecognised resource stays with the browser.

One further mismatch was found and deliberately not acted on here: the *file name* `EditorPaneView.qml` still says
"pane", while every other handler is named `Viewer*View`. Renaming it would touch
`src/app/tests/tst_coldstart.cpp`, `src/editor/tests/tst_liveeditor.cpp` and `docs/PLAN.md`, all outside this area
and live under other reviewers, so it was reported upward instead. Test names and incidental shorthand were left
alone.

**Test.** None: comment-only changes with no behaviour attached.

### Verified in this area and found correct

* **Both examples of specification-framing drift that the sweep was asked to look for were already right.** The
  viewer pane already draws its header, address bar and out-of-project marker around every handler rather than
  inside one, and the editor view draws no pane chrome. Only the two header comments in QM19 and the fallback
  branch in QM18 actually contradicted the specification.
* **`app.layouts` is genuinely nullable and its guards were left in place.** Unlike the property in QM14, it is an
  injected `QPointer` (`src/app/AppController.h` line 252); its roughly twelve null tests are real and were not
  touched.
* **No new lint warnings.** `qmllint` 6.10.2 over every `.qml` file in the module, with the module registered,
  produces the same set of warnings as the same lint run against a copy of the module at `git HEAD`; the only
  difference is one warning whose line number moved as its file grew.

### Test totals for this area

Built out of tree with Qt 6.10.2 and the offscreen platform, so the shared development build was untouched. Five
