test targets, all green with zero failures: `tst_palette` 18, `tst_sidebar` 18 (1 new), `tst_uxshell` 26,
`tst_qmlload` 9, `tst_paneidentity` 31 (4 new) — 102 tests passed, 0 failed. Every new or changed test case was
additionally run against restored pre-fix sources and confirmed to fail there, with the failure output quoted in
the findings above.

# AG — agent awareness

## AG1 — Claude Code reported a helper agent finishing as the whole agent finishing

**Files:** `remote/src/adapters/claude-code.ts` lines 8–13 and 28–31; test at
`remote/test/adapters.test.ts` lines 188–193.

**What the code did before.** CodeHarbor learns what a coding agent is doing from small lifecycle messages the
agent's harness sends. For Claude Code the harness fires named hooks, and the adapter translates each hook name
into one of CodeHarbor's agent states. Two hook names were translated to the same state, "finished and the user
has not looked at the result yet": `Stop` and `SubagentStop`.

`Stop` fires when the agent has finished replying to the user. `SubagentStop` fires when a *helper* agent finishes
— the main agent is still working at that moment and will keep going for many more steps.

**Why that is wrong.** That state is not cosmetic. Every event carrying it sets the Dev Session's
unseen-completion badge in the sidebar and triggers a real desktop notification titled "Agent finished". So a
single Claude Code turn that fans out to, say, eight helper agents produced eight false "Agent finished"
notifications and a badge that kept re-arming while the agent was demonstrably still running — the next tool-use
event immediately flipped the state back to running. Worse, that badge is a "have you seen this?" signal:
re-arming it mid-turn destroys its meaning, and it re-arms even after the user has cleared it, because the monitor
deliberately re-arms on *every* such event rather than only on transitions.

**What changed.** `SubagentStop` now maps to "running", which is what the main agent is doing when a helper
finishes; only `Stop` remains a completion. A comment names the reason so the two are not folded back together.

**Test.** The existing lifecycle-mapping case was updated to assert the new mapping, with the reason inline. It
fails on the old mapping.

## AG2 — A blank command-line argument silently disabled the documented environment fallback

**Files:** `remote/src/hooks/oh-my-pi-hook.ts` lines 69–88; test at `remote/test/agent-hook.test.ts` lines 179–200.

**What the code did before.** The installed hook script is run once per lifecycle event with the event name as its
first argument. For a harness that cannot pass arguments to a hook, the name can come from an environment variable
instead. That was written using the "nullish coalescing" operator, which only falls through when the left side is
null or undefined.

**Why that is wrong.** The overwhelmingly common way to write a hook command in a shell configuration is to
interpolate a variable into the argument. When that variable is unset or empty, the shell still passes an argument
— an *empty string*. An empty string is not null, so the operator accepted it as the answer and the environment
variable was never consulted. The hook then printed its usage banner and emitted nothing: the documented fallback
was dead in exactly the configuration that needs it, and the user's agent status silently never updated. A
whitespace-only argument was worse — it passed the "is the event name missing?" guard, so the hook emitted a
message whose event name was a single space, which no adapter can map. That is a wasted socket round trip on every
single hook firing, with no visible symptom at all.

**What changed.** A blank (empty or whitespace-only) argument now counts as absent, so the environment fallback
applies. If both sources are blank the event name stays empty and the script prints usage exactly as it always did
for a genuinely missing name.

**Test.** The existing fallback case was extended: arguments of empty string, three spaces and a tab all resolve to
the environment value, and blank on both sides resolves to empty. Fails on the old version.

## AG3 — Whether an over-sized event was accepted depended on how the operating system chunked the socket

**Files:** `src/agent/AgentStatusMonitor.cpp` lines 98–109; test at `src/agent/tests/tst_agentmonitor.cpp` lines
991–1036.

**What the code did before.** The client reads agent events as newline-delimited JSON: one event per line, on a
stream. The read handler appends whatever arrived to a buffer, then cuts complete lines off the front and parses
each one. After that loop it has a safety valve: if what is *left* in the buffer — a line with no newline yet — is
bigger than one megabyte, the bytes are thrown away and the rest of that frame skipped, so a producer that streams
forever without a newline cannot grow client memory without bound. The loop that cuts complete lines had no size
check of its own.

**Why that is wrong.** The two paths disagree about the same event. Take one event line of 1.2 megabytes. If the
operating system happens to hand it over in pieces, the loop finds no newline, the safety valve fires, and the
event is dropped. If the same line happens to arrive in a single read, the loop sees a complete line and parses
and applies it. Identical bytes on the wire, opposite outcomes, decided by socket buffering that neither the
producer nor a test controls.

This is reachable, not theoretical. The remote relay caps the message it *accepts* at one megabyte and then builds
a strictly larger event line from it — it adds a version, a timestamp and the harness name, and re-escapes the
summary. A harness whose summary is close to a megabyte therefore lands precisely in the window where the two
endpoints disagree, and the client's behaviour is then a coin flip.

**What changed.** The cap is now a property of the frame rather than of the arrival pattern: inside the framing
loop, a complete line longer than the cap is removed and skipped without being parsed, and framing continues at the
following line. No discard-tail bookkeeping is needed, because the newline that closed it was actually seen.
Over-cap frames are now always dropped.

**Test.** New: `overCapCompleteFrameIsDroppedWhateverTheChunking`. It uses an in-memory buffer rather than a socket
pair on purpose, because that is drained in one synchronous read, which is the only way to *guarantee* the
over-cap line is framed as a single complete line — the case a socket pair cannot reproduce reliably. It feeds an
over-cap event followed by a normal one and asserts exactly one state change, for the normal event. Verified to
fail against the pre-fix source, where two state changes occur.

## AG4 — A type's documentation named the wrong key for one of the three harnesses

**File:** `remote/src/adapters/types.ts` lines 3–8. The type describing a harness's own raw event carried a comment
saying "the `type` field names the harness's own event", and declared only that field. That is true for two of the
three adapters. The Claude Code adapter reads a field called `hook`, not `type` — Claude Code has lifecycle hooks
rather than an event stream — and the relay reflects this by reading either. A reader following the comment would
conclude a Claude Code event has a `type`, and a new adapter author would key on the wrong field. That is the
"adapters disagreeing on field names" hazard, documented into the wrong shape. The comment now states which harness
uses which key, and the type declares both load-bearing keys so they are visible at the type level rather than only
in each adapter's body. No behaviour change.

## AG5 — A file header claimed an inference the program never performs

**File:** `remote/src/adapters/fallback.ts` lines 3–8. The header of the generic-harness activity detector read "so
**we infer** a coarse agent state purely from terminal output activity", written in the voice of something the
running system does. Nothing in the shipped program does it — the class has no production caller at all (see AG-N1
below and BD14). The comment read as a description of live behaviour and sent the reviewer, and would send the next
maintainer, looking for the code path that consumes it. Reworded to describe what the class *is*: the derivation
only, holding no clock and reading no stream, producing states solely for a caller that feeds it observations and
publishes its result. The class's behaviour is untouched.

## AG6 — A live-gate comment misnamed the hook's argument position

**File:** `src/agent/tests/tst_liveagent.cpp` lines 279–282. The comment describing the contract a harness must
honour said "the native event is argv[1] of the installed hook". The hook reads the *second* index — index 0 is the
interpreter and index 1 the script — and its own documentation says so. Anyone cross-reading the two files gets an
off-by-one contract for the single most important input of the whole subsystem. Reworded to "the hook's first
positional argument", which is unambiguous and matches both the hook and the live fixture script.

## AG7 — Three more comments cited the wrong specification section; one cited the right one

**Files:** `src/agent/tests/tst_agentmonitor.cpp` lines 1040–1044; `src/agent/tests/tst_liveagent.cpp` lines
546–548 and 279–283.

*Routed to this area from the build-and-documentation reviewer, with the explicit instruction to judge each site
individually rather than doing a blanket replace.*

Two comments claimed "SPEC 6.2 lists waiting_input and idle_unseen only". That is a claim about the state list,
which lives in 6.4 "Internal Event Schema", not in 6.2 "Supported Harnesses". Both now cite 6.4 for the state names
and add that the notification display layer has no section of its own and is a Phase 4 deliverable in section 16 —
matching the wording already used in the application core (AC6) rather than inventing a third phrasing.

A third comment was flagged as "probably fine" by the routing reviewer and this area disagreed after checking: the
sentence describes the hook's argument and environment calling convention, and section 6.2 defines none of that —
it lists four harness names. That convention is CodeHarbor's own hook design, documented in the hook source, so the
comment now points there and keeps its existing correct citation of 6.3 for the socket.

One was kept as correct: a comment citing the range "SPEC 6.2–6.5" for the whole harness chain. Every section in
that range is genuinely exercised by that gate — 6.2 the recognised harness, 6.3 the bridge and its socket, 6.4 the
event schema on the wire, 6.5 the state mapping — so it is accurate as written.

## AG8 — The generic harness produced no agent state at all, end to end (AG-N1 — FIXED)

**What was wrong.** The fallback detector existed and was correct, but nothing fed it terminal output and nothing
published its state. The earlier conclusion that the detector had to remain on the server led to considering a
per-pane server-side output tap or a client-to-daemon stream that duplicated every terminal byte over SSH. That
premise was wrong: `ch::TerminalController` already has every PTY byte in the client for the renderer, so the
client can derive activity without SSH byte duplication. The server-side fallback class was deleted instead of
leaving it with no caller.

**Client-side wiring.** The chain is payload-free `TerminalController::outputReceived()` to `TerminalFactory` to
`AgentStatusMonitor` for generic harnesses. `outputReceived()` is emitted by `ingestOutput()` before renderer
coalescing, so hidden panes and panes held back on acknowledgements still report that they are alive. The signal
carries only the fact of output, not terminal bytes. `TerminalFactory` binds the monitor using the server-returned
row id/devSessionId/harness; the identity is the server-minted `terminal_panes` row id, never the client layout
pane id.

The opened-pane path resolves a pane in `TerminalFactory` and calls `setTerminalHarness(devSessionId, rowId,
harness)` from the returned row. The workspace-refresh walk in `AppController` registers unopened panes after
`retainDevSessions()` has evicted the previous registrations. A successful attach calls
`noteTerminalAttached(dev, rowId)`, and each output signal calls `noteTerminalOutput(dev, rowId)`. Only the literal
harness `"generic"` participates; a plain shell and adapter harnesses do not infer agent state from prompt or
terminal output. Registration is silent, starts at `Unknown`, and attach produces `Starting`.

The two harness-registration sites intentionally remain: the factory covers opened resolved panes, while the
`AppController` refresh walk covers unopened refreshed panes and is the path that re-registers after its own
`retainDevSessions()` eviction. `setTerminalHarness` is idempotent, and each site has a cross-reference comment
naming the other, so neither is deleted as redundant. Fallback never produces `idle_unseen` or `waiting_input`.

**Identity and stale-answer handling.** Nothing binds identity at request start. `beginResolution()` records the
resolution key and, when the pane retargets to a different key, clears the old identity and tears down its output
connection while the old channel may still be printing. `finishResolution()` is the single adoption point for a
fresh, cached, or server-change answer, and adopts only when the controller's pending key still matches the answer.
A stale answer is therefore skipped. A failed resolution clears the identity, and a server change clears the
remembered resolutions. Identity is adopted only at answer delivery, cleared on failed/stale transitions and server
change, and comes from the returned row's id, devSessionId and harness rather than from the request.

The first request-time-binding implementation reintroduced QM1/TM1 and was rejected. It bound whatever identity
was known at request start, read the owning Dev Session from the request, and retained a second row-identity map
that a server change did not clear. The final implementation uses one resolved cache, clears it in `setServerId()`,
and tears down and recreates the output connection on identity changes. The output callback re-reads the current
identity for each batch, so losing identity stops reporting immediately.

**Tests and evidence.** Four real-factory tests cover row identity, retarget, failed resolution and server switch:
`aPaneReportsItsOutputUnderTheRowIdTheServerAnswered`,
`aPaneRetargetedMidLookupNeverReportsUnderTheSupersededPanesIdentity`,
`aFailedResolutionLeavesThePaneWithNoIdentity`, and
`changingTheWorkspaceServerForgetsRememberedRowIdentities`. The final figures recorded for the merged tree are
29 passed for `tst_terminalfactory`, 41 passed for `tst_agentmonitor`, 36 passed for `tst_terminalcontroller`,
and a clean TypeScript check.

Two tests initially passed for the wrong reason. A 150 ms wait let the monitor's 50 ms quiet timer demote the
wrong `Running` state before the assertion, masking the identity defect. They were rebuilt with immediate
assertions and zero-transition spies; against isolated variants that held the other defects constant, they failed
on the specific stale, failed-resolution and server-switch behaviours they claim to cover.

A client-chain smoke sequence is `unknown` → `starting` → `running` → `idle` → `running`. `attach()` itself is
only live-gate-covered because `startPty()` requires a live session; the monitor's attach transition and the
identity rules are covered offline, while the production attach path is exercised only by the live gates.

The targeted offline checks in the write-up were out of tree, and the full attach path remains limited to the live
gates; the coordinator's integrated build now passes. SPEC 6.5's precedence, SPEC 6.6's client-side reporter, and
SPEC 6.7's silence timeout were updated, and the plan checkbox for this work was legitimately ticked.

## AG9 — There was no timeout for an agent that went silent (AG-N2 — FIXED)

**What was wrong.** A harness that was killed or whose host rebooted could emit no shutdown event, leaving the
monitor's last `Running` state in place for the lifetime of the application. The sidebar could therefore report
work that had stopped hours ago.

**What changed.** `AgentStatusMonitor` now has a silence timeout and a single monitor-wide age timer. After fifteen
minutes without an observation, `Starting` and `Running` are demoted to `Unknown`, not `Idle`: the client cannot
claim that a silent agent is waiting for input, but it can withdraw the claim that the agent is live. Terminal
output refutes the silence window for every harness, and the window elapses only when a pane has said nothing at
all for fifteen minutes. `WaitingInput` and `IdleUnseen` remain attention states, while `Idle`, `Stopped`, `Error`
and `Unknown` do not assert liveness and do not age.

The timer runs only while some pane can still change with time, stops after the last such pane is evicted, and uses
the shorter configured window for its interval. Elapsed time comes from a monotonic `QElapsedTimer`, not the wall
clock, and transitions are collected before signals are emitted so a re-entering slot cannot invalidate the state
iteration.

**Test.** `aSilentRunningTerminalIsDemotedToUnknown` covers the `Running` and `Starting` transitions, the single
extra signal, the fact that `Unknown` does not age again, and disabling the feature with a zero timeout.
`terminalOutputRefutesTheSilenceTimeout` keeps a pane `Running` across eight windows of periodic output and then
demotes it once output stops. `attentionStatesAndSettledStatesNeverAge` verifies that `waiting_input`,
`idle_unseen`, `idle`, `stopped` and `error`, including the unseen badge, survive.

## AG10 — An error flag outranked a shutdown event in two adapters (AG-N3 — FIXED in both adapters)

**What was wrong.** A shutdown carrying an error flag mapped to `Error` instead of `Stopped`, leaving the row red
for a session that was already gone. The error flag is not a latch for ordinary events, but there is no later event
after shutdown to replace the state.

**What changed.** Both adapters now use the same explicit precedence:

1. A shutdown event (`session_shutdown`; `SessionEnd` for Claude Code) maps to `Stopped`, whether or not the error
   flag is set.
2. Otherwise the error flag maps the event to `Error`.
3. Otherwise the adapter's event table supplies the state.

The dead `SessionEnd` switch case was removed because the early shutdown return owns it. SPEC 6.5 now states this
three-step precedence for every adapter, including the shared Pi and Oh My Pi mapping.

**Test.** The adapter test covers shutdown with the stale flag, non-terminal flagged events that remain `Error`,
and near-miss names such as `session_shutdown_pending` and `SessionEnded`, which prove that the shutdown match is
exact rather than a prefix. The live gate also drives the terminal back to `Error`, fires `session_shutdown` with
`OMP_ERROR=1` still set, and checks for `Stopped` and a row back at `Idle`; that arm requires a real sshd.

## AG11 — The hook could not carry free-form metadata from the command line (AG-N4 — FIXED)

**What was wrong.** `HookInput` and `BridgeMessage` already declared a metadata bag and the bridge forwarded it,
but no installed hook invocation populated the bag. Only a programmatic caller could set it, so the declared wire
field was unreachable from the actual hook configuration.

**What changed.** The hook reads `$OMP_METADATA` as a JSON object of free-form fields such as a model name, turn
counter or run id, and forwards it through the existing bridge and `AgentEvent.metadata` surface. No new wire
shape was invented. Missing, blank, unparseable, `null`, scalar and array values all produce `undefined`; bad
metadata is discarded without discarding the event or throwing. An unusable value produces one stderr warning in
the shell where the JSON was written, so the operator can correct it.

**Test.** The hook tests cover nested metadata round-tripping through `toBridgeMessage`, all unusable inputs while
the event remains intact, bridge delivery over a real socket with the documented merge order, and the warning-plus
event path in `main()`.

## AG12 — One adapter was a byte-for-byte copy of another's mapping (AG-N5 — FIXED by deduplication)

**What was wrong.** Pi and Oh My Pi each carried a separate copy of the same thirteen-event mapping. A test
ensured that editing one copy alone failed, but that only defended the hazard of keeping two implementations in
lockstep.

**What changed.** `remote/src/adapters/pi-family.ts` now owns the shared event mapping and metadata. `oh-my-pi.ts`
and `pi.ts` retain their harness identities and delegate to the shared functions. The immediate benefit is that the
AG-N3 precedence fix is one edit for both harnesses rather than two copies that can drift.

**Test.** The adapter test now asserts that Pi and Oh My Pi share the same mapping and metadata function objects,
while retaining the thirteen-event behavioural sweep so a future split cannot quietly drop a case.

### Investigated in this area and deliberately left alone

* **AG-N6: the read buffer is shifted down for every line consumed — category (c), premise disproved.** On Qt 6,
  `QByteArray`'s `QArrayDataPointer` has free space at both ends, so Qt's front removal is a pointer adjustment on a detached buffer: it advances
the begin pointer and does not memmove the remaining bytes. The benchmark disproved the quadratic premise. The
shipped per-line removal was consistently faster than the proposed consume-offset rewrite: at 80,000 events the
shipped version took 521–587 ms versus 983–1143 ms for the rewrite, and four times the input cost about 3.2 times
as much on the shipped version, not four times as much. The consume-offset rewrite was therefore reverted. The two
framing tests, `manyEventsInOneReadAreFramedInOrder` and `reentrantFeedFromASlotIsFramedInOrder`, remain as guards
for the burst and re-entrant shapes and pass against the pre-fix source; they are not bug proofs.
* **AG-N7: re-entrancy of the read handler remains a confirmed, disproved category (a) non-defect.** The handler appends at the
  end, removes each complete line from the front before processing it, and therefore cannot re-frame a consumed line
  or reorder an event. `QLocalSocket` suppresses a `readyRead` raised while one is already being delivered; the
  purpose-built synchronous device test covers the mechanical re-entry that the socket does not deliver. No guard
  was added because there is no defect.
* **AG-N8: the suspected hook-process hang remains a confirmed category (a) non-defect and was not re-implemented.**
  The runtime destroys the socket once the writable side finishes when the readable side is never consumed, so an
  open socket handle does not keep the hook alive forever against a half-open peer. The attempted fix and test could
  not distinguish the two versions and were deleted; only the comment explaining why the timeout is disarmed remains.

# WS — the workspace half of the daemon

## WS1 — Copying a Dev Session silently lost any pane whose identifier collided with a built-in JavaScript name

**Files:** `remote/src/workspace.ts` lines 400–420, 1233 and 1255.

**What the code did before.** "Duplicate Dev Session" copies a session's panes into brand-new database rows with
brand-new identifiers, then rewrites the saved window-split layout so its leaves point at the copies instead of the
originals. The old-to-new translation table was a plain JavaScript object, and the rewrite looked an identifier up
with plain property access.

A plain object in JavaScript inherits properties from the language's base object — `constructor`, `toString`,
`valueOf`, `hasOwnProperty` and so on. So looking up `"constructor"` does not return "not found"; it returns the
built-in constructor **function**.

**Why that is wrong.** The pane identifiers inside a layout tree come from the desktop client, which is free to put
any string there — the terminal region legitimately stores human-authored slot labels. A leaf whose identifier was
the literal string `constructor` (or `toString`, `valueOf`, …) therefore resolved to a function instead of a string.
The rewritten tree is then serialised to JSON, and JSON serialisation **drops** properties whose value is a
function. The result: the copied leaf came out with no identifier at all, so the duplicated session showed an empty
pane where the original showed content, with no error anywhere. Reproduced directly: the old code produced a leaf
with no identifier; the new code preserves it.

**What changed.** Both translation tables are now real map objects and the rewrite uses their lookup method. A map
has no inherited keys, so an unmapped identifier always reads back as "not found" and the existing fallback keeps
the original string. This also matches the project's own rule that runtime-inserted keys belong in a map rather
than a plain object.

**Test.** New: "duplicateSession leaves a paneId named after an Object.prototype member alone". It duplicates a
session whose layout holds `constructor` in one leaf and a real identifier plus `toString` in the other, then
asserts the unmapped `constructor` survives verbatim, the real identifier is still remapped to the copied pane, and
the unmapped one is dropped rather than turned into a function. The first assertion fails on the old code.

## WS2 — A null for a column that cannot be null was accepted, discarded, and answered with "success"

**Files:** `remote/src/workspace.ts` lines 1920, 1951, 1952, 2000 and 2037; new guard at
`remote/src/validate.ts` lines 67–86.

**What the code did before.** Update requests carry only the fields the caller wants to change. Some of those
fields are genuinely nullable — sending a null task description means "clear it" — so the shared validation guard
accepts three things: absent, null, or a string.

That same permissive guard was also used for the fields that are **not** nullable: a group's name, a Dev Session's
name and repository root, a viewer pane's address, a terminal pane's name. When one of those arrived as null, the
guard let it through, and the store then fell back to the value already in the row, because the fallback operator
treats null exactly like absent.

**Why that is wrong.** A silent-failure path. A caller that sent an update with a null name got a normal success
response containing a session object, and the row was completely unchanged. Nothing told anyone the request had
been thrown away. Every other bad value on those same fields — a number, an array — is rejected with a clear
"invalid params" error; null alone was swallowed. It is also an inconsistency: the code deliberately distinguishes
"absent" from "null" for the fields that *can* be cleared, so the two families are handled by two different rules
while sharing one guard. Confirmed reachable in process: an update with a null name returned the old name.

**What changed.** A new guard was added — identical to the existing one except that it rejects an explicit null —
and applied to exactly those five non-nullable fields. The genuinely nullable fields keep the permissive guard and
keep their clear-on-null behaviour.

**Test.** New: "a null is rejected for the fields that cannot be cleared". It drives all five method-and-field pairs
through the real handler table and asserts each throws.

## WS3 — A negative or fractional ordering position was written straight into the database

**File:** `remote/src/workspace.ts` lines 1913, 1921, 1944, 1955, 1994, 2003, 2020 and 2042.

**What the code did before.** Every group, Dev Session and pane carries an integer position that fixes its order in
the sidebar. Every internal path keeps those numbers packed as contiguous integers starting at zero, and listing
queries sort by them. A client may optionally supply a position on create or update, and that value was validated
with a guard that accepts **any** finite number, including −5 and 2.5. The supplied value was then stored verbatim.
SQLite's integer column affinity does not coerce 2.5; it stores the real number as-is.

**Why that is wrong.** Reproduced against a live database: creating a group at position 2.5 stored 2.5, and the
*next* created group got 3.5, because the "next position" query is the maximum plus one. So once one fractional
value is in a scope, the corruption is sticky and spreads to every subsequent row. A negative position pins a row
permanently above whatever the user dragged to the top. This is the same class of defect the code already went to
some trouble to fix in the move-between-groups method, whose comment explains that storing a requested position
verbatim leaves two rows sharing one slot; the create and update paths never got the same treatment.

**What changed.** The position guard on all eight create and update handlers was switched to the existing guard
that requires a non-negative whole number, rejecting fractions, negatives and not-a-number with a precise message.
The C++ client's serializers were checked: the client omits the field when unset and sends a plain integer
otherwise, so nothing it can send is newly rejected. The move-between-groups method deliberately keeps the
permissive guard, and a comment now says why: there the value is a drag *index*, not a stored position, and the
method truncates and clamps it into the target group's range on purpose — pinned by an existing test that passes
−5.

**Test.** New: "a fractional or negative position is rejected by every create/update" — a table of all eight
handlers crossed with 2.5, −1 and not-a-number, asserting each is rejected.

## WS4 — Any failure reading the stored schema version was reported as "this database is empty"

**File:** `remote/src/workspace.ts` lines 894–911.

**What the code did before.** On open, the daemon reads the stored schema version to decide which migrations to
run. That read was wrapped in a bare catch-all that turned *every* failure into "version 0", i.e. a brand-new
unmigrated database.

**Why that is wrong.** Only one failure actually means "unmigrated": the version table not existing yet. Every
other failure — the file is not a SQLite database at all, the database image is malformed, the version column has
an unexpected shape, a lock outlasted the busy timeout — was swallowed. The daemon then went on to re-apply the
entire schema, and whatever failed *second* in that sequence became the error the operator saw, burying the real
cause. Demonstrated: given a database whose version table has a differently named column, the old path re-ran the
schema and failed on a later statement; the fixed path reports "no such column: version", which is the actual
problem.

**What changed.** The version read now returns zero only when the caught error is the specific "no such table"
case, and rethrows everything else, so the original diagnosis propagates.

**Test.** New: "openWorkspace reports why an unreadable database failed, not a downstream error". It hand-builds a
database whose version table lacks the version column and asserts the open throws with that exact message.

## WS5 — A helper read like a general-purpose function but only ever inspected one hard-coded table

**File:** `remote/src/workspace.ts` lines 651–675, with call sites at 780, 785 and 816.

A helper named "which unique index covers exactly these columns" took a database and a list of column names. Its
name and signature say nothing about a table, but its body queried a literal, hard-coded table name. The name
promises generality the function does not have: a future caller asking about a real unique constraint on a
*different* table would get "not found" back and would conclude the constraint is missing, then create a duplicate
one — and nothing in the signature or the call site would look wrong. Renamed to name the table it actually
inspects, with the documentation updated and all three call sites changed. No behaviour change; the two migration
steps that use it are already pinned by existing tests.

## WS6 — A "nesting depth counter" that can only ever be zero or one

**File:** `remote/src/workspace.ts` lines 921–925 and 1834–1859.

The transaction wrapper groups writes into one database transaction and is re-entrant, because SQLite rejects a
nested begin and some methods call each other. Re-entrancy was tracked by a field named "transaction depth",
commented as "nesting depth on this connection". It is not a depth counter: a nested call returns immediately
without incrementing, and the outer call assigns the literals 1 and 0 rather than incrementing and decrementing, so
the value can only ever be 0 or 1. The name and comment invite a future maintainer to "fix" the nested branch to
increment, at which point the assignments in the outer commit and rollback paths become wrong and a genuine
three-level nesting would leave a transaction open. Renamed to a boolean flag, with a comment stating that a flag
is deliberate because a nested call never opens a transaction of its own. Behaviour identical; the observable
contract is already pinned by an existing test.

## WS7 — Upgrading a database with an orphaned pane row failed, and left the whole workspace permanently unopenable

**Files:** `remote/src/workspace.ts` lines 903–942; new test and helper in `remote/test/workspace.test.ts`
lines 1421 and 1458.

**What the code did before.** One schema upgrade has to rebuild the `terminal_panes` table from scratch, because
SQLite cannot drop a constraint that was declared on the table itself — the only way to remove it is to create a
new table with the shape you want, copy every row across, and swap the names. That copy is an ordinary
`INSERT … SELECT`, and it ran with foreign-key enforcement switched on, inside the migration runner's single
transaction.

**Why that is wrong.** SQLite checks a foreign key only when a row is written, never when a row is read. A database
that already holds a pane row whose parent Dev Session no longer exists therefore opens and serves perfectly well
today — nothing re-examines the stale row. The rebuild, though, *writes* every row again, so every one of those
untouched keys is re-checked. On such a database the copy failed and the exception came straight out of the open
call:

```
Error: FOREIGN KEY constraint failed
    at migrateDropTerminalPaneAddressUnique (remote/src/workspace.ts:822:8)
    at migrate (remote/src/workspace.ts:871:22)
    at openWorkspace (remote/src/workspace.ts:603:9)
```

The whole upgrade then rolled back and `openWorkspace` threw, so the database stayed on its old version and failed
the same way on the next attempt: **permanently unopenable**. One stale pane row took every group, Dev Session,
viewer pane and layout in the file down with it.

**What changed.** `migrate()` now follows the procedure SQLite's own documentation prescribes for a table rebuild:
it records whether foreign-key enforcement is currently on, turns it **off**, and restores the previous setting in
a `finally` block so the very next ordinary write is enforced again. The pragma is set *outside* `BEGIN`, which is
the point — SQLite documents `PRAGMA foreign_keys` as a no-op inside an open transaction, and the deferred variant
only postpones the identical failure to `COMMIT`. The previous round concluded from this that no fix was possible
inside the transaction; that was right as far as it went, and the answer is to move the pragma out of the
transaction rather than to move the rebuild out.

This is also the only candidate that loses no user data. Deleting the orphaned rows discards a pane the user may
still be able to adopt; re-parenting them invents an owner nobody chose, which is not the server's decision to
make; detecting the condition and failing with a clear message still leaves the workspace unopenable, which is the
actual defect. With enforcement off the rebuild is a plain copy, and nothing between `BEGIN` and `COMMIT` inserts a
row that would need checking — the migration steps only run schema statements and repair rows that are already
there. The connection pragmas that set the write-ahead journal and the busy timeout still run first, before
anything else, exactly as before; only the foreign-key setting is touched, only for the duration of the migration.

A database that already contains an orphan now migrates successfully and keeps the orphan. That is deliberate: it
is the state that database was already in, and both refusing it and silently "cleaning" it are worse.

**Test.** New: "the v4 rebuild migrates a legacy database that contains an orphaned pane row". It seeds a real
workspace, downgrades `terminal_panes` to the older shape through a new helper, plants an orphaned row with
enforcement off (the way one comes to exist on disk), reopens, and asserts that the open succeeds, the schema
version reaches the target, the constraint that should go is gone while the one that should stay survives, the
orphan is still present unchanged, the reachable workspace lists in full, and ordinary writes are foreign-key
enforced again afterwards. Against the unfixed source this test fails with the `FOREIGN KEY constraint failed`
stack quoted above; with the fix it passes.

## WS8 — A migration step was justified by a reason that does not hold; the step is still required, for a different one

**Files:** `remote/src/workspace.ts` lines 737–745; `remote/test/workspace.test.ts` lines 1369–1377.

**What the code did before.** One migration renames duplicate terminal-slot labels so that no two panes in a Dev
Session share a label, and the very next migration removes the rule that made duplicates illegal. That looks like
pointless data damage, so both the source comment and the previous round's report explained why the rename must
stay: it "is still what makes the legacy by-label lookup land on exactly one row".

**Why that is wrong.** The stated reason does not hold. The legacy by-label lookup is
`SELECT * FROM terminal_panes WHERE dev_session_id = ? AND name = ? ORDER BY rowid LIMIT 1`
(`resolveTerminalPane`). It takes the oldest matching row and returns exactly one result whether or not duplicates
exist, so it does not depend on the rename at all. A maintainer who checked that claim would find it false and
would reasonably conclude the whole step is dead weight.

Deleting the step would in fact be a serious regression, for a reason nobody had written down. The same migration
later runs `CREATE UNIQUE INDEX terminal_panes_address_unique ON terminal_panes (dev_session_id, name)`
(`remote/src/workspace.ts` line 807), and that statement fails outright on a database that still holds two rows for
one slot. Both migrations run inside the migration runner's single transaction, so the failure rolls the entire
upgrade back — leaving a legacy database with duplicate labels permanently unopenable, exactly the failure
described in WS7.

**What changed.** No behaviour: the migration step stays as it is. What changed is the justification, in both the
source comment and the comment above the test that pins the behaviour. Both now say that the step exists for the
unique index created two phases later, and that the legacy by-label lookup is single-valued without it. The
decision was right; the reason recorded for it was not, and a wrong reason attached to load-bearing code is how it
gets deleted later.

**Test.** The existing test "the v3 migration de-duplicates slot names, and v4 drops the index again" already pins
the behaviour and still passes unchanged. Nothing behavioural changed, so there is nothing that would fail without
this correction.

## WS9 — An index name taken from the database was quoted with JavaScript escaping instead of SQL escaping

**Files:** `remote/src/workspace.ts` lines 659–661, 681 and 839; `remote/test/workspace.test.ts` lines 1010, 1030
and 1207.

**What the code did before.** Two migration steps read index names back out of SQLite's own catalogue and then
interpolate them into further statements — `PRAGMA index_info(…)` and `DROP INDEX …`. The name was wrapped for
those statements with `JSON.stringify`, which escapes an embedded double quote as `\"`.

**Why that is wrong.** SQL and JavaScript escape identifiers differently. SQL has exactly one rule: wrap in double
quotes and write an embedded double quote twice. A backslash means nothing inside a SQL identifier, so an index
whose name contains a double quote produced a statement SQLite could not parse.

The previous round recorded this as unreachable, on the grounds that the names are either generated by SQLite or
written as literals in this file. That is not so: the names are read out of `sqlite_master` and `PRAGMA
index_list`, so they are whatever the database on disk happens to contain — an index created by an older build, by
a hand edit, or by another tool can carry any name at all. It is **reachable**, and the consequence is not
cosmetic. Given an index literally named `odd"name_unique`, the migration threw out of the open call:

```
Error: near "name_unique": syntax error
    at terminalPaneUniqueIndexOver (remote/src/workspace.ts:669:14)
    at migrateDropTerminalPaneAddressUnique (remote/src/workspace.ts:816:19)
    at migrate (remote/src/workspace.ts:871:22)
    at openWorkspace (remote/src/workspace.ts:603:9)
```

The database does not open at all.

**What changed.** A small `quoteIdentifier` helper applies the SQL rule — wrap in double quotes, double any
embedded one — and both call sites use it. The two test helpers that read index names out of the catalogue were
given the same treatment, since they would otherwise have broken on the new test's index name.

**Test.** New: "the v4 migration drops an address index whose name contains a double quote". It builds a legacy
`terminal_panes`, creates a unique index named `odd"name_unique` over it, stores the schema version so that only
the final migration runs, reopens, and asserts the index is gone and every row survived untouched — that is, it was
dropped by name rather than by rebuilding the table. Against the unfixed source it fails with the syntax error
above; with the fix it passes.

## WS10 — Creating a row at an explicit position left it tied with an existing row, so the order came out arbitrary

**Files:** `remote/src/workspace.ts` lines 977–978, 1081–1083, 1161–1163, 1440–1449, 1537–1546 and 1905–1928.

**What the code did before.** Groups, Dev Sessions, viewer panes and terminal panes each carry an integer position
that fixes their order, and every scope keeps those numbers packed as `0, 1, 2, …`. When a create request asked for
a specific position, that number was written straight into the new row's column.

**Why that is wrong.** The new row then shared its position with whichever row already held it. Every listing
orders by position and then by identifier, so the tie was broken by a random UUID: the new row appeared above or
below its neighbour by chance, and stayed tied for every later read. Reproduced: three groups created as A, B and
then M at position 1 list back as `A, B, M` instead of `A, M, B`. The move-between-groups method had already been
fixed for exactly this failure; all four create paths still had it.

**What changed.** A new private `placeAt` splices the just-inserted row into its scope at the requested index and
re-packs the scope through the existing packing routine, so positions stay dense and no two rows share one. An
index past the end appends, which is the only sensible reading of "create at position 99" in a scope of three. The
four create methods call it only when a position was actually supplied, so the ordinary append path is unchanged
and costs nothing. While doing this, the three helpers that name an ordered table and its scope column each spelled
out their own inconsistent list of allowed names; those are now two shared types.

The previous round left this alone as a product decision it could not make — what "create at position N" ought to
mean. On a closer look there is no decision to make: `position` was already defined as an ordering index, the
packing routine already defines what a packed scope is, and the move method already established that placing a row
re-packs. This change only makes creation agree with the interface that was already there.

**Test.** New: "creating a row at an explicit position re-packs its scope instead of tying". It covers all four
ordered tables and asserts both the resulting order and that positions come back dense from zero, plus the
append-past-the-end case. Against the unfixed source it fails on its first assertion, with the group created at
position 1 coming back last.

## WS11 — A test file opened database connections without the settings the daemon requires on every connection

**Files:** `remote/test/schema.test.ts` lines 7, 24–34 and 36.

**What the code did before.** The daemon has one routine that applies the required per-connection settings — the
write-ahead journal and a five-second busy timeout — and its documentation says to call it on *every* connection to
a workspace database, the daemon's own and any raw connection a test opens. The schema test file opened in-memory
connections and loaded the schema into them without calling it.

**Why that is wrong.** No user-visible failure: an in-memory database with a single connection has nothing to
contend with. It is a correctness gap in the test itself. The file exists to check that the schema loads and
behaves, and it was checking that on a connection configured differently from every connection the product uses,
so any future defect that depends on those settings would be invisible here.

**What changed.** The schema test's loader now applies the connection settings immediately after opening the
connection and before running any schema statement, as the routine's documentation requires.

**Test.** The file's first case was extended and renamed to "schema.sql loads into an in-memory database, on a
properly configured connection", and now asserts that the busy timeout reads 5000 on the connection the schema was
loaded into. SQLite's default is 0, so the assertion fails the moment the loader stops applying the settings —
confirmed by removing that one line, which produces `actual: 0, expected: 5000`.

## WS12 — The listing path recompiled the same five queries on every call

**Files:** `remote/src/workspace.ts` lines 15, 964–971, 1006 and 1803–1847.

**What the code did before.** Listing a whole workspace runs one query for the groups, one per group for its
sessions, and three per session for its viewer panes, terminal panes and layouts. Each of those compiled its SQL
text from scratch on every call, so a workspace with ten sessions recompiled the same handful of statements
dozens of times per listing.

**Why that is wrong.** Two neighbouring methods — duplicating a session and re-packing an order — already lift
their statements out of their loops, with comments explaining that this is to avoid exactly this cost. The listing
path is the one that runs most often, and it was the one not doing it, so the file contradicted its own established
practice.

**What changed.** The connection now holds a lazily built set of the five compiled statements, and the listing
methods use it. Lazy rather than built in the constructor for two reasons stated in the code: a connection used
only for writes should not pay for it, and compiling a statement requires the tables to already exist, which the
constructor cannot promise for a caller that did not come through the normal open path. No SQL text changed.

The alternative considered was rewriting the listing as four set-based queries. That was rejected: it changes the
shape of the read and its ordering guarantees for a saving the simple hoist already achieves, and the hoist is what
the neighbouring methods do.

**Test.** None, and this is untestable at this seam — the statements are private to the connection and Node's
SQLite binding exposes no counter of how many times a statement was compiled, so "compiled once" is not observable
from a test. The change is behaviour-preserving and the behaviour it preserves is pinned by the existing listing
tests, including the close-and-reopen byte-identical-state test, all of which pass.

### Verified in this area and found correct

Recorded so these checks are not repeated. **Write-ahead log and busy timeout** (the standing project rule): the
busy timeout is set **before** the journal-mode switch, and the pragma routine runs before the foreign-key pragma,
before migration and before the index definitions — so contention during schema changes is covered too. The
journal-mode attempt is wrapped because some network filesystems cannot host its side file, which is correct. Both
are pinned by tests. **Transaction boundaries and rollback**: every read-then-write method is inside the wrapper,
which takes a write lock before reading so two daemons serialise rather than clobber, and rolls back on any throw;
the migration runner has its own transaction around the whole upgrade. **Parameter binding**: no user-controlled
value is ever interpolated into SQL; the only interpolated identifiers are table and column names typed as closed
sets of string literals, and the type is what prevents a caller-supplied name reaching the query text. **Cascade
deletes and orphans**: children are deleted before parents, because the foreign keys reject rather than cascade;
pinned by tests including one proving a naive parent delete really is rejected. **Migrating an old database
forward**: an era-appropriate database was hand-built on disk in the shape where a constraint is expressed as an
automatic index SQLite cannot drop, and opened with this build; the table rebuild ran correctly, the version
advanced, rows survived, the constraint that should be kept was preserved, the one that should go was gone. **Index
recreation after that rebuild**: dropping the table takes its index with it, but the index definitions are applied
unconditionally *after* migration, so it comes back; pinned by a test. **The layout upsert**: keyed on the schema's
unique constraint, inside one transaction with the parent lookup and the read-back, so two concurrent writers
cannot both insert and neither can be handed the other's tree. **The wire name mirror**: every workspace method
name is set-compared against the C++ header in both directions, and a separate test asserts every contract name has
a server handler; the handler table is typed so a missing handler is a compile error. **Every handler validates
before use**: all twenty check that the parameter is an object first, then apply a guard per field; unknown extra
fields are ignored, null and wrong types are rejected, and the one enumerated field is checked against a closed set
rather than being left to a database constraint.

### Investigated in this area and deliberately left alone

The previous round's list of six items has been worked through: five were fixed (WS7, WS9, WS10, WS11, WS12) and
one was kept, but for a corrected reason (WS8). What remains are the choices made inside those fixes.

1. **The migration that renames duplicate slot labels stays.** Removing it would make a legacy database holding
   duplicate labels permanently unopenable, because a later migration in the same transaction creates a uniqueness
   constraint that fails on them. Written up as WS8, together with the correction to the reason previously
   recorded for keeping it.
2. **A database that already contains an orphaned pane row keeps that row after migrating.** The WS7 fix makes the
   upgrade succeed rather than making the data consistent. Deleting an orphan throws away a pane the user may
   still be able to adopt, and choosing a new parent for it is a decision the server has no basis to make. The
   database is left in exactly the state it was already in, and the test asserts that.
3. **The nested listing was not rewritten as four set-based queries.** Hoisting the compiled statements (WS12) gets
   the same saving without changing the shape of the read or its ordering guarantees, and it is what the
   neighbouring methods in the same file already do.

# DF — the file-serving and process half of the daemon

## DF1 — A single line reading `null` on the bridge socket killed the whole bridge process

**Files:** `remote/src/bridge.ts` lines 50–67 and the socket line handler around line 176.

**What the code did before.** The bridge is a small helper that listens on a Unix socket for status messages posted
by harness hooks, converts each line of JSON into a CodeHarbor agent event, and prints it for the desktop client.
The line processor parsed the line inside a try/catch, and if the parse succeeded it went straight on to read fields
off the result. Its documentation comment claims the function is "pure and total: never throws".

**Why that is wrong.** Parsing the four characters `null` does **not** throw — it returns the value null, so the
catch never runs — and reading a property off null throws a type error. That throw happens inside the socket's line
handler, where nothing catches it, and an exception escaping an event handler in this runtime terminates the
process. So any producer that writes four bytes and a newline — a buggy hook, a truncated write, a stray shell
command — takes the bridge down, and with it every harness's status reporting for the whole session. That is exactly
the failure mode the specification says must not happen: "a broken line is dropped, never thrown". Arrays and bare
numbers did not crash, but they are not messages either and were only rejected by accident, further down.

**What changed.** After the parse, the processor now rejects anything that is not a plain object using a guard it
already imports, and only then treats the value as a message. Because a decoded object may legitimately be missing
any field, the message type's four required members were made optional, which is what the wire actually looks like;
the per-field guards below are unchanged.

**Test.** The existing "drops malformed, unknown-harness, and no-op lines" case was extended with the lines `null`,
`[]` and `42`, all of which must return nothing. The first throws before the fix.

## DF2 — The bounded notification queue was defeated whenever a large reply stalled the output stream

**Files:** `remote/src/codeharbord.ts` line 385, lines 430–535 and lines 585–635.

**What the code did before.** The daemon writes two kinds of output on the same stream: replies to requests, and
unsolicited "this file changed" notifications from the file watcher. A stream write returns false to mean "my
internal buffer is full, stop writing" and later emits a drain event. The notification relay implements careful
backpressure: on a false return it flips a stalled flag and starts putting notifications in a bounded, coalescing
queue instead of writing them, reporting anything it had to drop. But it only ever saw the return value of **its
own** writes. Replies were written directly and their return value discarded.

**Why that is wrong.** The two writers share one stream, so the stall is a property of the stream, not of the
writer. If the client's end stops reading and the write that first reports the full buffer happens to be a large
reply — a file read of a big file, by far the most likely candidate — the relay never learns the output stalled.
From then on every notification is written straight through and appended to the runtime's internal buffer, which has
no limit. A churning directory then grows the daemon's memory until it dies, killing the whole workspace connection.
That is precisely the failure the bounded queue exists to prevent, and the bound simply did not apply in that
ordering.

**What changed.** The relay gained an explicit "someone else's write to the same stream reported a full buffer"
method, which sets the same flag its own writes set and is cleared by the existing drain handler. All three
reply-writing sites now go through one small helper that calls it when the write returns false.

**Test.** New: "a stall caused by a response write still bounds the relay's queue". It stalls the fake stream and
reports the stall through the new method — i.e. the reply writer stalled it, not the relay — delivers two events,
and asserts nothing was written and both are queued; then drains and asserts both flush. Without the fix the relay
writes both into the full buffer.

## DF3 — The line framer copied quadratically, and framed the tail of an over-sized frame as if it were a request

**File:** `remote/src/codeharbord.ts` lines 293–361.

**What the code did before.** The daemon reads newline-delimited JSON from its input with a hand-written framer.
Every incoming chunk was appended to one growing buffer by concatenation, then scanned for newlines. When the
unfinished line passed the 16-megabyte cap the framer emptied the buffer and reported an overflow — but kept framing
whatever arrived next as if it were the start of a fresh line.

**Why that is wrong.** Two distinct problems.

The cost: concatenation copies everything received so far, on every chunk. A 16-megabyte request — which is routine,
because writing a large file sends its contents base64-encoded on one line — arriving in 64-kilobyte reads costs on
the order of 2 gigabytes of memory copying for a single request, and the cost grows with the square of the frame
size.

The correctness: after an overflow, the remainder of the over-sized frame is not a request the peer sent; it is the
middle of a payload the peer never finished. The old code emitted everything between the overflow point and the next
newline as a line and handed it to the dispatcher, which parsed it as a request. It also re-reported an overflow for
every further 16 megabytes of the same frame, so one runaway producer produced an unbounded number of "closing
connection" messages and stream-destroy calls. In production the caller destroys the input on the first overflow, so
this was latent rather than active — but it is exactly the kind of thing a reordering or a second caller turns into
a live bug, and the exported helper has no such contract written down.

**What changed.** The framer now holds unfinished input as a *list* of chunk slices with a running byte count and
joins once when the newline arrives — one copy per line instead of one per chunk. A discarding state was added:
after an overflow the framer drops everything up to and including the next newline and then resumes framing
normally. Behaviour for well-formed input is unchanged.

**Test.** New: "createLineFramer discards the rest of an over-cap frame instead of framing it". It floods past the
cap, asserts exactly one overflow report, floods four more megabytes and asserts still exactly one, then feeds
trailing garbage followed by a valid line and asserts only the valid line is framed. The two existing framer tests
still pass unchanged.

## DF4 — Saving a file with a long name always failed

**File:** `remote/src/files.ts` lines 309–329 and 338.

**What the code did before.** An atomic save writes the new contents to a temporary file in the same directory and
then renames it over the target. The temporary name was a dot, plus the target's filename, plus a dot, plus twelve
random hexadecimal characters, plus `.tmp` — the original name plus 18 characters.

**Why that is wrong.** A single filename component is limited to 255 bytes on Linux and macOS. A file named with 250
bytes is perfectly legal and not exotic — generated files, long test-case names, some data exports. For any target
name longer than 237 bytes the temporary name exceeds the limit, the open fails with "file name too long", and
**every** save of that file fails with an error the user can do nothing about. The file itself opens and reads fine,
so it looks like the editor is broken.

**What changed.** The temporary-name construction was extracted into its own function, which clips the base-name
part so the whole name fits in 255 **bytes**. Clipping is measured in bytes rather than in string length — the
kernel limit is a byte count while a JavaScript string is counted in 16-bit units — and never cuts a multi-byte
character in half. Uniqueness comes from the random suffix, which is preserved intact.

**Test.** New: "writeFile saves a file whose name nearly fills the 255-byte limit". It creates a 250-character name,
saves it once (the create path), saves it again (the overwrite path, which resolves the target through the real path
and preserves the file mode), verifies the contents both times, and asserts no temporary file was left behind. It
fails with "file name too long" before the fix.

## DF5 — The server reported version 0.1.0 while the release was 0.1.8

**Files:** `remote/src/codeharbord.ts` lines 66–73; test at `remote/test/adapters.test.ts` around line 135.

**What the code did before.** The server version was a hand-written literal reading `"0.1.0"`. The package manifest
beside it says 0.1.8, and the release script rewrites the top-level build file plus the four package manifests — it
did not know about this constant. The value is returned by the `server.info` request and the desktop client puts it
in front of the user verbatim: "Server too old: codeharbord **0.1.0** speaks workspace schema N, but this client
needs …". The existing test asserted the literal `"0.1.0"`, so the drift was frozen in place rather than caught.

**Why that is wrong.** A user diagnosing a version-mismatch error is told the server is three releases older than
it is. Anyone who then checks the deployed artefact's manifest sees a different number and cannot tell which is
lying.

**What changed.** The constant was set to 0.1.8, with a comment naming the package manifest as the source of truth
and explaining why it drifted. The test was rewritten so that instead of pinning the literal it reads the manifest
at test time and asserts the reported version equals it — so the next release fails this test unless the constant is
bumped too. The durable half of the fix, teaching the release tooling and the version checker about this file, is
BD15.

## DF6 — The output sink stacked one close-listener per stall cycle on the same socket

**File:** `remote/src/bridge.ts` lines 133–163.

**What the code did before.** When the bridge's output stalls, the sink pauses the producer socket that fed the
event, adds it to a paused set, and subscribes to that socket's close event so a producer that disconnects
mid-stall is removed from the set. The subscription was made every time the socket was paused.

**Why that is wrong.** A long-lived producer is paused and resumed once per stall, and a busy agent stalls the
output repeatedly. Each pause added another close listener to the same socket; none of them fire until the socket
actually closes, so they accumulate. At eleven, the runtime prints a "possible memory leak" warning to its error
stream — which the client displays as session diagnostics — and every one of those closures is retained for the
life of the connection. Same class of leak as the one the surrounding comment was written to fix, one level up.

**What changed.** A weak set of sockets that already carry the cleanup listener was added, and the subscription now
happens only the first time. Weak, so a closed socket stays collectable.

**Test.** New: "makeStreamSink subscribes to a source's close only once across stalls", running three full
stall-and-drain cycles and asserting exactly one subscription. The shared fake-source helper gained a counter,
because it previously overwrote its record of the handler and so could not see the duplicates.

## DF7 — The file-status reply mixes information about a link with information about its target, with no explanation

**File:** `remote/src/files.ts` lines 135–144 (comment only).

For a symbolic link, the file-status reply returns the kind, size, modification time and permission bits describing
the **link itself**, but the revision token and the writability flag describing the **file the link points at**.
Each of those two decisions is separately justified in a comment; the fact that the result is a mixture is not
stated anywhere.

That is a hazard because the size through a symbolic link is the length of the link's own path text — a couple of
dozen bytes — not the size of the file a read would return. Any future caller that uses the size to decide "is this
file small enough to load inline?" would wave through a link to a multi-gigabyte file. No consumer in either
language reads that field today, so this is latent rather than active, and the client explicitly documents the
permission bits as link-blind, so changing which node those fields describe would contradict a deliberate
cross-language contract. Documentation only: an explicit comment now states which fields describe the link and
which describe the target, why, and that a consumer wanting the target's size must resolve the link itself.

## DF8 — Saving through a broken symbolic link destroyed the link and put a regular file in its place

**Files:** `remote/src/files.ts` lines 309–344 (new link-following loop), 346–364 and 426–441; test at
`remote/test/files.test.ts` lines 1226 and 1261.

**What the code did before.** Before writing a file the save routine asks the operating system for the file's
status. That call follows symbolic links, so a link whose target does not exist reports "there is nothing at
this path". A create-only save therefore took the *create* branch, in which the write target is the path the
caller named — the link itself. The routine writes to a temporary file and renames it over the target, so the
rename replaced the link with a fresh regular file.

**Why that is wrong.** The user's link is gone, silently, with nothing to undo it. It also disagrees with what
every other tool does: opening a dangling link for creation creates the link's *target*, leaving the link
alone. A second, quieter problem sat beside it: saves are serialised by a per-path lock, and the lock key was
computed from the unresolved path whenever the target was missing, so a save addressed to a link and a save
addressed to the same file by its real name took two different locks and could run at the same time.

**What changed.** The save now writes *through* the link, which is what a plain create does and is the only one
of the two available behaviours that cannot destroy something the user made. (Refusing the save would also have
been safe, but it would make a broken link permanently unsaveable from the editor.) Because the standard
resolver reports "no such file" the moment any component of the chain is missing — exactly the dangling case —
the chain is now followed by hand: read the link, repeat, and stop at the first name that is not a link, which
is the file to create. The loop is capped at 40 levels, the same limit the Linux kernel uses, and reports the
same "too many levels of symbolic links" error when it hits the cap; without a cap two links pointing at each
other would spin forever inside a request handler. The lock key is computed from the resolved chain as well, so
the link and its target now share one lock.

**Test.** Two new cases. *"writeFile through a dangling symlink creates the target and keeps the link"* creates
a broken link, saves through it, and checks that the link is still a link, still points where the user pointed
it, that the bytes landed in the target, that no temporary file was left behind, that the revision token
returned matches what a status call through the link reports, and that a second save still goes through the
link. Run against an unmodified copy of the tree it fails with "the symlink was replaced by a regular file".
The second case, *"writeFile refuses a symbolic-link cycle instead of looping forever"*, is a **regression
guard and not a demonstration of the old bug**: before the fix the status call reported the loop before this
code was ever reached, so that case passes against the unfixed tree too. It exists only because the fix
introduces a hand-written follow loop, and it is wrapped in a timeout so an unbounded loop fails rather than
hangs. Stated here plainly rather than counted as evidence of a failure.

## DF9 — One ordinary directory could drop the entire workspace connection

**Files:** `remote/src/files.ts` lines 75–94 and 776–825; `remote/src/codeharbord.ts` lines 250–261;
`remote/src/rpc-types.ts` line 218; test at `remote/test/files.test.ts` line 1289.

**What the code did before.** A directory listing was serialised and written on the wire with no size check at
all. Both ends of the connection frame messages as one line of JSON each and reject any line longer than
16,777,216 bytes (16 MiB).

**Why that is wrong.** The previous round of this report recorded this as needing "roughly half a million
entries" and left it alone. Both halves of that are wrong, and the measurement is much worse than the
description.

A probe built a real directory holding **66,000** entries — a build output tree, a mail spool or a cache
directory reaches that easily — and asked the unmodified server to list it. The reply was one line of
**17,952,081 bytes** against the 16,777,216-byte cap. And the consequence was not that the listing failed: the
client's bounded reader treats an over-cap line as a transport fault and **tears down the whole SSH transport**.
Every terminal, every open editor and every file-watch subscription in that session dies with it, and nothing
in the failure names the directory that caused it. So the bug was never "one directory is unopenable"; it was
"one ordinary directory kills the connection".

**What changed.** The listing is now measured before it is built, and a directory whose reply would not fit is
refused with an error naming the directory, its **real** entry count and the limit. The budget is 15 MiB,
deliberately a megabyte under the transport cap so the surrounding message envelope and the directory path have
room.

A refusal was chosen over a truncated listing on purpose. A short listing would be presented by the viewer as
the directory's complete contents, and a user who does not see a file concludes the file is not there — a
silent wrong answer is worse than a visible refusal. A truncation *flag* would avoid that, but only once the
desktop client renders it; shipping the flag without the client work would ship the silent lie. The refusal
needs no wire-format change at all, only an error reply the protocol already allows.

The measurement uses each name's **serialised** form rather than its byte length, because escaping is what
expands. A filename may contain any byte except NUL and `/`, and a control byte becomes the six characters
`\u00XX` once written into JSON — so a byte-length estimate is short by a factor of six on precisely the input
this bound exists to catch. The check reads the directory entries the code already has rather than a copy of
their names, so measuring costs no extra allocation on a large directory.

Refusals of this kind travel on a new implementation-defined error code, `-32003`, declared beside the existing
revision-mismatch (`-32001`) and database-busy (`-32002`) codes. It means: your request was well formed, nothing
was changed, and the server declines to answer at that size — deliberately not the generic internal-error code,
which would tell the user the server had broken, and not the invalid-parameters code, since the parameters are
fine. **One** code was added rather than one per limit, and DF12 uses the same one; the client has no special
case for the database-busy code either and shows the server's message as written, so both messages are phrased
for a person and say what to do instead.

**Test.** New: *"a listing too big for one transport frame is refused, not put on the wire"*. It pins the
listing budget below the transport cap, checks that a just-under-budget listing is allowed, checks that a
slightly-over one is refused with the new error code and with the directory, the true entry count and the limit
in the message, and checks that the *same number* of 40-byte names is refused when those bytes are control
characters — the case a byte-length estimate would have waved through onto the wire. The end-to-end evidence is
the 66,000-entry probe described above, which is refused against the fixed tree.

## DF10 — The daemon died on the signal that ends every SSH session

**Files:** `remote/src/codeharbord.ts` lines 557–566 and 693–738; tests at `remote/test/files.test.ts` lines
1424–1480.

**What the code did before.** The daemon's only orderly shutdown was its input stream closing. It installed no
signal handlers, so an ordinary `kill`, Ctrl-C on a hand-started daemon, or the hang-up signal an ending SSH
session delivers to the process it launched — the daemon's single most common way of being stopped — all hit
the runtime's default behaviour and killed the process where it stood.

**Why that is wrong.** The previous round called this safe because "there is no state to protect". There are
three separate things it costs, not one.

First, the observable exit status becomes "died on a signal" rather than "exited normally" — which is precisely
the signal a supervisor or a packaging script reads to tell a clean stop from a crash loop. Second, every
filesystem watch handle and poll timer is abandoned rather than released, so the operating system reclaims them
only when the process table entry goes away, and the notification relay is never told to drop what it had
queued for them. Third, a reply already handed to an output stream the SSH channel had not yet drained is
discarded, so the client's pending call never completes.

**What changed.** The daemon now handles the terminate, interrupt and hang-up signals, and each one runs the
*same* shutdown path that closing the input stream already ran: close every watch subscription (releasing each
operating-system handle and announcing the release so the relay drops its queued notifications), unhook the
input feed, destroy the input stream, and set the exit status to zero. The shutdown function is idempotent, so
repeated signals collapse into one shutdown.

One design detail is load-bearing: the handlers **stay registered** for the life of the process. Removing them
on first use would restore the runtime's default behaviour, so a supervisor's impatient second terminate signal
would kill the process in the middle of its own shutdown — putting back exactly the signal-death being removed.
Keeping them costs nothing: a registered signal listener does not by itself hold the runtime's event loop open,
so the process still exits on its own.

A two-second backstop timer forces an exit if shutdown does not finish. It is registered so that it does not
itself keep the process alive, which means it can only ever fire while something *else* is still holding the
loop open — an in-flight large file read, or an output stream the far end has stopped draining. Installing a
handler takes away "die immediately", and a daemon that then ignored a terminate signal would be worse than one
that died rudely.

For completeness on what is *not* released: this process writes no pid file and owns no Unix socket (the socket
and its own signal cleanup live in the bridge, which already had handlers), and tmux sessions are meant to
outlive the daemon. The workspace database keeps its own crash durability.

**Test.** Three new cases, each driven through a real child process, because signal handling is process-wide
and cannot be exercised in-process without tearing down the test runner's own input. Each starts a daemon,
serves one request first so the signal lands on a daemon that is genuinely up, then signals it and asserts the
process exited normally with status zero rather than dying on the signal: one for the terminate signal, one for
the hang-up signal an ending SSH session sends, and one that sends terminate, terminate and interrupt in quick
succession — that last case is what pins the keep-the-handlers-registered decision. Against an unmodified copy
of the tree the first fails with the process reporting death by the terminate signal.

## DF11 — The tmux session parser read `3abc` as the number 3

**Files:** `remote/src/tmux.ts` lines 175–179 and 196–218; test at `remote/test/tmux.test.ts` line 307.

**What the code did before.** The daemon lists the user's tmux sessions by running tmux with a tab-separated
output format and parsing each line. The numeric fields — window count and creation time — were read with the
standard integer parse, and the attached flag was read by comparing the field to `"1"`.

**Why that is wrong.** The standard integer parse is a prefix scanner: it consumes as many leading digits as it
finds and silently discards the rest. `"3abc"` became 3, `"1753372800junk"` became a valid timestamp, and
`" 7"` became 7. The finiteness check that followed never caught any of this, because that parse returns either
a number or "not a number" and never an infinity. The attached field was worse in a different way: any value
that was not exactly `"1"` fell through to "not attached", so a malformed record was reported to the user as a
real, detached session that a later kill request could act on. The previous round left this alone on the
grounds that the fields come from tmux itself, so nothing hostile reaches them; that argument is about
attackers, but the failure here is a garbled or unexpected record being turned into a plausible-looking session
in a list the client then acts on.

**What changed.** Both numeric fields must now match "one or more digits and nothing else" — no sign, no
whitespace, no trailing characters — and a line that fails is dropped rather than half-read. The attached field
must be exactly `"0"` or `"1"`, which is all the tmux format can emit. The finiteness check was replaced with a
check that the value is an integer the language can represent exactly, because a twenty-digit creation time
passes a digits-only test but lands beyond the exact-integer range and silently rounds to a different instant.

**Test.** New: *"a partly-numeric field is skipped, not read as its numeric prefix"*. It first confirms the
well-formed record it varies from still parses, then requires an empty result for `3abc`, `1753372800junk`, a
leading space, a leading `+`, `3.7`, `0x10`, an empty numeric field, an attached field of `yes` and of empty,
and a twenty-digit timestamp. Against an unmodified copy of the tree it fails, reporting that `3abc` was
accepted as a session with three windows.

## DF12 — Nothing limited how many live file watches a client could accumulate

**Files:** `remote/src/files.ts` lines 559–579 and 614–643; tests at `remote/test/files.test.ts` lines 1326,
1365 and 1392.

**What the code did before.** Every file-watch subscription holds an operating-system watch handle plus a
polling timer, and the table holding them had no upper bound.

**Why that is wrong.** The previous round dismissed this because the only client is our own, driven by the
files the user has open, so a server-side cap "would only convert a client bug into a different client bug".
That reasoning does not hold, for two reasons. "The client only opens a few" is not a bound the server can
verify, and a client that leaks unsubscribe calls walks the table upward until the process reaches the
operating system's per-user watch limit — at which point **every** watch on that machine starts failing, ours
and other software's alike, for a daemon that is meant to run for days. And converting an unbounded resource
leak into one visible refusal is not "a different bug"; it is the difference between a diagnosable message and
a machine-wide failure.

**What changed.** Live subscriptions are capped at 512, and a request past the cap is refused with the same
