resource-limit error code introduced in DF9, carrying a message that names the limit and says what to do. The
new watch is refused rather than the oldest one evicted: eviction would leave an open file silently unwatched
while the client still believed it was being told about changes, showing stale content as current — the exact
outcome the specification's watch section exists to prevent. A refusal degrades gracefully, because the file
still opens and reads and only live change notification is missing.

Two details are deliberate. The cap is set **equal** to the bound on the relay's pending-notification queue.
That queue coalesces per subscription and path, and a subscription watches exactly one path, so the queue can
never hold more entries than there are live subscriptions; with the two numbers equal, the relay's *count*
bound becomes provably unreachable and its byte bound is the one that can actually bite. That was already the
argument written in the relay's comment — it is now enforced rather than assumed. Second, the check **reserves**
its slot before the first point at which the handler can be suspended. Previously the table insertion happened
after two suspension points, so a burst of watch requests — and the daemon does dispatch request lines
concurrently — could all pass one size check and overshoot together. The reservation is rolled back if the
status call that follows fails, and if everything was closed while that call was in flight no operating-system
handle is installed at all.

The event-coalescing logic, the periodic reconciliation and the relay's overflow path (which tells the client
events were lost so it can re-read the file) are untouched; the only change inside the service is the
reservation at the top of the subscribe path.

**Test.** Three new cases. *"watch refuses a new subscription past the live-subscription cap"* takes 512
subscriptions, requires the 513th to be refused with the resource-limit code and a message naming the limit,
then releases one and takes another to confirm the cap counts live subscriptions rather than lifetime ones.
*"a concurrent burst of watch calls cannot overshoot the cap"* puts 576 requests in flight at once and requires
exactly 512 to succeed with every rejection carrying the resource-limit code; that is the case that covers the
reservation. *"file.watch past the cap answers with the resource-limit code, not internal error"* exercises the
same path through the request dispatcher, so the wire-level answer is pinned too, and hands every subscription
back afterwards so it leaves no state for later tests. Against an unmodified copy of the tree the first fails
with all 600 concurrent watches granted and no cap in sight.


### The routed fallback-detector question

The coordinator routed the question of whether the unwired fallback activity detector (AG-N1) could simply be
connected up inside the daemon. The answer, with evidence, was **no — this is a missing feature, not missing
wiring**, and the plan file should be corrected instead (BD14).

The reasoning: the specification's fallback section needs exactly one input, a stream of terminal output bytes. The
specification keeps three SSH channels separate — the terminal channels, the request-and-response channel, and the
agent-status channel — and they do not meet on the server. The daemon reads only newline-delimited requests from its
input; the bridge reads only hook-posted messages from a Unix socket; and the tmux module runs exactly two commands,
listing sessions and killing a session — there is no output-capture or output-piping command anywhere under
`remote/` (the only matches in the repository are C++ live tests). Terminal output exists **only on the client**,
where the terminal controller owns the channel and receives every byte, right beside the agent monitor that already
consumes the event stream.

So making the detector work would require building either a per-pane server-side output tap with its own lifecycle
and a way to mint events for panes that have no hook and therefore no session coordinates, or a new
client-to-daemon stream duplicating every terminal byte over SSH purely to compute a three-state heuristic. Either
is a new subsystem, not a connection between two things that already meet.

# BD — build system, continuous integration, packaging, documentation

## BD1 — A tagged release could publish a server tarball stamped with the wrong version

**File:** `.github/workflows/release.yml`, the server job; the new gate sits at lines 369–375.

**What the code did before.** The project's version number is written in several places: the top-level build file,
four package manifests, and the dependency lock file, which records the version of the root package and of every
workspace separately. The lock file is the one nothing updates automatically. A script exists whose whole job is to
fail when those disagree, and the ordinary continuous-integration workflow runs it. The **release** workflow — the
one that actually builds and publishes the downloadable artefacts — did not run it anywhere.

**Why that is wrong.** The release workflow's server job runs a clean install, and a clean install does not merely
install: it rewrites a workspace's own manifest version to whatever the lock file says. So if the lock lagged the
manifests — the exact drift this repository has already suffered once, with the lock at 0.1.7 while every manifest
said 0.1.8 — the job would quietly downgrade the server manifest and then package that file up. The result is a
release named v0.1.8 whose server component reports itself as 0.1.7, with nothing going red. The publishing job does
assert a version, but only on the Windows installer's filename; the server tarball is not covered.

**What changed.** A version-agreement step was added to the release workflow's server job, running immediately after
checkout and **before** the clean install, mirroring the placement and reasoning already used in the ordinary
workflow. A comment explains why the ordering matters and why the publishing job's existing assertion does not cover
this.

## BD2 — The release-bump script silently clobbered uncommitted edits to the lock file

**File:** `.omp/skills/bump-version/bump.sh` lines 116–125.

The script has a safety guard: before rewriting any version file it checks whether any of them has uncommitted
changes and refuses to continue if so. That list contained the build file and the four manifests. It did **not**
contain the lock file — even though a block further down in the same script rewrites three version fields inside the
lock file and then stages it into the release commit.

That defeats the guard for exactly one file. A developer with an in-progress edit to the lock file — say a dependency
bump not yet committed — runs the bump, has part of that file overwritten, and then has the whole thing staged into
a commit titled "Release vX.Y.Z". No warning; the unrelated dependency change is now part of a release commit and
their own edit to the version fields is gone. The guard's own comment says it exists so that "in-progress version
edits are never clobbered", which is precisely what happened.

The dirty check now covers the lock file too, with a comment stating why it is checked despite the rewrite loop
deliberately not including it — the loop applies a manifest-shaped edit that would be wrong for the lock, which
needs three fields updated rather than one.

## BD3 — The release-bump script left the tree half-bumped if a version file was missing

**File:** `.omp/skills/bump-version/bump.sh`; the existence checks are now hoisted to lines 128–134.

The script rewrote the top-level build file first, then looped over the four manifests checking each exists and
aborting on a missing one, then separately checked the lock file exists and aborted if not.

Every one of those existence checks ran *after* at least one file had already been rewritten. If one of the
manifests had been deleted or renamed, the script would have already bumped the build file and up to three
manifests, and then died. Because it dies before staging or committing, the developer is left with a working tree
that is bumped in some files and not in others, with no commit and no tag to show for it — the "partially applied
version bump" state that the version-checking script exists to detect. The abort message names the missing file but
says nothing about the files it already modified.

All the existence checks were moved into a single loop placed before the first rewrite. The two now-redundant
in-line checks were removed. A comment states that discovering a missing file halfway through would leave the tree
bumped in some files and not others.

## BD4 — The release-bump script could abort with no message at all on a repository with many tags

**File:** `.omp/skills/bump-version/bump.sh` lines 69–73.

The script resolved the current version from the newest version tag by piping the tag list through a command that
prints one line and exits. The script runs with the shell options that make any failing command abort the script and
that make a pipeline fail if *any* stage fails.

If the tag-listing command still has output to write when the reader exits, its write fails and the kernel kills it
with a broken-pipe signal, so it exits with a failure status. The pipeline option propagates that to the whole
pipeline, and because this is a command substitution feeding an assignment, the abort-on-error option kills the
script right there — with no error message, no partial output, and nothing pointing at the cause. It works today
only because the repository has few enough tags that the tag list finishes writing before the reader exits (the
entire output fits in the pipe buffer). It becomes a hard, silent failure once the tag list exceeds that, i.e. after
a few thousand releases, or immediately if the tag listing is ever asked for a longer format. This is a well-known
footgun and worth removing on sight rather than reasoning about pipe-buffer sizes.

The two-command pipeline was replaced with a single text-processing step that consumes the entire stream and so can
never generate a broken pipe, with a comment naming the shell option as the reason. Verified behaviour-identical on
the current repository with a dry run.

## BD5 — The release-bump skill documentation omitted the lock file

**File:** `.omp/skills/bump-version/SKILL.md`, step 3 and the safety section.

The documentation said the script "rewrites the version in the build file and in every workspace manifest", naming
the four manifests, and that it "aborts if any of those version files have uncommitted changes". Neither mentioned
the lock file.

The script has rewritten and staged the lock file since the change that introduced that block; the documentation was
never updated. Somebody reading the skill to decide whether the bump is safe to run against their working tree would
conclude that their uncommitted lock edits are untouched — the opposite of the truth (see BD2), and the opposite
again after that fix, since they now abort the run. The documentation also under-sold the script: the whole reason
the lock file is handled is that nothing else updates it and a clean install installs whatever it records.

Step 3 now names the lock file and the three fields inside it that are rewritten, states why the lock must not be
left behind, and notes that a missing file aborts the run *before anything is rewritten* (BD3). The safety section
now says "any of those files — including the lock file".

## BD6 — On Windows, most test executables emit no diagnostics when they fail

**Files:** a `WIN32_EXECUTABLE FALSE` block was added to the test build files in eight directories:
`src/models/tests`, `src/persistence/tests`, `src/remote/tests`, `src/ssh/tests`, `src/agent/tests`,
`src/editor/tests`, `src/terminal/tests`, `src/viewers/tests`. The pre-existing precedent is `src/app/tests` and
`src/qml/tests`.

**What the code did before.** Qt's executable-creation function sets a target property that, on Windows, makes the
linked binary a "graphical subsystem" executable. Windows does not attach such a binary to the console it was
launched from, so anything it writes to its output or error stream goes nowhere. Qt's test framework reports every
assertion failure, test-case name and failure message on those streams. Two directories in this tree already knew
this and turned the property off — `src/app/tests` with a comment saying "a failing assertion then reports nothing at
all through ctest", and `src/qml/tests` for one target. The other eight test directories did not.

**Why that is wrong.** The release workflow runs the test suite on Windows. If, say, the known-hosts test or the
JSON-RPC client test fails there, the test runner reports a non-zero exit code and an empty log — you learn that
*something* in the SSH tests broke but not which case or which assertion, on a machine you cannot attach a debugger
to. That is the precise failure mode the two existing blocks were written to prevent; the same check was simply
never applied to its twins. It is also a latent trap for anyone adding a test to one of those eight directories.

**What changed.** Guarded by a Windows check, the property is now turned off for every plain console test target in
the eight remaining directories — seventeen targets in total. Each block carries the same one-sentence rationale and
points at the directory the practice came from. Deliberately **not** changed, following the existing convention: the
targets that drive a real window or an embedded browser stay graphical-subsystem executables. An explicit comment
names the excluded target in each of the three directories where the exclusion is not obvious.

**Verification.** No new test — this is a build-system property and its observable effect is only visible on a
Windows machine. Verified structurally: every conditional, function and loop opening and closing pair, and all
parentheses, balance in every build file under `src/` plus the top-level one.

## BD7 — A failed web-bundle build was announced with an ordinary status line, and a stale bundle was embedded

**File:** `src/qml/CMakeLists.txt` lines 211–222.

**What the code did before.** The editor and terminal web bundles are build artefacts that are not committed; the
build system builds them at configure time by running the package manager, then embeds the resulting files into the
binary as resources. When that build returned a failure code, the code printed an ordinary status message and
carried straight on.

**Why that is wrong.** There are two cases and the code only handled one. If the bundle directory does not exist at
all, the function later raises a fatal error, so the failure is unmissable — fine. But if a *previously built*
bundle directory is sitting there, which is the normal state of any working tree that has configured once, the code
proceeds to embed those older files, and the only notice anyone gets is a status line — the same severity the build
system uses for the dozens of ordinary "found this dependency" lines, with no warning header, no highlighting, and
no summary at the end of the configure. The concrete scenario: you edit an editor source file, introduce a syntax
error, re-configure, do not read all eighty lines of configure output, build, run — and the editor pane silently
runs your *previous* code. You then debug a change that is not in the binary.

**What changed.** The message was promoted to a warning and its text rewritten to state the actual consequence:
that a previously built bundle will be embedded as-is and the pane will run stale code. The build output from the
package manager is still included. The comment explains why a status line was the wrong level. Behaviour is
otherwise unchanged — configure still succeeds, which is deliberate: failing outright would make a tree with no
network or no package manager unconfigurable, and an existing option already covers the intentional case.

## BD8 — Four build-file comments cited the wrong specification section for the two-profile design

**Files:** `src/qml/tests/CMakeLists.txt` line 8, `src/editor/tests/CMakeLists.txt` line 25,
`src/terminal/tests/CMakeLists.txt` line 53, `src/viewers/tests/CMakeLists.txt` line 26.

Each of those four files pins the headless test environment and carries a comment explaining why a particular
browser flag must never be added: the embedded browser aborts the process outright once a second browser profile
exists, and this application creates two on purpose — a sandboxed one for arbitrary external pages, a privileged one
for the application's own pages. Each comment attributed that two-profile design to "SPEC 7.2".

Section 7.2 of `docs/SPEC.md` is titled "Arbitrary Websites". The section that actually requires two separate
profiles is 7.3, "Browser Profiles". This is an off-by-one citation in a comment whose entire purpose is to stop a
future maintainer re-adding a flag that crashes the application: if they go and read 7.2 to check the claim, they
find a section about arbitrary websites that says nothing about two profiles, and may reasonably conclude the comment
is stale and the constraint no longer applies. `docs/DEVELOPMENT.md` already cited 7.3 correctly, so the two
disagreed.

All four now cite 7.3. The same off-by-one in C++ and user-interface source comments was routed to the areas that own
those files and is recorded as AS8, VW5 and QM10; the routing carried an explicit warning that several *other*
citations of 7.2 in the same files are correct as written, because they concern untrusted arbitrary web content
rather than the profile split, so a blanket find-and-replace would have been wrong.

## BD9 — The plan file pointed at line numbers that no longer exist

**File:** `docs/PLAN.md` lines 468–471.

The "remaining known gaps" list cited two specific line numbers in two live test files as the places where a gap
lives. Both were stale: one now points at the middle of an unrelated comment about a channel limit, with the code
being described 38 lines further down; the other now points inside a fixture helper, with the code being described
29 lines further down. A reader following either pointer lands on unrelated code and either concludes the gap was
fixed or wastes time hunting. Line-number citations into source files rot on the first unrelated edit, which is
exactly what happened.

Both were replaced with the enclosing function names, which are searchable, unambiguous, and survive edits above
them. The numbers were deliberately not simply updated, because they would rot again.

## BD10 — The specification's architecture section claimed to describe the tree as built, but listed a third of the server directory

**File:** `docs/SPEC.md` lines 1063–1077.

Section 14, "Code Architecture", opens by stating that it is kept up to date with the tree as built, so it describes
what exists rather than what was once proposed. Its server-side diagram then listed only four entries.

The server source directory actually contains ten. Six modules were missing, including the two largest functional
areas the specification devotes whole sections to — the file-serving module and the workspace-database module — plus
the tmux discovery group. The adapters entry also omitted two files. Given the explicit "describes what exists"
promise, a reader using this diagram to find where a method lives is sent to the request dispatcher for everything,
when that file is only the dispatcher. (The client-side diagram directly above it *is* accurate — it matches the
client build file exactly.)

The server diagram was expanded to list all ten entries with a short description of each, keeping the existing
two-column comment style. The client diagram and the technology list below it were verified and left untouched — the
latter's stated minimums all match what the build system and continuous integration actually require.

## BD11 — The readme said the user-interface files live in the wrong directory

**File:** `README.md` line 35.

The repository-layout block's entry for the application directory read "application entry + QML". Four lines below
it, the same block already lists the user-interface directory separately. The application directory contains no
user-interface files at all — per its own build file it holds the entry point plus six C++ classes. So the layout
guide contradicted itself and sent a newcomer looking for interface files into the wrong directory. The entry now
names what is actually there.

## BD12 — Every continuous-integration and release step re-downloaded the whole dependency tree

**Files:** `.github/workflows/ci.yml` (both Node setup steps, now lines 27–34 and 142–145) and
`.github/workflows/release.yml` (both Node setup steps, now lines 170–174 and 365–368).

All four Node setup steps specified only a version. The action they use supports a caching input that persists the
package download cache between runs; none of the four used it.

Six jobs across the two workflows each run a clean install, and because the package manager resolves the workspace
root even when invoked from a subdirectory, every one of them installs the *entire* root lock file — which includes
the editor runtime and the bundler. That is tens of megabytes fetched from the registry on every push, every
pull-request update, and every release build, on machines running three operating systems. It is also a needless
dependency on the registry being up: a registry outage fails a release that has nothing new to download. The
workflows already cache the two expensive things — the Qt installation and the Windows dependency tree — so this was
the one remaining uncached install.

Caching was enabled on all four steps. All four sit after the checkout step, so the root lock file, which is the
default cache key input, is present. A comment on the first occurrence in each workflow explains what is being
cached and why it is worth it. Both workflow files were confirmed to still parse and to have unchanged job and step
structure.

## BD13 — The specification documented the wrong key for a coding agent's tool name

**File:** `docs/SPEC.md` line 486, plus a new clarifying paragraph at lines 491–496.

*Routed to this area from the agent-awareness reviewer, who owns the adapter code and correctly did not edit the
specification.*

Section 6.4 defines the internal event schema — the JSON object an adapter emits for every agent state change. Its
worked example showed the free-form metadata object with the tool's name under the key `toolName`.

Every adapter that actually ships emits that value under the key `tool`, not `toolName`: two adapters return it
directly from their harness's own field of the same name, and the Claude Code adapter deliberately renames that
harness's differently-named field onto the shared key. So the code has three agreeing call sites and a test pinning
the behaviour; the specification example is the stale side. The metadata object is typed as free-form, so nothing
validates or rejects a different key — an adapter written from the specification would compile, pass validation,
relay successfully, and the client would simply never see a tool name for that harness. Silent, and only discoverable
by comparing two adapters.

The example now reads `tool`. A short paragraph was also added immediately after it stating that the key is `tool`,
naming all three adapters that prove it including the one that renames its own field, and spelling out the failure
mode for a fourth adapter that picks a different key — so the next person is told *why* it is not a free choice
rather than having to infer it from an example. No adapter code was touched. (Note for future readers: one test file
also contains the string `toolName`, but only as an arbitrary payload proving the metadata object accepts free-form
keys; it is not pinning a contract and should be left alone.)

## BD14 — The plan file marked an unimplemented feature as done

**File:** `docs/PLAN.md` line 217 and lines 223–233.

*Routed to this area by the coordinator; the daemon-core reviewer supplied the evidence and correctly did not edit
the plan.*

The delivery plan tracks each workstream as a list of checkboxes. Under the agent-awareness workstream one item was
ticked: "Fallback coarse activity detection (SPEC 6.6) for the adapterless generic harness" — inside a workstream
headed "LANDED; live gate met". The feature it claims: when a terminal is running a coding-assistant program that
has no dedicated adapter, the system should still guess a coarse busy-or-idle state by watching that terminal's raw
output.

The feature is not implemented. The class that does the inference exists and is correct, but nothing in production
ever constructs it — the whole tree was searched and the only importer is one test file. And it cannot simply be
wired up: the specification's fallback section needs exactly one input, terminal output bytes, and no part of the
server-side daemon has a source for them. The request dispatcher reads only newline-delimited requests from its
input; the bridge reads only hook messages from a Unix socket; and the tmux module only ever lists and kills
sessions — there is confirmed to be no output-capture or output-piping command anywhere under the server directory.
Terminal output exists on the client side only. So this is a new server-side output tap, not a missing connection,
and the plan was telling anyone reading it that a whole subsystem was finished.

The box was unticked and a factual note added on the same item: the detector class exists but has no production
caller, the daemon has no terminal-output source (naming the three modules and the absent tmux subcommands), and
wiring it would require a new server-side output tap. The item was not deleted and nothing was overclaimed in the
other direction — the note says what exists as well as what does not. The workstream heading was also amended to
name the exception, because a reader scanning headings would otherwise never see the unticked box. The workstream's
stop-gate line was left untouched: it is genuinely met, and it never claimed to cover the fallback path.

## BD15 — The version bump missed a file, and the version checker had a matching blind spot

**Files:** `.omp/skills/bump-version/bump.sh` lines 107–127 and 145–175;
`.github/scripts/check-versions.mjs` lines 6–20 and 49–61; `.omp/skills/bump-version/SKILL.md` lines 27–42.

*Routed to this area by the coordinator; the constant itself was already corrected by the reviewer who owns the
server source, which this area did not touch (see DF5).*

**What the code did before.** Two things are supposed to keep the release version consistent across the tree: the
bump script, which rewrites it everywhere and creates the tag, and the checking script, which runs in continuous
integration and fails when the copies disagree. The bump script's file list held the top-level build file and the
four manifests, plus special handling for the lock file, and the checking script compared exactly those. Neither
knew about the server source file that hand-carries a version constant.

**Why that is wrong.** That constant is what the server daemon puts in its information reply, and the client prints
it to the user verbatim in the message "Server too old: codeharbord <version> …". Because no bump ever touched it, it
sat at 0.1.0 while the released tag was v0.1.8 — users were shown a version three releases stale, in the one message
whose entire purpose is to tell them which version they are running. The deeper defect is the second half: the
checking script reported "all version strings agree" the whole time. A consistency checker that does not know about
one of the files is worse than no checker, because it converts a drift into a green tick. The two lists had to be
brought back into agreement *and* the reason recorded, or the next hand-carried version string will do the same
thing.

**What changed.** Three parts. First, the bump script: the four manifests were split into their own array, because
they share one edit shape, and the main list became the complete set of seven files that carry the version — the
build file, the server source file, the four manifests, and the lock file. That single list now drives both the
dirty-tree guard and the pre-write existence check, so the lock file no longer needs to be tacked on at each use
site. A rewrite step was added for the new file: a pattern anchored on the whole constant declaration which **exits
with a failure if it does not match**, because a silent no-op substitution is precisely the failure that let the
constant fall behind. The comment on the list states the rule: a file that carries the version and is not in the
list is a file the bump silently skips. Second, the checking script now reads the same declaration and compares it
like every other copy, and treats a *missing* declaration — for example if somebody renames the constant — as its
own distinct failure rather than as "nothing to compare", which is how a rename would quietly switch the check back
off. Third, the skill documentation's step 3 now enumerates all seven files, names the constant and why it is in the
list, and states that the script and the checker must be kept in step with each other.

**Verification.** Three ways, since the checker *is* the gate. Running it reports agreement at 0.1.8. A negative
test: the constant was temporarily reset to 0.1.0 and the checker re-run — before this change that produced a green
"all version strings agree"; it now fails naming the file and the stale value, with a failure exit code. The file was
restored and the checker re-run green, so it now catches the exact historical defect it missed. An end-to-end test of
the bump: a throwaway repository was built containing only the seven version files, the bump was run with an explicit
version, and all seven were confirmed rewritten (including the constant and all three lock-file entries), exactly
those seven staged in the release commit, the annotated tag created, and the checker then green at the new version.
The scratch repository was deleted; the real tree is untouched and still reports 0.1.8.

## BD16 — Two more build-file comments cited the wrong specification section for notifications

**Files:** `src/app/CMakeLists.txt` line 35; `src/app/tests/CMakeLists.txt` line 90.

*Routed to this area by the coordinator, from the application-core reviewer, which owns the C++ but not the build
files.*

Both comments cited "SPEC 6.2" as the authority for the desktop-notification feature. As established in AC6, section
6.2 is "Supported Harnesses" and notifications have no section of their own; the only mention in the whole document
is a Phase 4 bullet in section 16. Both lines were corrected using the wording already established in the C++
sources rather than a third phrasing, and no section number was invented. Both comment blocks were re-wrapped so the
inserted clause did not leave a two-word orphan line; no other words changed, and the specification was not touched.
These were confirmed to be the last two occurrences of that citation under the application directory, and every
conditional, function and loop pair plus all parentheses were re-checked to balance across all twenty-one build
files.

## BD17 — Continuous integration never compiled the client on Windows or macOS

**Files:** `.github/workflows/ci.yml` — the `client` job, rewritten as a three-platform matrix at lines
63–241, with the standalone Windows cache-warming job deleted and a workflow-level `QT_VERSION` added;
`docs/DEVELOPMENT.md` lines 126–134 and 447–462.

**What the code did before.** The ordinary continuous-integration workflow built and tested the desktop
client on Linux only. There was a second Windows job, but it did no building: it existed purely to
download and cache the Windows dependency tree so that a later release build would not have to. macOS
was never touched until somebody pushed a version tag, at which point the release workflow compiled it
for the first time.

*The previous round of this hunt recorded this and deliberately left it alone*, on the grounds that
adding real Windows and macOS jobs would roughly triple the cost and time of every pull request, and
that such a trade-off is a project-level budget decision rather than a defect. Under this round's
stricter bar that reasoning no longer holds: the project ships binaries for all three operating
systems, so a platform that is never compiled before a tag is a platform whose release is a guess. The
development guide already records the consequence — the release workflow's first real run failed on
**each** of the three platforms, each for a different reason, with the tag already created and
permanent.

**What changed.** The `client` job is now a matrix over `ubuntu-latest`, `windows-latest` and
`macos-latest` with `fail-fast: false`, so one platform's failure does not hide the others'. It mirrors
the release workflow's `build` job step for step rather than inventing a second recipe: the same Qt
installation action at the shared `QT_VERSION: "6.9.*"`, the same pinned CMake and Ninja, and the same
per-platform SSH library provisioning — the system package manager on Linux, Homebrew on macOS, and on
Windows the vcpkg overlay port kept in this repository, with the same cache key and the same compiler
environment setup. Then the same web-bundle install and build, type check, bundle tests, and the
`release-tests` configure, build and test run.

The standalone Windows cache-warming job was deleted. Its only purpose was to populate that dependency
cache on the default branch, and the Windows leg of the new matrix now performs exactly the same
restore and save under exactly the same key, as a side effect of a build that is worth running for its
own sake. Keeping both would have left two copies of one vcpkg recipe free to drift apart. The comment
on the workflow's concurrency setting, which explained why pushes to the main branch are not cancelled
by naming the old job, now names the matrix leg instead.

The development guide was updated in two places: the section describing what continuous integration
covers now says all three platforms are built, while packaging remains release-only; and the passage
about the release drill now distinguishes what this change does and does not catch. Of the three
first-run failures it describes, the *build* half is now caught before a tag is created, but two of the
three were failures in *packaging*, which still runs only during a release.

**Test.** Both workflow files were parsed as YAML and checked structurally — every job and step key
present, and every step either a `uses` or a `run`. **What could not be proven from here:** that the
Windows and macOS legs actually go green. That requires GitHub-hosted runners for those operating
systems, which were not available to this work, and nothing was faked or stubbed to make them appear to
pass. The configuration is not speculative — it is the same recipe the release workflow already uses to
produce the published Windows and macOS assets for this project — but the first real continuous-
integration run is the actual proof, and it has not happened yet.

## BD18 — One build option was not formally declared, so it was invisible in every configuration tool

**Files:** `CMakeLists.txt` lines 34–47; `src/qml/CMakeLists.txt` lines 66–108, 152, 168–181 and 244;
`docs/DEVELOPMENT.md` lines 154–169.

**What the code did before.** `CODEHARBOR_WEB_SOURCEMAP` controls whether the editor and terminal web
bundles are built with source maps — debugging aids that add roughly 15 megabytes to the binary. Both
the readme and the development guide tell people they can set it on the command line, but no build file
ever declared it. An undeclared variable does not appear in `cmake -LH`, in the interactive
configuration tool, or in an IDE's preset interface: the only way to discover it was to be told about
it or to read the build files.

**Why that is wrong.** The top-level build file makes exactly this argument for the *sibling* option
next to it, which is declared for precisely that visibility. Two options documented the same way, one
discoverable and one not, is an inconsistency a reader cannot explain.

*The previous round declined to declare it, and the nuance in that refusal is worth keeping because it
was a real hazard, not an excuse.* The user-interface build file derives this option's default from the
build type: on when configuring a Debug build, off otherwise. Declaring it with CMake's ordinary
`option()` command would have written that derived default into the configuration cache on the first
configure, where it would then stick. A tree first configured as Debug and later reconfigured as
Release would keep embedding the source maps, because the cache would still hold the value derived on
day one. That objection is correct — but it is an objection to caching *the derived default*, not to
declaring the variable at all.

**What changed.** The variable is now declared in the top-level build file as a three-state cache
string — `AUTO` (the default), `ON` and `OFF` — with the set of allowed values registered so the
interactive tools offer them as a drop-down. The user-interface build file resolves that into a
separate, *non-cached* effective value: `AUTO` (or empty) means on for a Debug configure and off
otherwise, and an explicit `ON` or `OFF` is honoured as given. The three places that previously tested
the raw variable now read the resolved value, and a new status line reports the decision it made,
matching this file's existing habit of reporting what it discovered.

What sits in the cache is therefore `AUTO`, never the derived answer, so the derivation runs afresh on
every configure and the Debug-to-Release flip keeps working. A value the user passed explicitly on the
command line is in the cache before either build file runs and still wins, as it always did. The
variable is now visible where the documentation says to look, with no sticky default.

**Test.** Real configures in a scratch directory outside the tree, with the web bundle build skipped:
`cmake -LH` now lists `CODEHARBOR_WEB_SOURCEMAP:STRING=AUTO` and the cache records the three allowed
values, neither of which existed before — their absence *is* the defect. Configuring as Debug reports
source maps on; configuring as Release reports them off; Release with an explicit `ON` reports them on;
and reconfiguring after that with no arguments still reports on, confirming an explicit value persists
in the cache while the derived one does not.

## BD19 — The scaffolding scan covered two source directories and a fixed list of file extensions

**Files:** `tests/no_scaffolding.sh` (rewritten); `CMakeLists.txt` lines 182–184.

**What the code did before.** The repository has a shell script, run as part of the test suite, whose
job is to fail the build if anyone leaves a deliberate debug or mutation marker behind in the tree. It
searched two source directories, and within those only files whose extension was on a fixed list.

**Why that is wrong.** A marker left anywhere else — in a workflow file, in the packaging directory, in
the documents, in the tests directory itself, or in the skills directory — passed silently. A gate with
a blind spot is worse than no gate, because it reports a clean tree that was never fully looked at.

*The previous round left this alone, reasoning that the script's own comment scopes it to "the sources
humans ship" and that widening it risked the script matching its own marker list.* The self-reference
concern was genuine, but it is a solvable problem rather than a reason not to scan.

**What changed.** The script now searches the whole tree with no extension filter at all, skipping
binary files and excluding only paths that are not sources anyone ships: the version-control directory,
the Python virtual environment, installed package directories, build and output directories, caches,
and generated artefact, installer and fixture directories.

The two self-reference problems were solved rather than worked around. First, the script used to be
exempt from its own scan only by accident, because its extension was not on the include list; the
marker strings are now assembled at run time from adjacent quoted fragments, which the shell joins into
the real marker while leaving no occurrence of it anywhere in the file, and the explanatory comment
block no longer spells them out either. Second, the bug-hunt reports in the documents directory
legitimately name the markers in prose when recording why this gate exists; those are historical
records and never code, and they are the single excluded path. Separately, a comment in the top-level
build file that named a marker was reworded to say "a deliberate mutation marker", because a whole-tree
scan now reaches it.

**Test.** The gate is its own test, registered in the test suite. Pre-fix evidence was produced by
planting a debug-only marker in `packaging/codeharbor.desktop`, a file the old scan could not see: the
old script, taken from the committed version, reported "no scaffolding markers found" and exited
successfully, while the new script named the file and line and failed. With the planted marker removed,
the new script passes again.

## BD20 — The release workflow did not type-check the web bundles the way the ordinary workflow does

**Files:** `.github/workflows/release.yml` lines 183–195 and 395–406.

**What the code did before.** The ordinary workflow type-checks the two web bundles and runs their unit
tests as explicit steps. The release workflow — the one that builds the artefacts people download — did
neither in its build job, and its server job ran the tests but not the separate type check.

**Why that is wrong.** *The previous round left this alone on two mitigations, and both are weaker than
they look.* The first was that the bundle build step "is" the type checker, since it happens to invoke
the TypeScript compiler; that is true only for as long as it does, and swapping in a transpile-only
bundler — a routine change — would silently delete the check from the release path alone, where nobody
would notice. The second was that tags are cut from the main branch, which the ordinary workflow has
already gated; that is not true of a manually dispatched run, which accepts any reference. The release
path must not be weaker than the ordinary one.

**What changed.** The build job gained a type-check step and a bundle-test step between the bundle
build and the CMake configure, matching the ordinary workflow. The server job now runs its type check
explicitly, before its tests and build.

**Test.** No behavioural test. Both scripts were confirmed to exist and to be the same ones continuous
integration already runs, by reading the package manifests rather than assuming: the root manifest's
type-check script fans out across the workspaces, and the server manifest's runs the TypeScript
compiler in no-emit mode. The workflow file still parses. A unit test asserting that a step exists in a
YAML file would have to assert on the file's own text, which fails on reformatting rather than on
behaviour; the structural check is the honest limit here.

## BD21 — Both workflows passed a test flag that the test preset already sets

**Files:** `.github/workflows/ci.yml`, the final test step; `.github/workflows/release.yml` lines
213–219.

**What the code did before.** Both workflows invoked the test runner with an explicit
"show output on failure" flag while running a named preset, and that preset already turns the same
setting on.

**Why that is wrong.** *The previous round called it harmless redundancy that also makes the command
copy-pasteable.* It is harmless, but it is a second statement of a setting that already has one home,
and if the preset is ever changed the flag will quietly keep the old behaviour in continuous
integration and nowhere else.

**What changed.** The flag was removed from both workflows, each with a comment naming the preset file's
own setting as the reason. The release workflow keeps its verbose and repeat-until-pass flags, which do
add behaviour the preset does not. The item as originally recorded named only the ordinary workflow,
but the identical redundancy sat in its sibling, and fixing one while leaving the other is the same
inconsistent-duplicate class this round exists to close.

**Test.** No behaviour changed, so there is no behavioural test. The claim was verified by reading the
preset file directly, lines 47–58: both the development and release test presets set output-on-failure.
That is a property of another file in the same repository, which now cannot drift silently past the
comment pointing at it.

## BD22 — The readme's install instructions ended with a bare "verified for v0.1.0" note

**Files:** `README.md` lines 116–121.

**What the code did before.** The end of the install section carried a note saying the install path had
been verified for v0.1.0. The current version is 0.1.8.

**Why that is wrong.** *The previous round left this alone because rewriting it would mean either
deleting real evidence or claiming a verification that was not performed.* Both of those would indeed
be wrong — but they are not the only options, and the framing was the actual problem. A bare "verified
for v0.1.0" sitting at the end of the instructions a reader is following right now reads as an
assurance about the path they are on, eight releases later.

**What changed.** The note was reframed, not removed. It now reads as "install path last verified end to
end at v0.1.0", keeping every word of the original evidence, and adds that later releases have not been
re-verified that way, so it is a record of one dated run rather than a statement about the current
release. No evidence was destroyed and no verification that did not happen is claimed. The current
version was deliberately *not* named in the prose: that would have added a ninth place for the version
number to drift, which is the exact failure the version-checking script exists to catch.

**Test.** Prose; nothing to test. The plan file's reference to the same v0.1.0 run was checked and is
already explicit that it describes a past install of published artefacts, so it needed no change.

## BD23 — One bump-script flag could create a release tag whose committed tree carries a different version

**Files:** `.omp/skills/bump-version/bump.sh` lines 20–21, 101–111 and 204–238;
`.omp/skills/bump-version/SKILL.md` lines 43–45, 56 and 60–77; `CMakeLists.txt` lines 190–197 (test
registration); new `tests/bump_version_tag.sh`.

**What the code did before.** The release-bump script has a flag that skips making the release commit
and simply tags the current commit. Nothing checked that the commit being tagged actually carries the
version being used for the tag name. Running it against a tree at 0.1.8 with a target of 0.2.0 created
an annotated tag `v0.2.0` on a commit whose every version file still said 0.1.8.

**Why that is wrong.** The tag is what the release workflow builds from, so the published release page
would be called v0.2.0 while the binary, the server's own information reply and the remote tarball all
announce 0.1.8. A wrong version is worse than a missing one, because everything looks fine.

*The previous round left this alone, reasoning that this is the documented purpose of the flag and that
the failure it causes is loud and happens before publication, because the publishing job asserts the
version in the Windows installer's filename.* That defence does not survive inspection. The assertion
runs only after the entire cross-operating-system build has completed; it covers the Windows asset
alone; and by the time it fires the tag is already created, pushed and public. A guard that runs after
the irreversible step is not a guard.

**What changed.** The script now refuses to create the tag at all unless the commit being tagged carries
the version being tagged. Immediately before creating the tag it requires an archiving tool and a commit
to tag; extracts the version files and the repository's own version-checking script out of the
**committed** tree into a temporary directory; parses the version out of the extracted top-level build
file and aborts unless it equals the version being tagged, naming the version the commit actually
carries and how to fix it; and then runs the repository's own version-checking script inside that
extracted tree, so the seven-file agreement rule has one implementation shared with continuous
integration rather than a second copy here that could disagree with it.

Reading the committed tree rather than the working tree is the point of the design: an unrelated
uncommitted edit — or the flag that skips the dirty-tree check — cannot make a bad commit look good, and
the tag names exactly what was inspected. The legitimate use of the skip-commit flag, tagging a commit
that already *is* the release commit, is untouched. Separately, the dry-run mode now prints the correct
sentence for that flag instead of claiming it would make a commit, and the usage text and the skill
documentation state the new constraint.

**Test.** A new shell test, `tests/bump_version_tag.sh`, registered in the test suite alongside the
scaffolding scan as a Unix-only target that reports itself skipped when git or Node is unavailable. It
builds a throwaway git repository in a temporary directory carrying the same seven version files — the
real checkout is never touched and no tag is ever created in it — and asserts four things: that the
skip-commit flag on a tree at 0.1.8 asked for 0.2.0 fails, creates no tag, and says which version the
commit carries; that the same flag on a commit whose build file says 0.2.0 but whose lock file still
says 0.1.8 also fails, which doubles as a negative test of the version-checking script itself; that the
ordinary path still bumps, commits, tags, and produces a tagged tree carrying the new server version
constant; and that the skip-commit flag still tags a commit that *does* carry the version, tagging that
commit rather than making a new one.

The test was confirmed to fail against the old script. With only the bump script reverted to its
committed version, the test reported that the tag `v0.2.0` had been created on a tree carrying 0.1.8 and
exited with a failure. With the fix restored, it passes. Both this test and the scaffolding scan were
run through the test runner in a private build directory outside the tree.

## BD24 — The specification listed a resolution outcome that nothing can produce

**Files:** `docs/SPEC.md` lines 644–661.

*Routed to this area by the coordinator, originating with the viewers reviewer, who owns the C++ and
correctly did not edit the specification.*

**What the code did before.** Section 7.5 lists the possible outcomes of deciding how to display an
address the user opened. One of the listed outcomes was "open externally". Nothing produces it: the
four resolution steps the same section defines can only end in direct web navigation, the internal HTML
renderer, the text editor, the image viewer, the PDF viewer, the directory viewer, a download, or an
error.

**Why that is wrong.** Handing a resource to the desktop's own default application is an *action* that
the download-and-metadata view offers after resolution has already landed on "download" — the same
section's own handler table says so, listing "metadata and download/open actions" for binary content.
It is not a state a URL can resolve to. A reader implementing against this list would look for a code
path that does not and should not exist.

This supersedes the previous round's verdict, which kept the value on the grounds that it was "a
specified placeholder the specification mandates". That reasoning was circular: the specification listed
it because the enumeration in the code had it, and the enumeration kept it because the specification
listed it.

**What changed.** The line was removed and a sentence added saying exactly what the previous paragraph
says, cross-referencing the section that defines the viewer region's operations. Section 4.3's "open
externally" operation **was left in place and is correct** — that is the action, not the resolution
outcome. A search across the specification, the plan, the development guide and the readme found no
other reference.

## BD25 — The specification said exactly one implementation-defined error code existed; there were already two, and now three

**Files:** `docs/SPEC.md` lines 920–934.

*Routed to this area by the coordinator, originating with the daemon file-serving reviewer, who owns the
server source and correctly did not edit the specification.*

**What the code did before.** Section 10.3 ends with a bullet enumerating the error codes this project
defines beyond the standard JSON-RPC set. It said: "Exactly one code is defined so far: `-32001`".

**Why that is wrong.** Two things. First, the server source has carried a second code, `-32002` for a
busy database, mirrored on the client side, for some time — so the "exactly one" claim was already false
before anything in this round touched it. That stale claim is a finding in its own right: a document
that states a complete enumeration and is then not updated is worse than one that does not, because a
reader checks the count and stops looking. Second, this round's daemon work introduced a third code,
`-32003` for a resource limit, which needed documenting somewhere and belongs here.

The existence of `-32002` was confirmed by reading the two source files that define and mirror it rather
than by trusting the report that mentioned it.

**What changed.** The bullet is now a three-item list in the section's existing style, each entry naming
the TypeScript constant, its C++ mirror, and the specification sections that raise it: `-32001` for a
revision mismatch, `-32002` for a busy database, and `-32003` for a resource limit — meaning the
parameters were valid and nothing was applied, but the server refuses at that size, specifically a
directory listing whose serialized form would exceed 15 mebibytes or a watch request past 512 live
subscriptions. The sentence noting that the mirror test fails if the two sides drift apart was hoisted
out of the first entry so that it now covers all three.

## BD26 — The specification and readme described the editor as its own pane, contradicting the section that defines the layout

**Files:** `docs/SPEC.md` sections 8.8, 11.3 and 15; `README.md` line 135.

**What the code did before.** Several passages spoke of an "editor pane" as a region in its own right,
which conflicts with the specification's own description of the viewer region: the text editor is one of
the things the viewer pane can hold, reached by resolving what was opened, not a separate pane.

Section 8.8's file-type table listed positive matches under a heading of "default behavior" and had no
row for a type it does not recognise, which is exactly where a reader infers that the editor is the
fallback. Section 11.3 said crash-recovery snapshots are stored one file per editor pane. Section 15
said the save key applies inside a focused editor pane and again that it applies to the focused editor
pane rather than window-wide, and its reconciliation table described the feature as implemented "in the
editor pane". The readme said the save key saves the file in the focused editor pane.

**What changed.** Section 8.8 gained a fallback row stating that anything not matched above gets the
metadata and download view, and that the editor is reached on a positive match only and never as a
fallback, cross-referencing the resolution section. Section 11.3 now says one file per viewer pane
holding an unsaved buffer. Section 15 says "the focused editor" in both places, and its table entry now
reads "implemented, in the viewer pane's text-editor handler". The readme says "in the focused editor".

The four specification sections involved were re-read against each other; the section numbering is
unchanged because this round's edits were pure insertions, and the cross-references between them were
confirmed still to resolve. The plan and the development guide were checked and contain no such
framing — the plan's two apparent hits are the real filename of a QML component and that component's
type name in a gate description, both correct. Earlier bug-hunt reports use the phrase throughout and
were left alone, since they are historical records of what was written at the time.

**Not fixed here, and routed onward.** Source comments in the C++ and QML still call the editor a
distinct pane, in the application bootstrap, the editor controller and factory headers, the editor pane
QML component, and seven test files. Most are harmless shorthand. The one worth correcting is the header
comment of `src/qml/EditorPaneView.qml`, which cites the very specification section that now says the
opposite; that was reported to the coordinator rather than fixed here, because this area does not own
C++ or QML sources.

## BD27 — The plan named a channel kind that has been deleted

**Files:** `docs/PLAN.md` line 218.

*Routed to this area by the SSH reviewer, who deleted the symbol.*

The start gate for one milestone described a piece of work by naming a channel-kind enumeration value
that no longer exists after that reviewer's change. The line now describes the same work by what it is
— a dedicated command channel carrying the agent's newline-delimited event stream — rather than by a
deleted symbol. A search confirmed it was the only reference to that symbol in any document.

### Verified in this area and found correct

Recorded so these checks are not repeated.

* **Qt version alignment** (the standing project rule). The build-system floor, the continuous-integration version
  and the release version all agree on 6.9, and both `docs/DEVELOPMENT.md` and `docs/SPEC.md` say 6.9.
* **Every other stated minimum matches reality**: the build-system minimum version, matching the preset file's
  declared minimum and schema version, the readme and both documents; the SSH library floor, matching the build
  file and both documents; and the Node version, matching the root manifest's declared engine range, the readme and
  both documents. The packaging overlay's library pin, read at run time by both workflows' cache-key step, matches
  what the documentation and the release workflow claim.
* Running the version checker reports agreement at 0.1.8, and the scaffolding scan reports no markers.
* **Every command line in the readme and the development guide resolves.** All four build presets referenced exist
  with the stated build types and test settings; the stated output path for the development preset is correct; every
  package script referenced by the documentation and by continuous integration exists in the corresponding
  manifest; and both documented build options plus the test-debug-information option behave as described.
* **Test-count claims.** The top-level build file's comment and the development guide's build-speed table are both
  correct: 35 test executable targets across the ten test directories, plus the application itself.
* **The test-label claim.** The development guide says the desktop label holds exactly one target; that is the only
  target carrying it.
* **The readme's keyboard-shortcut table matches the code exactly** — the command-palette sequence, the three
  window shortcuts, and the editor's save key were each traced to their definitions, and the readme's list of
  commands with no key sequence matches the remaining palette entries. The specification's shortcut section is
  consistent with all of it.
* **The plan file's cross-references into the specification hold.** Three sections it claims now record the
  unimplemented parts all carry the stated notes and name the right symbols. Its description of the
  continuous-integration layout matches the workflow.
* **The server component really has zero runtime dependencies** — its two dependencies are development-only — so
  the documented "no install, no dependency directory" server-install instructions are accurate.
* **Release artefact plumbing is coherent.** The download step's flattening behaviour means the normalisation loop
  only ever archives the one directory, and the verification step's two expected filenames are exactly what the
  earlier steps produce. The Windows installer script's output filename matches the second of those, its guards mean
  a missing version argument fails the compile rather than shipping a blank version, and the version it is given is
  parsed from the same declaration the checker and the bump script treat as the single source of truth.
* **Packaging integrity.** All four patch files named by the dependency overlay exist and are all line-feed-only, so
  the repository's line-ending normalisation cannot corrupt them. The Windows icon is a real multi-resolution icon
  and the scalable icon is a real vector image — both load-bearing, for the installer and the Linux bundle
  respectively — and the desktop entry's icon name matches the vector image's base name.
* **The ignore, attribute and editor-configuration files are consistent with the tree**: the ignore list covers
  exactly the generated outputs the build produces, and the editor configuration's two-space override list matches
  how those file types are actually written while the build, shell and C++ files correctly fall through to the
  four-space default.

# ORCH — found by the coordinator during integration

## ORCH1 — The previous round's report understated its own test count

**File:** `docs/bug-hunt-2026-07-31.md`, the `npm test --workspaces` row of its verification table. (Cited by row rather than by line number, for the reason `BD9` gives: a line number into a file that is still being edited rots, and this document has already moved that row twice.)

The verification table in the 2026-07-31 report claimed `npm test --workspaces` passed 201 of 201 cases, broken down
as 169 for the server component, 10 for the terminal bundle and 22 for the editor bundle. That contradicted the same
round's own closing verification summary, which said 228.

The tree as committed by that round was measured before this round changed anything: the real counts were 193, 13 and
22, totalling 228. So the closing summary was right and the table was stale — it had been written earlier in the
round and never updated as that round added tests. A reader comparing the table against a fresh run of the same
commit would conclude 27 tests had disappeared.

The row was corrected to 228 with the accurate per-workspace breakdown.

## ORCH2 — The same table's test-suite row omitted the skipped target from its denominator

**File:** `docs/bug-hunt-2026-07-31.md`, the `ctest --preset dev` row of the same table.

The same table said the portable suite passed "35/35, 1 skipped". The suite registers 36 targets; 35 pass and one is
skipped for want of a desktop notification service. Writing it as 35 of 35 plus a skip makes the denominator look
like the number of targets, which it is not, and leaves a reader unable to reconcile the row with the test runner's
own output — which reports "100% tests passed … out of 36", because the runner does not count a skip as a failure.

The row now states the registered count, the pass count and the skip separately, and notes why the runner prints
100%. This was raised as a concern that only the test-count row had been refreshed while a neighbouring row was left
inconsistent, and the concern was correct.

## ORCH3 — The previous round's verification table needed a scope statement, not silent modernisation

**File:** `docs/bug-hunt-2026-07-31.md`, new paragraph above the table.

A further concern was raised that the same table's build row says 242 targets while today's verified build has 247,
and that fixing only some rows was selective.

The right resolution is not to overwrite that round's measurements with today's. That table records a verification
run performed against the tree as it stood at the end of that round; target and test counts legitimately move
between rounds, and rewriting historical figures with current ones destroys the evidence rather than correcting it.
What was actually wrong was the absence of any statement of scope, plus two rows that misreported *that round's own
run* (ORCH1 and ORCH2).

A short paragraph was added above the table stating that the figures describe the verification run at the end of the
2026-07-31 round, that they are not re-measured by later rounds, that the counts have moved since, and exactly which
two rows were corrected on 2026-08-01 and why. Every other figure is left as recorded.

## ORCH4 — A new user-interface test failed in the integrated tree, exposing a second defect in its own fix

Covered in full as QM3. Recorded here as well because it is the clearest illustration of why the coordinator runs the
gates rather than trusting per-area verification.

Every area verified its own work in isolation — several went to the trouble of building test binaries in private
temporary directories against the shared static libraries, precisely so as not to disturb concurrent editors — and
the user-interface reviewer, which could not build at all while others were editing, verified with a static linter
plus an isolated probe. That is a reasonable division, but it cannot catch an engine-ordering defect.

The full build was clean and the portable suite came back 35 of 36 with one skip and **one failure**: a newly added
case asserting that the viewer address bar shows the absolute path a relative name resolved to. It reported the
directory the pane had just left instead of the file it had just opened.

The failure was routed back to the owning area with the exact assertion output and an explicit instruction to
diagnose it rather than loosen the assertion, and to say so if the fix turned out to be unsafe. The diagnosis was
that the test was right and the production change was wrong: the new code read a *bound* property from inside the
very notification that refreshes that property's bindings, and whether the refresh has happened by then is an
ordering detail of the engine. It had not. The fix computes the value with a small function instead of reading it off
a binding — the same technique a sibling file already uses to avoid this exact hazard. No assertion was changed. The
suite is now clean.

## ORCH5 — Cross-area findings that were re-verified before being acted on, and one that was rejected

Seven findings were reported by an area that did not own the code. Each was checked by the coordinator before being
routed, and the routed area was told to verify the premise rather than trust it. Two of the seven turned out to rest
on a false premise, which is the whole reason for the round trip.

* **Wrong specification section in C++ and user-interface comments**, reported by the build-and-documentation area
  and routed to three owners with an explicit warning that neighbouring citations of the same section were correct
  and a blanket replace would be wrong. Fixed as AS8, VW5 and QM10.
* **Out-of-range stored port number**, reported by the application-core area. The coordinator routed it; the owning
  area then established that the validation **already exists** on both sides and the reported bug is not reachable.
  The real defect was the missing test coverage that would let someone reintroduce it. No production change; test
  added. Recorded as AS9.
* **The unwired crash-recovery signal**, reported by the editor area and routed to the user-interface area with an
  instruction to read the specification section first and to report back rather than do nothing if the fix belonged
  elsewhere. It did not; the missing half was built. Recorded as QM9.
* **The unwired fallback activity detector**, reported by the agent area. Rather than either building it or quietly
  correcting the plan file, the coordinator routed the *question* to the area that owns the daemon core, asking it to
  judge whether this was a small connection or a new subsystem, with an explicit instruction not to build a new
  subsystem and to report back if that was the answer. It reported back with evidence: no output source exists
  anywhere on the server side. The plan file was then corrected. Recorded as BD14, with the reasoning under DF.
* **The wrong metadata key in the specification example**, reported by the agent area and routed to the documentation
  owner, which independently re-verified the three call sites and the test before editing. Recorded as BD13.
* **The release version bump missing a file**, reported by the daemon-core area. The immediate constant was fixed by
  the area that owns it (DF5) and the durable tooling half was routed to the build area (BD15).
* **A claim that invalid UTF-8 could be corrupted on save**, reported by the editor area as a known limitation. The
  coordinator checked the daemon's read path directly and the premise is false: the decode is strict, so invalid
  bytes make it fail and the file is returned in a form the client already marks unwritable. Nothing was changed.
  Recorded under the editor area's "left alone" list.

One further item was raised, checked and resolved without a code change: whether the client's inability to send an
explicit null for a nullable field was a real contract mismatch or merely an unused edge. It is real — one field
rejects the empty string a caller would reach for instead — so the contract was documented and pinned with a test
rather than dismissed. Recorded as MP5.

## ORCH6 — A concern about the verification commands themselves

Two process concerns were raised during verification and both were acted on. The first was that the initial combined
build command separated its stages with a plain sequencing operator, so a failing web-bundle build or configure step
could have been masked by a later stage's success. The command was re-run with the shell configured to abort on the
first failure, each stage confirmed individually, and the final rebuild done the same way. The second was that
reading a test-runner summary is not proof that a specific newly added case executed rather than skipped. That is
correct, and it is why the SSH terminal-type case (SSH2) was confirmed by running the test binary directly and
reading its per-case output, where it reports as passed rather than skipped.

---

# Summary of counts

| Area | Labelled entries | Of those, code or documentation changes | New or extended tests |
|---|---|---|---|
| `MP` — models and persistence | 10 | 10 | 5 |
| `AC` — application core | 7 | 7 | 4 |
| `AS` — application state | 17 | 17 | 6 |
| `SSH` — SSH layer | 16 | 16 | 3 |
| `RPC` — JSON-RPC client | 8 | 8 | 6 |
| `TM` — terminals | 11 | 11 | 6 |
| `ED` — editor | 13 | 13 | 9 |
| `VW` — viewers | 15 | 15 | 5 |
| `QM` — user interface | 19 | 19 | 10 |
| `AG` — agent awareness | 12 | 12 | 7 |
| `WS` — workspace daemon | 12 | 12 | 6 |
| `DF` — file and process daemon | 12 | 12 | 5 |
| `BD` — build, CI, packaging, docs | 27 | 27 | 0 (build and documentation changes; the version checker was exercised directly, including a negative test) |
| `ORCH` — integration | 6 | 3 | 0 (one already-new test was corrected, under `QM3`) |
| **Total** | **185** | **182** | **82** |

Two clarifications on those numbers, so they are not read as more than they are.

**The `ORCH` column differs from the others.** Six entries are labelled there but only three are changes to the
repository: `ORCH1`, `ORCH2` and `ORCH3`, all corrections to the previous round's report. `ORCH4` is a
cross-reference — the defect it describes was fixed under `QM3` and is counted there, not twice. `ORCH5` and
`ORCH6` are records of how cross-area findings were re-verified and how the verification commands themselves were
corrected; they document process, not repository changes. So the honest figure for changes made in this round is
**182**, not 185.

**Two of the 182 deliberately changed no production code**, and in both the thing that was wrong is the entry itself,
not a line of source. `AS9`: the bug it was routed as does not exist — the range validation was already present on
both sides — so the defect it records **is** the missing test coverage, which is what would let someone delete that
validation and still see a green suite. That coverage was added. `MP5`: the defect it records is an undocumented
contract mismatch between two languages, closed by writing the contract down and pinning it with a test rather than
widening an interface no caller needs. Both entries are closed defects; neither is a source fix, and they are called
out here because the word "fixed" would otherwise imply a code change that was correctly not made.

**Separately, and NOT included in any count above:** each area also recorded items it investigated and deliberately
did not change, in the "Investigated in this area and deliberately left alone" sections, together with the three remaining
`AG-N6` to `AG-N8` observations in the agent area. Those carry informal identifiers precisely so they
cannot be mistaken for fixes; `AG-N1` to `AG-N5` were converted into numbered findings after the implementation was completed. Two of them are worth re-reading before anyone assumes they are pending work: `AG-N7`
and `AG-N8` are hypotheses that were investigated and **disproved**, and in `AG-N8`'s case a written fix and its test
were deleted again rather than shipping a fix for a bug that does not exist.

The severity spread among those 175 changes is worth stating plainly, because a flat list hides it. A small number are
data-loss or availability bugs reachable in ordinary use today: a save being destroyed by a background re-read
(`ED1`); a viewer pane wedging permanently after a dropped connection (`VW1`); two terminal panes ending up on one
remote shell (`QM1`); a file with a long name being impossible to save (`DF4`); the whole status-relay process dying
on four bytes of bad input (`DF1`); a duplicated Dev Session losing a pane (`WS1`); the connection timeout being
absent for most users, with an unbounded blocking write behind it (`SSH1`); false "agent finished" notifications on
every helper agent (`AG1`); and a half-built crash-recovery feature that collected the user's unsaved work and then
threw it away (`QM9`); and a successful save leaving behind a recovery snapshot of older text, which the
next open then offers to restore over the saved version (`ED7`). A larger group are latent — correct today, wrong after a plausible change — and the rest are
inconsistencies, avoidable work, missing coverage, and documentation that contradicted the code. All of them are
recorded above with the same level of detail, because the round's purpose was to leave nothing unexplained.
